It is used to forecast how a price change will affect quantity sold, to anticipate how a cost shock upstream will pass through to prices, to evaluate the effect of a competitor's entry, and to understand the incidence of a proposed tax or regulation. This chapter develops demand and supply as formal mathematical objects (functions, not just curves), states the market equilibrium conditions precisely, and works through the comparative-statics machinery managers use to predict how equilibrium price and quantity respond to shifts in the underlying determinants.

In everyday terms: demand is simply how much of something people want to buy at a given price, and supply is how much producers are willing to sell at that price. You have already seen this idea in action anytime you have noticed that airline tickets get more expensive around holidays (demand rises while the number of seats, the supply, stays fixed) or that a bad harvest sends coffee prices soaring (supply falls while demand for your morning coffee does not). "Equilibrium" is simply the price at which what buyers want to buy exactly matches what sellers want to sell, so there is no leftover shortage or surplus pushing the price to keep moving; think of it as the market's natural resting point, defined formally below. This chapter turns these everyday observations into a set of predictive tools a manager can actually use.

4.1 The Demand Function

Definition 4.1 (Demand Function). The quantity demanded of a good, \(Q_d\), is a function of its own price P, the prices of related goods \(P_r\), consumer income M, consumer tastes T, and expectations of future prices \(P^e\):
\[ Q_{d} = f(P, P_{r}, M, T, P^{e}, \ldots) \]
The demand curve is the relationship between \(Q_d\) and P holding all other determinants fixed (ceteris paribus). A change in P produces a movement along the demand curve; a change in any other determinant shifts the entire curve.

The law of demand, that quantity demanded falls as price rises holding other factors constant, follows from the substitution and income effects of consumer theory (except in the empirically rare case of a Giffen good) and is treated in this course as a maintained empirical regularity: \(\partial Q_d/\partial P < 0\). For managerial purposes, the demand curve is most often specified in one of two convenient functional forms. The linear form, \(Q_d = a - bP\) (equivalently, inverse demand \(P = (a/b) - (1/b)Q\)), is analytically tractable and adequate over the price ranges typically relevant to a pricing decision. The constant-elasticity (log-linear) form, \(Q_d = AP^{-\epsilon}\), or equivalently \(\ln Q_d = \ln A - \epsilon \ln P\), has the convenient property (developed fully in Chapters 5 and 6) that the exponent \(\epsilon\) is itself the price elasticity of demand at every point on the curve, which is why it is the workhorse specification in empirical demand estimation.

The demand curve shifts (the entire schedule of quantities demanded at every price moves) in response to changes in the non-price determinants. An increase in income shifts demand rightward for a normal good and leftward for an inferior good. An increase in the price of a substitute shifts demand for the good in question rightward (consumers switch toward it); an increase in the price of a complement shifts demand leftward. A favorable shift in tastes, or an expectation of future price increases (which induces consumers to buy now), shifts demand rightward.

4.2 The Supply Function

Definition 4.2 (Supply Function). The quantity supplied of a good, Qs, is a function of its own price P, the prices of inputs Pi, the state of technology τ, the prices of goods the firm could produce instead Pa (alternative outputs), and the number of firms in the industry N:
\[ Q_{s} = h(P, P_{i}, \tau, P_{a}, N, \ldots) \]
The supply curve holds all determinants but P fixed. The law of supply, ∂Qs/∂P > 0, reflects the underlying convex (increasing marginal) cost structure developed formally in Chapters 8–9: a competitive firm's short-run supply curve is its marginal cost curve above minimum average variable cost.

The supply curve shifts in response to non-price determinants: an increase in an input price (wages, raw material costs, energy) shifts supply leftward (less is offered at every price, since costs are higher); a technological improvement that lowers marginal cost shifts supply rightward; an increase in the price of an alternative product the firm could produce with the same resources shifts supply for the good in question leftward (resources are drawn away); and entry of new firms shifts industry supply rightward. These same determinants, read in reverse, explain leftward shifts: rising input costs, a deterioration in technology or a regulatory burden that raises compliance costs, a more attractive alternative product to produce instead, and exit of firms from the industry.

4.3 Market Equilibrium

Definition 4.3 (Competitive Market Equilibrium). The equilibrium price P* and quantity Q* are the values that simultaneously satisfy the demand function, the supply function, and the market-clearing condition:
\[ Q_{d}(P^{*}) = Q_{s}(P^{*}) = Q^{*} \]
At P*, the quantity buyers wish to purchase exactly equals the quantity sellers wish to offer; there is no excess demand (shortage) or excess supply (surplus), and hence no economic pressure for price to change further.

If price is temporarily above P*, quantity supplied exceeds quantity demanded (a surplus), and unsold inventory or unmet sales targets put downward pressure on price. If price is below P*, quantity demanded exceeds quantity supplied (a shortage), and unmet demand (queues, backorders, bidding by frustrated buyers) puts upward pressure on price. This adjustment process is what justifies treating P* as a stable equilibrium under standard (Walrasian, or "law of one price") stability conditions, which hold whenever the demand curve is downward-sloping and the supply curve is upward-sloping, as assumed throughout this chapter.

4.4 Comparative Statics: How Equilibrium Responds to Shifts

A manager rarely cares about the equilibrium at a single point in time; she cares about how the equilibrium changes in response to an event, whether that is a competitor's product launch, an input-cost shock, a change in consumer income, or a new regulation. This is the domain of comparative statics: comparing the old equilibrium to the new equilibrium after a specified shift in demand or supply, holding everything else fixed.

Market Equilibrium
Swipe to see the full diagram
Figure 4.1: Market equilibrium with D: P=100−Q and S: P=20+Q, and the effect of a demand increase to D′: P=120−Q.
The Four Basic Comparative-Statics Results.
  • An increase (rightward shift) in demand raises both equilibrium price and equilibrium quantity.
  • A decrease (leftward shift) in demand lowers both equilibrium price and equilibrium quantity.
  • An increase (rightward shift) in supply lowers equilibrium price and raises equilibrium quantity.
  • A decrease (leftward shift) in supply raises equilibrium price and lowers equilibrium quantity.
When both curves shift simultaneously, the direction of the quantity change is ambiguous unless the relative magnitudes of the shifts are known, and likewise for price, unless one shift direction reinforces the other on that dimension. A manager forecasting a joint demand-and-supply shock (e.g., an economic expansion that raises both consumer income and input costs) must estimate the relative magnitudes, not just the directions, of both shifts to sign the net effect on price.

4.5 Consumer and Producer Surplus

Beyond predicting price and quantity, the supply-and-demand framework quantifies the gains from market exchange, which matters for a manager evaluating the total value her product creates (useful in value-based pricing, Chapter 19) and for policy analysis of taxes or price controls.

Definition 4.4 (Consumer and Producer Surplus). Consumer surplus is the area below the demand curve and above the market price, up to the equilibrium quantity: the aggregate difference between what consumers were willing to pay and what they actually paid. Producer surplus is the area above the supply curve and below the market price, up to the equilibrium quantity: the aggregate difference between what producers received and the minimum they would have accepted (their marginal cost). For linear demand P = a − bQ and linear supply P = c + dQ with equilibrium (P*, Q*):
\[ CS = \tfrac{1}{2}(a - P^{*})Q^{*}, \qquad PS = \tfrac{1}{2}(P^{*} - c)Q^{*} \]

4.6 Price Controls: A Managerial Preview

Price floors and ceilings, though typically studied as policy tools, matter directly to managers who must operate under them (minimum wage laws affecting labor input costs, rent control affecting real-estate operators, price ceilings on regulated utilities or pharmaceuticals). A binding price ceiling (a maximum legal price set below P*) creates persistent excess demand, that is, a shortage, because quantity demanded at the ceiling price exceeds quantity supplied; a manager facing a binding ceiling must ration output through queues, waitlists, or non-price allocation mechanisms, and typically finds black or secondary markets emerging at prices above the ceiling. A binding price floor (a minimum legal price set above P*, of which the minimum wage is the canonical example when interpreted as a floor on the price of labor) creates persistent excess supply, that is, a surplus, which for a labor market translates into involuntary unemployment among workers willing to work at the floor wage but unable to find employers willing to hire at that wage. Both distortions are quantified by the same consumer-and-producer-surplus apparatus: price controls generically reduce total surplus relative to the competitive equilibrium (a deadweight loss), a theme developed fully in Chapter 24 (government, regulation, and market failure).

4.7 Short-Run vs. Long-Run Elasticities and Supply Responses

A subtlety with direct managerial relevance is that both demand and supply curves are not single, fixed objects but a family of curves that depend on the time horizon under consideration. In the immediate (market-period) run, supply is often nearly fixed: a fishmonger cannot conjure more fish onto the dock within the hour, and an airline cannot add seats to a flight departing in fifteen minutes. So the short-run supply curve is steep, or even vertical, and virtually all of a demand shock is absorbed by price rather than quantity. Over a longer horizon, firms can adjust variable inputs (labor shifts, overtime, temporary capacity), and supply becomes more elastic; over the longest horizon, firms can build new plants, and new firms can enter the industry, so long-run supply is typically the most elastic of all. The same logic governs demand: consumers facing a sudden price increase in the short run may have no ready substitute (a commuter cannot instantly relocate closer to work when gasoline prices spike), so short-run demand is relatively inelastic. Given time to adjust, such as buying a more fuel-efficient vehicle, moving, or switching to public transit, long-run demand elasticity is typically substantially larger in magnitude than short-run elasticity for the same good.

Managerial implication of time-varying elasticity. A firm that raises price in response to a temporary cost shock should expect the immediate quantity response to understate the eventual, long-run quantity response once customers have had time to find substitutes. This asymmetry is one reason firms are often reluctant to raise prices even when short-run demand appears inelastic: the short-run revenue gain can be reversed, or worse, as customers gradually defect once substitutes become available, a dynamic that a single-period elasticity estimate, of the kind developed in Chapter 6, will not by itself reveal. The same logic runs symmetrically in reverse for a price cut: a firm that lowers price expecting a large volume response may be disappointed in the short run, simply because habitual buying patterns and switching costs have not yet had time to adjust, even though the long-run response eventually materializes as expected.

4.8 Managerial Application: Cost Pass-Through

A recurring practical question is how much of an increase in a firm's own input cost a manager should pass on to customers in the form of a higher price, versus absorb through a lower margin. The supply-and-demand framework gives a precise answer for a competitive market: an upward shift in marginal cost shifts the supply curve upward (leftward) by the amount of the cost increase, and the resulting increase in the market-clearing price depends on the relative slopes (equivalently, elasticities) of demand and supply at the new equilibrium.

\[ \frac{dP}{dc} = \frac{E_{s}}{E_{s} + |E_{d}|} \]

where c is the per-unit cost increase, Es is the price elasticity of supply, and |Ed| is the (absolute) price elasticity of demand, both evaluated at the new equilibrium. This pass-through formula shows that the share of a cost increase passed on to consumers rises with the elasticity of supply and falls with the elasticity of demand: if demand is highly elastic (customers have many substitutes and will simply switch away from a price increase), sellers cannot pass much of the cost increase on and must absorb more of it in reduced margin; if demand is highly inelastic, sellers can pass through most or nearly all of the cost increase without losing much volume. A manager anticipating an input-cost shock, whether a tariff, a commodity price spike, or a new minimum wage affecting labor costs, should use an estimate of demand elasticity (Chapter 6) before deciding how large a price increase to implement, rather than mechanically passing through the entire cost increase or absorbing it entirely. In the polar case of perfectly inelastic demand (|Ed| = 0), the pass-through formula reduces to dP/dc = 1: the entire cost increase is passed through to price, and quantity does not fall at all. In the polar case of perfectly elastic demand (|Ed| → ∞), pass-through falls to zero: sellers must absorb the full cost increase themselves, since any attempt to raise price would cause quantity demanded to collapse entirely.

Analytical note. The pass-through formula in Section 4.8 is derived under the maintained assumption of a competitive (price-taking) market, where price adjusts to clear supply and demand directly. Under monopoly or oligopoly (Chapters 13–16), the pass-through rate instead depends on the curvature of the demand curve and the nature of competitive interaction, and can exceed 100% (over-shifting) or fall short of the simple competitive formula, so this result should not be applied uncritically to a market with significant market power. Even in a competitive market, the pass-through formula is a comparative-statics result comparing two equilibria; it says nothing about the speed of adjustment, which in practice can take anywhere from days (for a commodity traded on an organized exchange) to many months (for a manufactured good sold under long-term contracts or list prices that are revised only periodically).

Worked Example: Solving for Equilibrium and a Comparative-Statics Shock

A firm's market research estimates demand as Qd = 100 − P and supply (aggregating all producers in the industry, including the firm) as Qs = −20 + P.

Step 1: Solve for equilibrium. Set Qd = Qs: 100 − P = −20 + P ⇒ 120 = 2P ⇒ P* = $60. Then Q* = 100 − 60 = 40 units. (Equivalently, in inverse form, P = 100 − Q and P = 20 + Q, matching Figure 4.1.)

Step 2: Compute surplus. CS = ½(100−60)(40) = ½(40)(40) = $800. PS = ½(60−20)(40) = ½(40)(40) = $800. Total surplus = $1,600.

Step 3: A demand shock. A successful marketing campaign shifts demand to Qd′ = 120 − P (a parallel outward shift of 20 units at every price). New equilibrium: 120 − P = −20 + P ⇒ 140 = 2P ⇒ P′ = $70, Q′ = 120 − 70 = 50 units. Price rises by $10 and quantity by 10 units. The manager can now compute the resulting change in revenue: old TR = 60 × 40 = $2,400; new TR = 70 × 50 = $3,500, an increase of $1,100, information she needs before deciding whether the marketing campaign's cost was justified by its revenue effect.

Practice Problems

  1. Demand is Qd = 200 − 2P and supply is Qs = −40 + 4P. (a) Solve for equilibrium price and quantity. (b) Compute consumer and producer surplus. (c) A new input tariff raises production costs, shifting supply to Qs′ = −70 + 4P. Find the new equilibrium and describe, in words, who bears more of the resulting price change, buyers or sellers, and why.
  2. Explain why a simultaneous rightward shift in both demand and supply leaves the direction of the equilibrium quantity change unambiguous, but leaves the direction of the equilibrium price change ambiguous in general. Illustrate with a numerical example in which price rises, and another in which it falls, holding the demand shift fixed and varying only the size of the supply shift.
  3. A city imposes a rent-control price ceiling on apartments at $1,200/month, while the market-clearing rent is $1,600/month. Using a supply-and-demand diagram (described in words if you cannot draw one), explain the resulting shortage, identify who gains and who loses, and explain why landlords facing a binding ceiling might respond by reducing non-price aspects of quality (maintenance, amenities) even though they cannot legally raise the rent.
  4. A minimum wage is set at $18/hour in a local labor market where the market-clearing wage is $15/hour. Using the supply-and-demand framework (supply of labor upward-sloping in the wage, demand for labor downward-sloping in the wage), explain why this creates unemployment, and explain the difference between the number of workers who lose their jobs and the number of workers who are newly attracted into the labor market by the higher wage but remain unable to find work.
If demand is Qd = 100 − P and supply is Qs = −20 + P, what is the equilibrium price?
Correct: setting 100−P = −20+P gives 120 = 2P, so P* = $60 and Q* = 40 units.

Chapter 5: Elasticity of Demand and Managerial Applications

Slope alone tells a manager almost nothing useful about how consumers will respond, in percentage terms, to a price change, and percentage response is exactly what matters for predicting the effect on total revenue and for setting a profit-maximizing markup. Elasticity is the tool that converts a demand curve into an actionable pricing instrument. This chapter defines price elasticity rigorously, proves the frequently misunderstood but essential fact that elasticity varies continuously along a straight-line demand curve even though its slope does not, links elasticity mechanically to the revenue effect of a price change, and derives the optimal markup rule that connects elasticity directly to profit-maximizing pricing.

In plain terms: elasticity answers a question every manager eventually asks: "if I raise my price by 10%, will I lose a few customers or a lot of them?" Elasticity is just a number that captures how sensitive customers are to a price change. A product with "elastic" demand (like a brand of soda with many close substitutes sitting right next to it on the shelf) will lose a lot of sales if you raise its price, while a product with "inelastic" demand (like a medicine patients need regardless of cost) will lose relatively few. Getting a feel for this number, and learning how to compute it, turns pricing from guesswork into a calculation, which is exactly what this chapter develops.

5.1 Point Price Elasticity of Demand

Definition 5.1 (Point Price Elasticity of Demand). The price elasticity of demand at a point (P, Q) on the demand curve is the percentage change in quantity demanded resulting from a one-percent change in price:
\[ E_{d} = \frac{\% \Delta Q}{\% \Delta P} = \frac{dQ/Q}{dP/P} = \frac{dQ}{dP} \cdot \frac{P}{Q} \]
Because demand curves slope downward (dQ/dP < 0), Ed is negative by this convention; managerial practice commonly reports its absolute value, |Ed|, and classifies demand as elastic if |Ed| > 1, unit elastic if |Ed| = 1, and inelastic if |Ed| < 1. This chapter follows that convention and reports |Ed| throughout.

5.2 Elasticity Is Not Slope: The Critical Distinction

The single most common error in applied treatments of elasticity is conflating it with slope. The two are related but fundamentally different objects: slope, dP/dQ (or its reciprocal dQ/dP), is measured in units (dollars per unit, or units per dollar) and is constant along a straight-line demand curve by definition. Elasticity, by contrast, is a pure (unit-free) number, a ratio of percentage changes, and it varies continuously along a straight-line demand curve, from zero at the quantity-axis intercept to infinity at the price-axis intercept.

Proposition 5.1 (Elasticity Along a Linear Demand Curve). For the linear inverse demand curve P = a − bQ (equivalently Q = a/b − P/b, so dQ/dP = −1/b is constant), the point price elasticity is:
\[ |E_{d}(Q)| = \left| \frac{dQ}{dP} \right| \cdot \frac{P}{Q} = \frac{1}{b} \cdot \frac{a - bQ}{Q} = \frac{a - bQ}{bQ} \]
As Q → 0 (P → a, the vertical intercept), |Ed| → ∞. As Q → a/b (P → 0, the horizontal intercept), |Ed| → 0. At the exact midpoint of the line, Q = a/(2b) and P = a/2, |Ed| = 1 (unit elastic). Demand is elastic on the upper half of the line (above the midpoint price) and inelastic on the lower half (below the midpoint price), despite the slope −b being identical everywhere on the line.

This result is worth dwelling on because it is so frequently drawn incorrectly in introductory treatments (as the SPEC for this course explicitly flags): a straight demand line is not uniformly elastic, uniformly inelastic, or uniformly unit-elastic; it passes through the entire range from perfectly inelastic to perfectly elastic as price falls from the vertical intercept to zero. The interactive figure below lets you verify this directly by moving a point along a linear demand curve and watching the computed elasticity change continuously, even though the line itself never bends.

Customisable, drag the slider
Elasticity Along a Linear Demand Curve
Swipe to see the full diagram
Q = 50
Figure 5.1: Elasticity varies continuously from 0 to ∞ along the linear demand curve P = 100 − Q, even though the slope (−1) never changes. Drag the slider to move the point and watch |Ed| update.

5.3 Elasticity and the Revenue Effect of a Price Change

The practical payoff of Proposition 5.1 is a precise link between elasticity and the direction in which total revenue moves when price changes. Total revenue is TR = PQ. Differentiating with respect to P (moving along the demand curve, so Q = Q(P)):

\[ \frac{dTR}{dP} = Q + P\frac{dQ}{dP} = Q\left(1 + \frac{P}{Q}\frac{dQ}{dP}\right) = Q\left(1 - |E_{d}|\right) \]
Proposition 5.2 (Elasticity and Total Revenue).
  • If demand is elastic (|Ed| > 1), dTR/dP < 0: a price increase reduces total revenue (the percentage drop in quantity outweighs the percentage rise in price), and a price cut raises total revenue.
  • If demand is inelastic (|Ed| < 1), dTR/dP > 0: a price increase raises total revenue, and a price cut reduces total revenue.
  • If demand is unit elastic (|Ed| = 1), dTR/dP = 0: total revenue is at a local maximum with respect to price. This is exactly the point Q = a/(2b) on a linear demand curve, and it is also, not coincidentally, the output at which marginal revenue equals zero (recall Baumol's unconstrained sales-maximization rule from Chapter 2).

This proposition yields an immediate, powerful, and frequently violated managerial rule: a profit-maximizing firm should never operate on the inelastic portion of its demand curve. If demand is inelastic at the current price, the firm could raise price, sell fewer units, and yet collect more total revenue, while simultaneously producing (and incurring the cost of) fewer units. Since revenue rises and cost falls, profit must rise. It follows that no output level with |Ed| < 1 can ever be profit-maximizing, a result that also falls directly out of the formal MR = MC condition, since MR(Q) = P(1 − 1/|Ed|), which is negative whenever |Ed| < 1, and marginal cost is never negative in any standard cost structure, so MR = MC cannot hold there.

5.4 The Optimal Markup Rule

The relationship MR(Q) = P(1 − 1/|Ed|) is derived directly from the definition of elasticity and is one of the most useful results in all of managerial economics, because combined with the profit-maximizing condition MR = MC it yields a direct formula for the optimal price as a markup over marginal cost.

\[ MR = P\left(1 - \frac{1}{|E_{d}|}\right) \]

Derivation: TR = PQ, so MR = dTR/dQ = P + Q(dP/dQ) = P[1 + (Q/P)(dP/dQ)] = P[1 + 1/Ed] where Ed is signed (negative); writing in terms of the absolute value |Ed| = −Ed gives MR = P(1 − 1/|Ed|) as stated. Setting MR = MC and solving for P:

The Optimal Markup (Inverse Elasticity) Rule.
\[ \frac{P - MC}{P} = \frac{1}{|E_{d}|} \qquad \Longleftrightarrow \qquad P = \frac{MC}{1 - 1/|E_{d}|} \]
The left-hand side, (P−MC)/P, is the Lerner index of market power. The rule states that the profit-maximizing percentage markup over marginal cost is exactly the reciprocal of the (absolute) price elasticity of demand at the optimum. Products or customer segments with less elastic demand warrant higher markups; products or segments with more elastic demand warrant markups closer to marginal cost. This is the analytical foundation of third-degree price discrimination (Chapter 17), where a single firm charges different markups to segments with different elasticities. It is also the reason a common single-margin cost-plus pricing policy applied uniformly across a firm's entire product line (Chapter 19) is generically suboptimal: unless every product and segment happens to share exactly the same elasticity, a uniform markup necessarily over-prices some items and under-prices others relative to the profit-maximizing benchmark this rule identifies.

5.5 Arc Elasticity: Estimating Elasticity from Two Observed Points

Point elasticity, as defined in Section 5.1, requires knowledge of the derivative dQ/dP, the exact instantaneous slope of demand at a point. In practice, a manager often observes only two discrete price-quantity pairs (for instance, sales before and after a price change), not the continuous demand function itself, and the point-elasticity formula becomes ambiguous because it gives a different numerical answer depending on whether P and Q in the denominator are evaluated at the initial or the final point. The standard remedy is the arc elasticity formula, which uses the midpoint (average) of the two observations as the base for both percentage changes, guaranteeing the same answer regardless of the direction of the change:

\[ |E_{d}^{arc}| = \left| \frac{(Q_{2}-Q_{1})/[(Q_{1}+Q_{2})/2]}{(P_{2}-P_{1})/[(P_{1}+P_{2})/2]} \right| \]

Arc elasticity is a discrete approximation to the point elasticity evaluated near the midpoint of the observed price range, and it is the appropriate tool whenever a manager is working from a small number of directly observed price experiments rather than a fully estimated continuous demand curve (Chapter 6 develops the regression-based alternative for larger datasets, where the continuous point-elasticity formula, or its constant-elasticity log-log estimate, becomes directly usable instead).

5.6 Determinants of Price Elasticity

Empirically, price elasticity of demand for a given product is systematically higher (demand is more elastic) when: (i) close substitutes are readily available; (ii) the good represents a large share of the consumer's budget (making the percentage income effect of a price change larger); (iii) the time horizon is longer (consumers have more time to find substitutes, adjust habits, or switch suppliers, so short-run elasticities are systematically smaller in magnitude than long-run elasticities for the same good); and (iv) the good is more narrowly defined (the elasticity of demand for "Coca-Cola" is higher than for "soft drinks" in general, which is higher than for "beverages"). These regularities allow a manager, even absent a full econometric demand study, to make an informed qualitative judgment about where a product likely sits on the elasticity spectrum. A useful cross-check is to ask all four questions together for a specific product: bottled water sold at an airport gate, for instance, has few available substitutes at the point of sale, represents a trivial share of a traveler's budget, must be purchased (if at all) within a very short time horizon, and is narrowly defined by the immediate need for hydration: every one of the four determinants points toward highly inelastic demand, which is exactly why airport concessions can sustain prices several multiples of a supermarket price for the identical product.

5.7 Income and Cross-Price Elasticity

Definition 5.2 (Income and Cross-Price Elasticity). The income elasticity of demand is EM = (dQ/dM)(M/Q); a good is normal if EM > 0 and inferior if EM < 0, and normal goods are further classified as necessities (0 < EM < 1) or luxuries (EM > 1). The cross-price elasticity of demand between goods i and j is Eij = (dQi/dPj)(Pj/Qi); goods are substitutes if Eij > 0 and complements if Eij < 0.

Both concepts have direct managerial applications beyond own-price pricing decisions. A firm estimating a positive and large cross-price elasticity between its product and a rival's product knows it is in intense head-to-head competition and should closely monitor the rival's pricing; a firm estimating a negative cross-price elasticity between two products in its own portfolio (e.g., printers and ink cartridges) recognizes a complementary pricing opportunity: it may be optimal to price one good (the printer) near or even below cost in order to capture profit on the complementary good (the ink), a strategy examined further in Chapter 18. Income elasticity estimates inform product positioning and forecasting over the business cycle: a firm selling goods with high income elasticity (luxury goods) should expect demand to be more cyclically sensitive than a firm selling goods with low or negative income elasticity (staple or inferior goods).

Worked Example: Using Elasticity to Make a Pricing Decision

A firm currently prices its product at P = $40 and sells Q = 8,000 units per month. Market research indicates that if price were raised to $42, quantity demanded would fall to 7,520 units. Marginal cost is constant at MC = $25.

Step 1: Estimate the point (arc) elasticity. Using the midpoint (arc elasticity) formula to reduce sensitivity to the direction of the change:

\[ |E_{d}| = \left| \frac{\Delta Q / \bar{Q}}{\Delta P / \bar{P}} \right| = \left| \frac{(7520-8000)/7760}{(42-40)/41} \right| = \left| \frac{-0.0619}{0.0488} \right| \approx 1.27 \]

Demand is elastic (|Ed| ≈ 1.27 > 1) at the current price, which by Proposition 5.2 already tells the manager that the proposed price increase to $42 will reduce total revenue: TR at $40 is $320,000; TR at $42 is 42 × 7,520 = $315,840, confirming the prediction.

Step 2: Apply the optimal markup rule. With MC = $25 and |Ed| ≈ 1.27, the optimal markup rule gives:

\[ P^{*} = \frac{MC}{1 - 1/|E_{d}|} = \frac{25}{1 - 1/1.27} = \frac{25}{1 - 0.787} = \frac{25}{0.213} \approx \$117.4 \]

This implausibly high implied optimal price signals that |Ed| ≈ 1.27, estimated locally near P=$40–42, cannot be treated as constant all the way out to $117, since elasticity itself would rise sharply as price rose that far along a normal demand curve (Proposition 5.1), making this linear extrapolation invalid at prices far from the observed range. The safe, locally valid conclusion is the qualitative one from Step 1: since demand is elastic at the current price of $40, the firm should not raise price to $42 (which reduces revenue and, since cost also falls only modestly with lower quantity, plausibly reduces profit too); it should instead test whether a price cut below $40 raises both revenue and profit, and should re-estimate elasticity locally at each candidate price rather than relying on a single point estimate over a wide range.

Analytical note. The optimal markup rule P* = MC/(1−1/|Ed|) is an equilibrium condition: it must be evaluated at the elasticity prevailing at the optimal price, not at some other observed price. Applying an elasticity estimated at the current price to solve for a very different candidate optimal price, as the worked example shows, can generate a misleading or nonsensical result whenever the true demand curve is not iso-elastic (constant-elasticity) over the relevant range. This is precisely why demand estimation (Chapter 6) typically uses the constant-elasticity (log-log) functional form for pricing applications: it guarantees the same elasticity applies at every candidate price, so the markup rule can be applied directly without this extrapolation hazard.

Practice Problems

  1. Demand is Q = 200 − 4P. (a) Derive the general expression for |Ed| as a function of Q. (b) Find the quantity and price at which demand is unit elastic. (c) Evaluate |Ed| at Q = 40 and at Q = 160, and confirm one point is elastic and the other inelastic.
  2. A firm has constant marginal cost MC = $12 and estimates |Ed| = 2.5 at its current price. (a) Compute the profit-maximizing price using the optimal markup rule. (b) Compute the implied Lerner index. (c) If a rival's entry increases |Ed| to 4.0 (demand becomes more elastic because consumers now have a closer substitute), recompute the optimal price and explain the direction of the change in economic terms.
  3. A grocery chain finds cross-price elasticity of demand for its store-brand cereal with respect to the price of the national-brand cereal is +1.8, and the income elasticity of demand for its premium organic product line is +2.3. Explain what each number implies for (a) how the chain should respond if the national brand cuts its price, and (b) how the chain should expect sales of the organic line to behave during a recession.
  4. Explain, using Proposition 5.2, why a monopolist facing a linear demand curve never sets its profit-maximizing output on the inelastic segment of that curve, and connect this result to the marginal revenue curve implied by a linear demand curve (MR curve has the same price-intercept and twice the slope of the demand curve).
Along the linear demand curve P = 100 − Q, what happens to the price elasticity of demand as you move from the price-axis intercept (Q near 0) toward the quantity-axis intercept (Q near 100)?
Correct: |Ed| = (100−Q)/Q falls continuously from ∞ (as Q→0) through 1 (at the midpoint Q=50) to 0 (as Q→100), even though the slope of the line is constant everywhere. Elasticity is a percentage-based, unit-free concept, not the same thing as slope.

Chapter 6: Demand Estimation: Regression & Empirical Methods

Every formula in Chapter 5, from the optimal markup rule to the elasticity-revenue relationship, is useless to a manager without a number for the elasticity itself. This chapter closes the loop between demand theory and demand practice by developing the statistical methods managers and business economists use to estimate demand functions from real data: the least-squares regression estimator, its use in recovering elasticities from a fitted demand equation, the measures used to judge how well a model fits, and the identification problems that arise because observed price-quantity data are generated by the intersection of shifting demand and shifting supply, not by demand alone.

Before the statistics: Chapter 5 showed why knowing your product's elasticity is valuable, but where does that number actually come from? This chapter answers that in plain terms. Firms use their own historical sales data (for example, months of records showing what price was charged and how many units sold) and a statistical technique called regression to find the straight line, or curve, that best fits that data, in the same way you might eyeball a trend line through a scatter of dots on a chart. That best-fit line then tells the manager, numerically, how sales respond to price, which is exactly the elasticity estimate the pricing formulas from Chapter 5 need. No statistics background is assumed; every concept, including regression itself, is built up from the ground up with a fully worked numerical example.

6.1 From Theory to Numbers: Why Estimate Demand?

Demand theory (Chapter 4) tells a manager the qualitative shape and determinants of demand; it does not, by itself, tell her whether the price elasticity of her product is 0.4 or 4.0, numbers that call for opposite pricing responses under the logic of Chapter 5. Demand estimation is the empirical discipline of using observed data on quantities sold, prices charged, and other relevant variables to recover numerical estimates of demand parameters (intercepts, slopes, and elasticities) that can be plugged directly into the decision rules developed in earlier chapters.

6.2 Direct (Non-Regression) Methods: A Brief Survey

Before turning to regression, it is worth noting that demand can also be estimated without historical sales data, which is essential for new products with no sales history. Consumer surveys ask respondents directly about purchase intentions at various hypothetical prices, but suffer from hypothetical bias: stated intentions often diverge from actual purchase behavior, since survey respondents bear none of the real cost of an inaccurate answer. Consumer clinics (controlled laboratory purchase experiments) give respondents real, limited budgets to spend in a simulated store, improving realism at the cost of small, non-representative samples and high expense. Market experiments (field experiments, A/B tests) vary actual prices across comparable stores, regions, or online users and measure the resulting difference in sales, yielding the most credible causal estimates of any direct method but requiring the firm to accept the short-run cost of deliberately mispricing in some test cells. All three direct methods are complements to, not substitutes for, statistical estimation from historical market data, which is the primary subject of this chapter because it is the method most firms rely on for products with an established sales history.

6.3 The Simple Linear Regression Model

The workhorse tool of demand estimation is ordinary least squares (OLS) regression. Consider the simple bivariate model relating quantity sold Q to price P over a sample of n observations (e.g., n weeks or months of sales data):

\[ Q_{i} = \beta_{0} + \beta_{1}P_{i} + \varepsilon_{i}, \qquad i = 1, \ldots, n \]

where β0 and β1 are unknown population parameters (the true intercept and slope of the demand relationship) and εi is a random error term capturing all factors affecting Qi other than price: measurement error, omitted determinants, and genuine randomness in consumer behavior. OLS chooses the estimates b0 and b1 that minimize the sum of squared residuals, ∑(Qi − b0 − b1Pi)², yielding the closed-form estimators:

The OLS Estimators.
\[ b_{1} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(P_{i}-\bar{P})(Q_{i}-\bar{Q})}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(P_{i}-\bar{P})^{2}}, \qquad b_{0} = \bar{Q} - b_{1}\bar{P} \]
where P̄ and Q̄ are the sample means. b1 is the estimated (average) rate of change of quantity demanded per one-dollar change in price, an estimate of the slope of the demand curve, and b0 is the estimated quantity at a price of zero.
OLS Demand Regression
Swipe to see the full diagram
Figure 6.1: Eight months of price-quantity data with the fitted OLS demand line Q̂ = 56.69 − 1.4524P.

6.4 Goodness of Fit and Statistical Significance

Having estimated b0 and b1, a manager must ask two further questions: how well does the fitted line explain the variation in the data, and is the estimated price effect statistically distinguishable from zero (i.e., is there credible evidence of a real price effect, or could the observed pattern have arisen by chance)?

Definition 6.1 (Coefficient of Determination). Decompose total variation in Q into explained and unexplained parts: total sum of squares SST = ∑(Qi−Q̄)²; sum of squared residuals SSE = ∑(Qi−Q̂i)²; SSR = SST − SSE (explained variation). Then:
\[ R^{2} = \frac{SSR}{SST} = 1 - \frac{SSE}{SST} \]
R² is the fraction of total variation in quantity demanded explained by variation in price (and any other regressors included), and lies between 0 and 1 for a regression that includes an intercept term.

Statistical significance of the price coefficient is assessed with a t-statistic, t = b1/SE(b1), where SE(b1) is the estimated standard error of the slope estimator (a measure of the sampling variability of b1 across hypothetical repeated samples). Under the standard OLS assumptions, this statistic follows a t-distribution with n−2 degrees of freedom (two parameters, b0 and b1, having been estimated from the data). A conventional rule of thumb rejects the null hypothesis of no price effect (β1 = 0) at the 5% significance level when |t| exceeds approximately 2 for moderate sample sizes. A high R² is not, by itself, evidence of a correctly specified or unbiased demand equation; it is possible to obtain a high R² from a badly misspecified or even spurious regression, particularly with time-series data that share a common trend, a caution developed further in Section 6.7.

6.5 Multiple Regression and the Log-Log Demand Specification

Real demand is rarely a function of price alone. A multiple regression model extends the framework to include additional demand determinants directly, controlling for their influence while isolating the effect of the firm's own price:

\[ Q_{i} = \beta_{0} + \beta_{1}P_{i} + \beta_{2}P_{i}^{rival} + \beta_{3}M_{i} + \beta_{4}A_{i} + \varepsilon_{i} \]

where Prival is a competitor's price, M is consumer income, and A is the firm's advertising expenditure. Each coefficient is interpreted as a partial effect (the effect of that variable holding all others in the equation fixed), which is precisely the ceteris paribus condition demand theory requires and which distinguishes a proper demand estimate from a naive univariate correlation between price and quantity that fails to control for confounding shifts in these other determinants.

For pricing applications specifically, the most useful functional form is the constant-elasticity (log-log, or double-log) specification introduced in Chapter 4:

\[ \ln Q_{i} = \beta_{0} + \beta_{1}\ln P_{i} + \beta_{2}\ln P_{i}^{rival} + \beta_{3}\ln M_{i} + \varepsilon_{i} \]
Why the Log-Log Form Is the Workhorse for Pricing. In this specification, each estimated coefficient is directly and exactly the corresponding elasticity, constant at every price in the sample: β1 is the own-price elasticity of demand, β2 is the cross-price elasticity with respect to the rival's price, and β3 is the income elasticity. This follows immediately from differentiating: ∂(ln Q)/∂(ln P) = (∂Q/∂P)(P/Q) = Ed by definition. No further transformation of the estimated coefficients is required before plugging the own-price coefficient directly into the optimal markup rule of Chapter 5, precisely the property the accuracy-note in that chapter flagged as essential for avoiding extrapolation error.

6.6 The Identification Problem: Simultaneity of Supply and Demand

A subtle but critical statistical issue arises because the price-quantity pairs a firm observes in the market are not generated by the demand curve alone; they are generated by the intersection of demand and supply, both of which can shift over the sample period. If a naive regression of quantity on price is run using historical market data in which both curves have been shifting, the estimated relationship reflects some mixture of the demand curve, the supply curve, and the pattern of co-movement between the two, not the demand curve alone. This is the classical identification problem in econometrics.

Critical caution. Suppose demand is stable over the sample period but supply shifts around (due to changing input costs, weather affecting an agricultural product, or capacity changes). Then the observed (P, Q) pairs trace out points along the stable demand curve, and OLS applied to Q on P recovers the demand curve correctly. But if demand is instead the curve that shifts (due to changing income, tastes, or a rival's advertising) while supply is comparatively stable, the observed (P, Q) pairs trace out points along the supply curve, and a naive regression will recover the slope of supply, not demand: a slope with the wrong sign economically, since supply slopes upward and demand slopes downward. In the realistic case where both curves shift, the naive regression coefficient is a biased blend of both underlying relationships and can even have the wrong sign or magnitude for either curve individually.

The standard solution is the method of instrumental variables: find a variable that shifts supply but has no direct effect on demand (a valid supply-side instrument, such as an input cost or a weather shock affecting production), use it to isolate the variation in price that comes purely from supply shifts, and use only that isolated variation to trace out the demand curve. Symmetrically, a valid demand-side instrument (a variable that shifts demand but not supply, such as a regional income shock) can be used to trace out the supply curve. The mechanics of two-stage least squares estimation are beyond the scope of this introductory treatment, but every manager using regression-based demand estimates should ask, at minimum, whether the price variation in the estimation sample plausibly came from demand-side or supply-side shocks (or, ideally, from a genuine price experiment, which sidesteps the identification problem entirely because the experimenter, not the market, is generating the price variation).

6.7 Specification Problems: A Brief Checklist

Beyond identification, several further specification issues commonly bias or degrade demand estimates in applied work. Omitted variable bias arises when a determinant of demand that is correlated with price (e.g., a rival's price, which often moves with the firm's own price due to common cost shocks or tacit coordination) is left out of the regression, causing the estimated own-price coefficient to absorb some of the omitted variable's true effect. Multicollinearity arises when included regressors are highly correlated with each other (e.g., a firm's own price and its advertising expenditure, if both tend to rise together during promotional periods), which does not bias the coefficient estimates but inflates their standard errors, making it hard to distinguish each variable's individual effect with statistical confidence. Autocorrelation (serial correlation of the error term) is common in time-series sales data, where an unusually high-sales month is often followed by another unusually high-sales month due to persistent unmeasured factors (a marketing campaign's carryover effect, seasonal momentum); it does not bias the coefficient estimates themselves but invalidates the standard formulas for their standard errors, requiring robust or corrected standard-error methods before any significance test can be trusted.

6.8 From Demand Estimates to Forecasts: A Bridge to Chapter 7

A fitted demand equation is not only a snapshot of current price sensitivity; it is also the foundation for forecasting future sales, the subject developed in full in Chapter 7. Once a demand equation such as Qi = b0 + b1Pi + b2Mi + … has been estimated, a manager can generate a point forecast of future quantity demanded by plugging in projected future values of price and the other right-hand-side variables (a projected income level, a planned advertising budget, an anticipated competitor price). The quality of this forecast depends on two distinct sources of uncertainty that a careful manager keeps separate: parameter uncertainty, reflected in the standard errors of b0, b1, and the other estimated coefficients (Section 6.4), and forecast uncertainty proper, which is generally larger than parameter uncertainty alone because it also incorporates the unexplained residual variation (captured by 1 − R²) and, critically, any uncertainty in the projected future values of the explanatory variables themselves (next year's income, next quarter's advertising budget) that must be forecast or assumed before the demand equation can even be applied. A regression with a high in-sample R² can still generate a poor out-of-sample forecast if the values of the right-hand-side variables used for the forecast lie well outside the range observed in the estimation sample (the same extrapolation hazard flagged for elasticity-based pricing in Chapter 5), or if the underlying structural relationship between price and quantity has genuinely shifted since the estimation period (a new competitor has entered, tastes have changed, or the product itself has been redesigned).

Worked Example: Estimating a Demand Equation by Hand

A firm collects eight months of data on its price P ($) and quantity sold Q (thousands of units):

Month12345678
P ($)1012141618202224
Q (000s)4239373330292521

Step 1: Means. P̄ = (10+12+…+24)/8 = 136/8 = 17. Q̄ = (42+39+…+21)/8 = 256/8 = 32.

Step 2: Deviations and cross-products. Deviations of P from 17: −7,−5,−3,−1,1,3,5,7. Deviations of Q from 32: 10,7,5,1,−2,−3,−7,−11. Sum of products (Pi−P̄)(Qi−Q̄) = (−70)+(−35)+(−15)+(−1)+(−2)+(−9)+(−35)+(−77) = −244. Sum of squared price deviations = 49+25+9+1+1+9+25+49 = 168.

Step 3: OLS slope and intercept.

\[ b_{1} = \frac{-244}{168} = -1.4524, \qquad b_{0} = 32 - (-1.4524)(17) = 32 + 24.69 = 56.69 \]

Fitted demand: Q̂ = 56.69 − 1.4524P (thousands of units).

Step 4: Goodness of fit. Sum of squared Q deviations SST = 100+49+25+1+4+9+49+121 = 358. Explained sum of squares SSR = b1² × 168 = (1.4524)² × 168 ≈ 354.4. R² = 354.4/358 ≈ 0.99: price alone explains about 99% of the month-to-month variation in quantity sold in this (deliberately clean, illustrative) dataset.

Step 5: Elasticity at the mean. Using the point elasticity formula evaluated at the sample means (a standard convention when the demand equation is linear rather than log-log):

\[ |E_{d}| = |b_{1}| \cdot \frac{\bar{P}}{\bar{Q}} = 1.4524 \times \frac{17}{32} \approx 0.77 \]

Demand is inelastic (|Ed| ≈ 0.77 < 1) at the average price of $17. By Proposition 5.2 of Chapter 5, this immediately tells the manager that, starting from the average historical price, a price increase would raise total revenue. That is valuable, immediately actionable information that could not have been read off the raw data table without the regression and the elasticity calculation built on top of it.

Practice Problems

  1. A firm has five months of data: P = (5,10,15,20,25), Q = (48,40,35,28,20) (thousands of units). (a) Compute P̄ and Q̄. (b) Compute the OLS slope b1 and intercept b0. (c) Compute R². (d) Compute the point elasticity of demand at the sample means, and classify demand as elastic or inelastic at that point.
  2. Explain, in your own words, why a regression of quantity sold on price using historical data in which both supply (input costs) and demand (consumer income) were changing over the sample period can produce a badly biased estimate of the true demand slope, even with a large sample size. Propose one supply-side instrument and one demand-side instrument that could, in principle, be used to separately identify each curve.
  3. A manager runs Q = 500 − 4P + 2A (A = monthly advertising expenditure in thousands of dollars) and obtains R² = 0.92 with a highly significant coefficient on A but an insignificant coefficient on P (t-statistic of 0.8). She observes that price and advertising spending were highly correlated in her sample (higher prices tended to accompany bigger ad campaigns). Which specification problem discussed in Section 6.7 most plausibly explains the insignificant price coefficient, and what would you recommend she do before concluding that price has no effect on demand?
  4. Explain why the log-log demand specification, ln Q = β0 + β1 ln P, is generally preferred to the linear specification Q = b0 + b1P when the estimated elasticity will be plugged directly into the optimal markup rule of Chapter 5 to set a price that may be far from the sample average price.
In the log-log demand specification ln Q = β0 + β1 ln P + ε, what does the estimated coefficient β1 represent?
Correct: because ∂(ln Q)/∂(ln P) = (dQ/dP)(P/Q) is exactly the definition of point price elasticity, β1 in the log-log specification is the elasticity itself, and by construction it is the same number at every price, unlike the linear specification, where elasticity varies continuously along the curve (Chapter 5).

Module II: Demand Forecasting, Production & Cost

Chapter 7: Demand Forecasting Techniques

Why Managers Forecast

In plain terms, before the formulas: forecasting demand just means making an organized, data-informed guess about how much of something people will want to buy in the future, so a business knows how much to make, order, or staff. A bakery owner deciding how many loaves to bake tomorrow morning is already forecasting demand, even without writing down a single equation: bake too few and customers are turned away; bake too many and the extra loaves are thrown out at a loss. Every technique in this chapter, from a simple rule of thumb to a full statistical model, is a more careful and more accurate version of that same everyday guess, and the payoff for getting it right scales with how much money rides on the decision.

Every operating decision a manager makes is a bet on future demand: how much raw material to order, how many shifts to staff, how much capacity to build, how large a credit line to arrange. Chapters 4 through 6 built the theory of demand and showed how to estimate a demand curve from historical data. This chapter turns that estimated relationship, together with a family of purely statistical techniques, into an actual number: next quarter's expected unit sales. Forecasting is best understood as the bridge between the backward-looking exercise of demand estimation and the forward-looking task of production and capacity planning, and it stands alongside optimization, demand analysis, and pricing as one of the four core managerial economics toolkits.

No forecast is ever exactly right. The manager's job is not to eliminate error but to choose a method whose expected error, weighed against its cost, is appropriate to the decision at hand. A weekly staffing decision can tolerate a crude method updated frequently, while a $200 million plant expansion decision justifies a structural econometric model built and validated over months. This chapter surveys the standard toolkit (qualitative methods, time-series methods, and causal econometric methods) and gives the formulas a manager needs to compute and evaluate a forecast by hand.

Qualitative forecasting methods rely on judgment rather than historical data series, and are used when data are scarce (new products, new markets) or when a structural break makes past data unreliable.

Sales-force composite: aggregate the individual forecasts of a firm's own salespeople, who often have the most current, ground-level read on customer intentions.

Jury of executive opinion: pool the subjective forecasts of senior managers, typically averaged or reconciled in a structured meeting.

Delphi method: an anonymous, iterated survey of experts; each round shares the aggregated (anonymized) responses of the prior round so opinions converge without groupthink or seniority bias.

Market research / survey methods: direct consumer surveys, purchase-intent scales, and test markets, most useful for genuinely new products with no sales history.

Time-Series Methods I: Naive and Averaging Models

Time-series methods extrapolate a variable's own history, with no explanatory variables. They are cheap, fast, and often surprisingly hard to beat over short horizons, because they let the data's own persistence do the work.

Naive forecast: the simplest possible rule: next period equals this period, or this period plus last period's observed change if a trend is presumed:

\[ F_{t+1} = Y_{t} \qquad \text{or} \qquad F_{t+1} = Y_{t} + (Y_{t} - Y_{t-1}) \]

Naive forecasts are the benchmark against which every more sophisticated model must prove its worth: if a regression model cannot outperform the naive forecast out of sample, it is not earning its complexity.

Simple moving average of order n smooths out short-run noise by averaging the n most recent observations:

\[ F_{t+1} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1} Y_{t-i} \]

A longer window (large n) smooths more but reacts to genuine trend shifts more slowly; a shorter window reacts quickly but is noisier. This is a bias-variance trade-off the manager sets directly through the choice of n. A weighted moving average generalizes this by assigning larger weights wi to more recent observations, with ∑wi = 1, so recent information counts for more without discarding older data entirely.

Exponential smoothing is a weighted moving average with weights that decay geometrically, requiring only the previous forecast and the previous actual value to be stored:

\[ F_{t+1} = \alpha Y_{t} + (1-\alpha) F_{t} \]

where 0 < α < 1 is the smoothing constant. Expanding the recursion shows the weight on Yt−k is α(1−α)k, which declines geometrically, so every past observation influences the forecast, but recent ones dominate. A high α (close to 1) tracks recent changes closely and suits volatile, fast-moving demand; a low α (close to 0) produces a heavily smoothed, sluggish forecast appropriate for stable demand with substantial noise. α is typically chosen to minimize an accuracy criterion (below) over a historical holdout sample.

Time-Series Methods II: Decomposition and Trend Projection

Most business time series are not a single undifferentiated wiggle; they are a composite of several distinct forces. The classical decomposition model (multiplicative form, standard for sales and revenue data where seasonal swings scale with the level of the series) writes:

\[ Y_{t} = T_{t} \times S_{t} \times C_{t} \times I_{t} \]

Tt is the long-run trend (secular growth or decline), St is the seasonal component, a repeating within-year pattern such as holiday retail spikes, agricultural harvest cycles, or back-to-school demand, Ct is the cyclical component (multi-year swings tied to the business cycle, longer and less regular than a season), and It is irregular (residual) noise not explained by the other three. An additive decomposition, Yt = Tt + St + Ct + It, is preferred when the seasonal swing is roughly constant in absolute (not proportional) terms regardless of the trend level.

Isolating the trend is usually done by fitting a linear (or log-linear) trend line to the deseasonalized data by ordinary least squares, exactly as in Chapter 6:

\[ T_{t} = a + bt, \qquad b = \frac{\sum (t-\bar{t})(Y_{t}-\bar{Y})}{\sum (t-\bar{t})^{2}}, \qquad a = \bar{Y} - b\bar{t} \]

where t is a simple time index (1, 2, 3, …). If growth is proportional rather than additive (a constant percentage growth rate, common for young or rapidly scaling products), fit ln(Yt) = a + bt instead; the estimated b is then approximately the continuously compounded growth rate per period, and the level forecast is recovered by exponentiating.

Seasonal indices are typically computed as the ratio of each period's actual value to its corresponding centered moving average (which removes trend and cycle, leaving seasonal times irregular), then averaged across years for each season and normalized so the indices average to 1.0 across a full cycle. A seasonal index of 1.35 for December retail sales means December sales run 35% above the deseasonalized trend level; multiplying the trend forecast by the seasonal index for the target period restores the seasonal pattern to the final forecast.

Causal (Econometric) Forecasting

Time-series methods extrapolate a series from its own past; they say nothing about why demand moves, and so are silent on how demand would respond to a price change, a competitor's entry, or a change in disposable income. Those are precisely the questions a manager most often needs answered. Causal (econometric) forecasting uses the estimated multiple regression demand model from Chapter 6,

\[ Q_{t} = \beta_{0} + \beta_{1}P_{t} + \beta_{2}P_{t}^{competitor} + \beta_{3}Income_{t} + \beta_{4}Advertising_{t} + \varepsilon_{t} \]

and plugs in projected future values of the right-hand-side variables (the firm's planned price, a forecast of competitor pricing, a macroeconomic forecast of income growth, the planned advertising budget) to generate a forecast of Qt. This is the only method among the three families that directly answers "what happens to demand if I change price by 5%," because it estimates the structural elasticities, not merely the historical trajectory. The cost is that the forecast is only as good as (a) the model's specification and (b) the forecasts of the explanatory variables themselves, which introduces a second, often underappreciated, layer of forecast error.

Leading indicators and barometric methods occupy a middle ground: a leading indicator is a variable that reliably moves before the variable of interest (housing permits lead lumber demand; semiconductor bookings lead electronics production) and can be built into a simple regression or lag model without a full structural theory of the underlying market.

Measuring Forecast Accuracy. Let et = Yt − Ft be the forecast error in period t, over a holdout sample of n periods. Three standard summary statistics:
\[ MAD = \frac{1}{n}\sum |e_{t}| \qquad MSE = \frac{1}{n}\sum e_{t}^{2} \qquad MAPE = \frac{100}{n}\sum \left| \frac{e_{t}}{Y_{t}} \right| \]

MAD (mean absolute deviation) weights all errors equally and is easy to interpret in the original units. MSE (mean squared error) penalizes large errors disproportionately, which is appropriate when a big miss is much more costly than several small ones (e.g., a stockout that idles a production line). MAPE (mean absolute percentage error) is scale-free and lets a manager compare forecast quality across products with very different sales volumes. The best method for a given series is simply whichever minimizes the chosen criterion on out-of-sample data, never on the data used to fit the model, since that will always look artificially good in-sample.

Forecasting a Genuinely New Product: The Bass Diffusion Model

All the methods above require a history of the series itself. For a genuinely new product with no sales history at all, managers turn to analogy forecasting (map the new product's adoption path onto a structurally similar product launched previously) or to formal diffusion models. The workhorse is the Bass model, which forecasts the rate of adoption as a mixture of two forces: innovators, who adopt independently of how many others already have, and imitators, who adopt because of social contagion from prior adopters:

\[ \frac{dN(t)}{dt} = \left(p + q\,\frac{N(t)}{m}\right)\big(m - N(t)\big) \]

where N(t) is cumulative adopters by time t, m is the total market potential, p is the coefficient of innovation, and q is the coefficient of imitation. The resulting adoption curve is S-shaped (slow initial uptake, an accelerating middle phase as word-of-mouth kicks in, then saturation as the market potential m is exhausted), the same qualitative shape underlying product life-cycle forecasts used for capacity planning ahead of a launch. Typical estimated values in consumer durables are p ≈ 0.03 and q ≈ 0.38; a manager with no company-specific data can start from published p, q estimates for structurally similar past product categories and refine them once even a few periods of actual sales data arrive.

Model Selection and the Danger of Overfitting

A more complex model, with more lags, more seasonal terms, more explanatory variables, will almost always fit the historical data better, simply because it has more free parameters to absorb noise. This is overfitting, and it is the central danger in forecast model selection: a model that fits history extremely well can forecast the future extremely poorly, because it has learned the idiosyncratic noise of the sample rather than the underlying signal. The discipline against overfitting is out-of-sample validation: hold back the most recent portion of the historical data (the "test" or "holdout" period), fit the candidate models only on the earlier "training" period, and compare their MAD, MSE, or MAPE strictly on the holdout data the model never saw during estimation. A model that wins in-sample but loses out-of-sample should be rejected, since its apparent superiority was an artifact of fitting noise, not of superior forecasting ability. This discipline directly parallels the train/test split used in the regression diagnostics of Chapter 6, and for the same underlying statistical reason.

Time-Series Forecast Methods
Swipe to see the full diagram
Figure 7.1: Twelve quarters of actual sales with a fitted linear trend extrapolated four quarters ahead, compared with a naive (flat, last-period) forecast. The shaded region shows how forecast uncertainty widens with the horizon.

Worked Example: Fitting and Extrapolating a Linear Trend by Hand

A regional distributor has six quarters of unit sales (thousands): t = 1,…,6 give Y = 20, 24, 27, 33, 35, 41. The manager wants a two-quarter-ahead forecast.

Step 1 (means): t̄ = (1+2+3+4+5+6)/6 = 3.5. Ȳ = (20+24+27+33+35+41)/6 = 180/6 = 30.

Step 2 (deviations and cross-products): (t−t̄): −2.5, −1.5, −0.5, 0.5, 1.5, 2.5. (Y−Ȳ): −10, −6, −3, 3, 5, 11. Products: 25, 9, 1.5, 1.5, 7.5, 27.5, summing to ∑(t−t̄)(Y−Ȳ) = 72. Squared deviations: 6.25, 2.25, 0.25, 0.25, 2.25, 6.25, summing to ∑(t−t̄)² = 17.5.

Step 3 (slope and intercept): b = 72/17.5 = 4.11. a = 30 − 4.11(3.5) = 30 − 14.4 = 15.6. Fitted trend: Tt = 15.6 + 4.11t.

Step 4 (forecast): T7 = 15.6 + 4.11(7) = 44.4 thousand units. T8 = 15.6 + 4.11(8) = 48.5 thousand units. If quarter 7 is historically a strong seasonal quarter with seasonal index 1.10, the seasonally adjusted forecast is 44.4 × 1.10 ≈ 48.8 thousand units. This illustrates why a trend forecast and a seasonal index are multiplied together, not used interchangeably.

Choosing a Method: Cost, Horizon, and Accuracy

Method familyData requiredTypical horizonBest used when
Qualitative (Delphi, sales-force composite)Expert judgment onlyLong (new products, strategic)No usable history exists, or a structural break has occurred
Naive / moving average / exponential smoothingOwn series history onlyVery short (days to a quarter)Demand is stable, decisions are frequent and low-stakes, speed matters more than precision
Trend / seasonal decomposition2–3+ years of periodic historyShort to medium (quarters to 1–2 years)A clear trend and repeating seasonal pattern exist and are expected to persist
Causal / econometric regressionHistory on Q and all drivers (P, income, advertising, rivals’ prices)Medium to long, and any "what-if" scenarioThe manager needs to know how demand responds to a controllable variable, not just its trajectory

Forecasting in Practice: Rolling Forecasts and Reconciliation

Operationally, few firms produce a single static forecast and leave it untouched for a year. Most run a rolling forecast: at the end of each period, the most recent actual observation is added to the data set, the model is re-estimated (or, for exponential smoothing, simply updated via the recursion), and the forecast horizon rolls forward by one period, so the firm always has, say, a rolling four-quarter-ahead view rather than a fixed year-end target that becomes stale as the year progresses. In organizations with both a top-down corporate forecast (built from macro drivers and the causal regression model of this chapter) and a bottom-up sales-force composite, a forecast reconciliation step is standard: material divergences between the two are flagged for review rather than silently averaged away, since a persistent gap often signals that the sales force has field information (a competitor's pending product launch, a large customer's changing order pattern) that has not yet entered the statistical model's data set, or conversely that the statistical model is picking up a structural shift the sales force has not yet internalized.

Managerial Note: Combining Forecasts

No single method dominates in every situation, and a substantial forecasting literature (Bates and Granger, 1969, onward) shows that a simple average of several methods' forecasts frequently outperforms even the best individual method, because different methods make different, partially offsetting errors. Many firms therefore run a naive or exponential-smoothing model as a fast operational check alongside a slower causal regression model used for planning, and treat large divergences between the two as a signal to investigate rather than a nuisance to be resolved mechanically.

Judgmental Adjustment of Statistical Forecasts. Even the best statistical or causal forecast should rarely be treated as final output without a judgmental review pass by someone close to the market. Statistical models cannot see a signed contract with a major new customer that has not yet appeared in the sales history, a competitor's announced plant closure, or a regulatory change scheduled to take effect next quarter: information a sales or product manager routinely holds. Best practice is to require any judgmental override to be logged with a stated reason and, where possible, a quantified magnitude, so that the override's accuracy can itself be tracked over time against the unadjusted statistical baseline; unlogged, unexplained "gut feel" adjustments are a well-documented source of forecast bias, since managers asked to hit a target have a structural incentive to adjust the forecast toward whatever number is easiest to beat, rather than toward the number most likely to be correct.
A firm's exponential smoothing forecast uses α = 0.8. What does this imply about the forecast's behavior?
Correct: the weight on the most recent actual value is α = 0.8, and weights on older observations decay geometrically as α(1−α)k. A high α makes the forecast track recent movements closely; a low α produces a smoother, more sluggish series.

Practice Problems

1. A product's sales for the past 4 months are 100, 108, 95, 112 (thousands). Compute a 3-month simple moving average forecast for month 5.

2. With F1 = 100 (initial forecast) and α = 0.3, compute F2, F3, F4 given actuals Y1=110, Y2=95, Y3=120.

3. A firm's forecast errors over 5 periods are 2, −3, 1, −4, 2 (thousand units), with actual sales of 50, 48, 52, 47, 51 in those periods. Compute MAD, MSE, and MAPE.

4. Explain why a causal regression forecast can answer "what happens to demand if we cut price 10%" while a moving-average forecast cannot.

5. December sales for a retailer average 40% above the deseasonalized trend for the past three years. If the trend forecast for next December is 220,000 units, what is the seasonally adjusted forecast?

Chapter 8: Production Theory: Isoquants, Returns to Scale, Optimal Input Combination

From Demand to Production

In plain terms, before the formulas: production theory is simply the study of how a business turns "stuff" it pays for, workers, machines, materials, into the products or services it actually sells, and how to combine those ingredients wisely. A coffee shop deciding how many baristas to schedule alongside how many espresso machines to buy is solving a small production problem, whether or not the owner ever calls it that. A key everyday idea shows up right away here: marginal just means "the effect of one more unit" (one more worker, one more machine-hour); a manager asking "what do I get from hiring just one more employee, everything else held fixed?" is asking a marginal question, and this chapter is built almost entirely around answering such questions precisely.

Having forecast how much output the market will absorb, the manager must now decide how to produce it at least cost. This is the domain of production theory: the technological relationship between the inputs a firm employs (labor, capital, materials, energy) and the output it generates. The production function, the marginal product, the isoquant, and the condition for the cost-minimizing input mix are the standard toolkit for this problem, and they underpin every cost curve derived in Chapters 9 and 10.

A critical distinction, repeatedly conflated in weaker treatments, organizes this entire chapter: the law of diminishing marginal returns describes what happens when one input is increased while others are held fixed (a short-run phenomenon), while returns to scale describes what happens when all inputs are increased in the same proportion (a long-run phenomenon). A production process can exhibit diminishing marginal returns to labor at every input level while simultaneously exhibiting constant, increasing, or decreasing returns to scale. These are logically independent properties of the same production function, and this chapter treats them as such.

Production function: Q = f(L, K), the maximum output obtainable from labor L and capital K given the firm's current technology.

Marginal product of labor: MPL = ∂Q/∂L, the extra output from one more unit of labor, capital held fixed. Marginal product of capital MPK is defined symmetrically.

Average product of labor: APL = Q/L, output per worker.

The Law of Diminishing Marginal Returns and the Three Stages of Production

Fixing capital at some level K̄ and progressively adding labor, total product TPL = f(L, K̄) traces out the standard S-shaped curve found in every operations setting, from an assembly line to a call center. Economists divide the resulting behavior of TP, MP, and AP into three stages.

StageBehaviorManagerial interpretation
Stage IMPL > APL and both rising; APL reaches its maximum at the end of Stage IThe fixed input (capital, floor space, equipment) is under-utilized relative to labor; each new worker benefits from specialization and better use of the fixed capacity
Stage IIMPL < APL but MPL > 0; both falling; TPL still risingDiminishing marginal returns have set in, but each additional worker still adds positive output; this is the economically rational range of operation
Stage IIIMPL < 0; TPL fallingWorkers now get in each other's way; the fixed input is so scarce relative to labor that adding more labor actively reduces output

A profit-maximizing firm never rationally operates in Stage I (it would raise output and profit simply by adding labor, since MPL exceeds APL there and the marginal worker is highly productive) nor in Stage III (a rational firm would never employ a unit of labor with negative marginal product, since removing it raises output while lowering cost). Rational short-run operation is confined to Stage II, precisely the region governed by diminishing, but still positive, marginal returns. Formally, diminishing marginal returns to labor means ∂MPL/∂L = ∂²Q/∂L² < 0: the second derivative of the production function with respect to the variable input is negative once Stage I ends.

\[ MP_{L} = \frac{\partial Q}{\partial L}, \qquad \frac{\partial MP_{L}}{\partial L} = \frac{\partial^{2} Q}{\partial L^{2}} < 0 \ \text{(Stage II)} \]

Isoquants and the Marginal Rate of Technical Substitution

When both labor and capital are variable (the long run), the relevant tool is the isoquant: the locus of all (L, K) combinations that produce the same output level Q̄. Isoquants are the producer's analogue of the indifference curve, and for a well-behaved technology they share the same qualitative shape: downward sloping and convex to the origin. Convexity reflects the fact that labor and capital are imperfect substitutes: as the firm substitutes more labor for capital along an isoquant, each additional unit of labor replaces progressively less capital, because labor becomes relatively more abundant (and hence less valuable at the margin) as the substitution proceeds.

The slope of the isoquant is the marginal rate of technical substitution (MRTS), derived by total differentiation of Q = f(L,K) along a constant-output locus (dQ = 0):

\[ dQ = MP_{L}\,dL + MP_{K}\,dK = 0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad MRTS_{LK} = -\frac{dK}{dL}\bigg|_{Q=\bar{Q}} = \frac{MP_{L}}{MP_{K}} \]

MRTSLK tells the manager how much capital can be released per additional unit of labor while holding output constant. A diminishing MRTS along the isoquant (the geometric statement of convexity) is the standard assumption; the special cases are worth knowing precisely. Perfect substitutes (Q = aL + bK) have straight-line isoquants with a constant MRTS = a/b: labor and capital trade off at a fixed rate regardless of the mix, as with two production lines of different but fixed relative productivity. Perfect complements / fixed proportions (Leontief, Q = min(L/a, K/b)) have L-shaped, right-angled isoquants, with no substitution possible at all, characteristic of processes with a rigid technical ratio (one operator per machine).

Isocost Lines and the Optimal Input Combination

The manager's constraint is the market cost of inputs. An isocost line is the locus of input bundles with the same total expenditure C: wL + rK = C, where w is the wage rate and r is the rental rate (or user cost) of capital. Solving for K gives K = C/r − (w/r)L, a straight line with vertical intercept C/r and slope −w/r. Higher isocost lines (larger C) lie further from the origin; the firm prefers the lowest isocost line it can reach.

The firm's cost-minimization problem, for a target output Q̄, is:

\[ \min_{L,K}\ wL + rK \quad \text{subject to} \quad f(L,K) = \bar{Q} \]

Geometrically, the least-cost bundle sits where the lowest attainable isocost line is tangent to the target isoquant. At a tangency, the slope of the isoquant equals the slope of the isocost line:

\[ MRTS_{LK} = \frac{MP_{L}}{MP_{K}} = \frac{w}{r} \]

This is the equal marginal product per dollar condition in disguise: rearranging gives MPL/w = MPK/r, meaning the last dollar spent on labor buys exactly as much extra output as the last dollar spent on capital. If this equality failed, say MPL/w > MPK/r, the firm could produce the same output more cheaply by shifting a dollar of expenditure from capital to labor, so the original bundle could not have been cost-minimizing. This is precisely the logic behind the make-versus-automate decision: when the wage rate rises relative to the rental rate of capital, the tangency condition tells the firm to substitute toward capital, flattening its input ratio along the isoquant until MRTS again equals the new, higher w/r.

The Elasticity of Substitution

How much does a firm actually shift its input mix when relative factor prices change? The answer depends on the curvature of the isoquant, summarized by the elasticity of substitution (in general, an elasticity is just a way of measuring responsiveness: the percentage change in one variable caused by a 1% change in another, so an elasticity of substitution close to zero means the firm barely changes its input mix when relative prices move, while a large elasticity means it substitutes readily):

\[ \sigma = \frac{d\ln(K/L)}{d\ln(w/r)} \]

σ measures the percentage change in the cost-minimizing capital-labor ratio for a 1% change in the relative price of labor to capital.
