Sources of market power include: (1) economies of scale (plainly: the average cost of making one unit falls as the firm makes more, so being big is itself a cost advantage) large enough that one firm serves the market more cheaply than two (natural monopoly, as with local water utilities and electric grids); (2) legal barriers such as patents, copyrights, licenses, and government franchises; (3) control of an essential input, as in De Beers’ historical control of diamond supply; (4) network effects, where the value of a product rises with the number of users, entrenching an early leader; and (5) reputation and brand, which raise switching costs and soften competitive pressure even without a formal barrier to entry.

Monopoly. A market structure with a single seller of a product with no close substitutes, protected by a barrier to entry that prevents new firms from competing away economic profit. The monopolist is a price-maker: its output decision determines the market-clearing price via the (downward-sloping) market demand curve p(q).

13.1 Deriving Marginal Revenue: Why MR Lies Below Demand

Plain-language note: "marginal" is one of the most important words in economics, and it simply means "the effect of one more unit." Marginal revenue is the extra revenue a firm collects from selling one additional unit; marginal cost (met in earlier chapters) is the extra cost of producing one additional unit. Nearly every optimization rule in this course, including the MR = MC rule below, is really just "keep going as long as one more unit adds more benefit than cost."

The monopolist’s profit is \( \pi(q) = p(q)\,q - C(q) \), where \( p(q) \) is inverse market demand and \( C(q) \) is total cost. Because the firm sells every unit at the same price (uniform pricing, revisited under price discrimination in Chapter 17), raising output by one unit requires lowering the price on all units sold, not just the marginal one. Total revenue is \( R(q) = p(q)q \), and by the product rule:

\[ MR(q) = \frac{dR}{dq} = p(q) + q\,\frac{dp}{dq} \]

The first term is the revenue collected on the additional unit at the going price; the second term, which is negative since \( dp/dq < 0 \), is the revenue given up on every infra-marginal unit whose price must fall to move the extra unit. This second term is precisely what a competitive firm does not have: a price-taker's output choice does not move the market price, so for it \( MR = p \) exactly. For a monopolist, \( MR < p \) at every quantity except \( q=0 \).

The MR curve has exactly twice the slope of linear demand. Let inverse demand be linear, \( p(q) = a - bq \) with \( a,b>0 \). Then \( R(q) = aq - bq^2 \) and \( MR(q) = a - 2bq \). Both curves share the vertical intercept \( a \), but MR falls at rate \( 2b \) while demand falls at rate \( b \). Consequently MR reaches zero at \( q = a/2b \), exactly halfway to the demand curve’s horizontal intercept \( q=a/b \). This is one of the most commonly mis-drawn diagrams in the field: MR must bisect the horizontal distance to the demand intercept, not merely lie somewhere below demand.

13.2 The Profit-Maximizing Rule and the Lerner Index

The monopolist maximizes profit where \( MR(q) = MC(q) \), not where price equals marginal cost, and not where MR equals average cost. The resulting price is read off the demand curve (not the MR curve) at the profit-maximizing quantity \( q_m \): \( p_m = p(q_m) \). Expressing MR in terms of the price elasticity of demand (in plain terms, elasticity measures how sensitive customers are to a price change: high elasticity means a small price increase drives many customers away, while low elasticity means customers keep buying even at a higher price) \( \epsilon = \frac{dq}{dp}\frac{p}{q} < 0 \), and writing \( |\epsilon| = -\epsilon \):

\[ MR = p\left(1 - \frac{1}{|\epsilon|}\right) \]

Setting \( MR = MC \) and rearranging gives the single most useful diagnostic in this chapter, the Lerner Index of market power:

\[ L = \frac{P - MC}{P} = \frac{1}{|\epsilon|} \]

The markup over marginal cost, expressed as a fraction of price, equals the reciprocal of the elasticity of demand at the chosen price. Two immediate implications a manager should internalize: (1) a profit-maximizing monopolist never operates where demand is inelastic (\( |\epsilon| < 1 \)), because there MR would be negative, so selling one fewer unit would raise revenue while cutting cost, a pure win; and (2) the optimal markup is large exactly when customers have few substitutes (low \( |\epsilon| \)) and small when they have many. This is the analytical backbone of differential pricing across products, regions, and customer segments developed in Chapters 17-18.

13.3 Deadweight Loss and the Monopoly Profit Rectangle

Because the monopolist restricts quantity below the level at which price equals marginal cost, mutually beneficial trades go unexecuted for every unit between \( q_m \) and the competitive (efficient) quantity \( q_c \), defined by \( p(q_c) = MC(q_c) \). The lost surplus is the deadweight loss:

\[ DWL = \int_{q_m}^{q_c} \big[p(q) - MC(q)\big]\,dq \]

For linear demand \( p = a-bq \) and constant marginal cost \( c \): \( q_c = (a-c)/b \), \( q_m = (a-c)/2b = q_c/2 \), and the deadweight-loss triangle evaluates to

\[ DWL = \frac{(a-c)^2}{8b} \]

Separately, the monopolist's economic profit is the rectangle \( \pi = (P_m - ATC(q_m))\,q_m \): the per-unit margin over average total cost, times quantity sold. This is a transfer from consumers to the firm's owners. It is not part of deadweight loss, because it is still counted in total social surplus (as producer surplus). The efficiency loss is strictly the triangle of unrealized trades; the rectangle is a distributional consequence, important for shareholders and for antitrust policy but conceptually distinct from the efficiency argument. A manager should be able to explain this distinction clearly to a board or a regulator: growing the profit rectangle through genuine cost reduction or demand-expanding innovation raises firm value without necessarily enlarging the deadweight-loss triangle at all, whereas growing it purely by restricting output further along an unchanged demand and cost structure enlarges both the rectangle and the triangle together, and is the pattern of behavior competition policy is specifically designed to discourage.

Key Result. Profit maximization: set \( MR=MC \), read price off demand. Markup rule: \( (P-MC)/P = 1/|\epsilon| \). Efficiency loss: the DWL triangle between \( q_m \) and \( q_c \), bounded above by demand and below by MC, and distinct from the profit rectangle \( (P-ATC)\times q_m \), which is a transfer, not a loss.

13.4 Regulation, Rent-Seeking, and the Limits of the Harberger Estimate

A classic estimate put the deadweight loss from monopoly power across U.S. manufacturing at roughly 0.1% of GDP, a strikingly small figure. Critics have pointed out that this ignores rent-seeking: if firms must spend real resources (lobbying, patent litigation, advertising to erect switching costs) to obtain or defend monopoly positions, and if that spending can approach the value of the monopoly rectangle itself, then the true social cost of monopoly includes the rectangle as well as the triangle. For a manager, the practical lesson of this debate is that the *private* value of market power (the profit rectangle) is real and defensible through legitimate innovation and differentiation, but competitive strategies premised on erecting artificial barriers invite antitrust scrutiny precisely because they convert private gain into social loss without offsetting efficiency benefits.

Worked Example: Monopoly Pricing at a Specialty Device Maker

A firm holds the only patent on a surgical device. Estimated market demand is \( p = 100 - 2q \) (price in \$100s, q in hundreds of units). Marginal cost is constant at \( MC = 20 \).

Step 1: Revenue and MR. \( R(q)=100q-2q^2 \Rightarrow MR(q)=100-4q \).

Step 2: Set MR = MC. \( 100-4q=20 \Rightarrow q_m = 20 \). Price: \( p_m = 100-2(20)=60 \).

Step 3: Profit. Suppose average fixed cost is \( 300/q \), so \( ATC(20)=20+300/20=35 \). Profit rectangle \( = (60-35)\times 20 = 500 \) (in \$100s \( \times \) hundreds of units, i.e. \$50,000).

Step 4: Efficiency benchmark. Competitive quantity: \( 100-2q=20 \Rightarrow q_c=40 \). Deadweight loss: \( DWL = \tfrac12(60-20)(40-20) = 400 \) (\$40,000).

Step 5: Lerner Index. \( L=(60-20)/60 = 2/3 \), implying an elasticity of demand at the optimum of \( |\epsilon| = 1/L = 1.5 \). A manager reading this off a demand study would confirm the firm is operating on the elastic portion of demand, as theory requires.

Barriers to Entry: What Actually Sustains Monopoly Power

A firm might earn temporary economic profit simply by being first, or by an unexpected demand shock; that profit is not "monopoly power" in the economically meaningful sense unless something prevents rivals from entering and competing it away. The standard managerial checklist of entry barriers is: economies of scale (a large minimum efficient scale relative to market size makes entry at a competitive volume unprofitable until the entrant captures substantial share, which incumbents can prevent by aggressive short-run pricing); absolute cost advantages (proprietary technology, exclusive access to a low-cost input, accumulated learning-curve experience per Chapter 10); product differentiation advantages (an incumbent's brand equity and switching costs, which force an entrant to spend heavily on marketing merely to be considered); and legal/regulatory barriers (patents, licenses, and exclusive franchises granted by government).

A crucial managerial distinction is between barriers that are structural (a durable feature of technology or law, such as a patent or a spectrum license) and barriers that are strategic (deliberately created or reinforced by the incumbent's own conduct, such as excess capacity held idle specifically to signal a credible threat of a price war against any entrant, or long-term exclusive contracts with key distributors). Structural barriers are the foundation of legitimate market power; strategic barriers, especially when they serve no purpose other than deterring entry and offer no efficiency benefit, are precisely the conduct that competition authorities scrutinize under monopolization and abuse-of-dominance statutes.

Using the Lerner Index in Practice

The Lerner Index is not merely a theoretical construct; it is directly estimable and widely used both by managers assessing their own pricing power and by antitrust economists assessing market definition. Rearranging \( L = 1/|\epsilon| \) gives a practical diagnostic: if a firm can estimate the price elasticity of demand it faces (from historical price-quantity variation, scanner data, or a controlled pricing experiment), it can immediately infer the profit-maximizing markup without needing to observe rivals' costs or behavior at all: a single-firm-level sufficient statistic for optimal pricing under the MR = MC rule.

The reverse direction is equally important for competitive strategy: if a manager observes their realized margin \( (P-MC)/P \) is systematically far below \( 1/|\epsilon| \) as separately estimated from demand data, that is evidence the firm is under-pricing relative to what its market power would support. This is a common finding when firms anchor prices on cost-plus rules of thumb (previewed in Chapter 19) rather than directly on estimated demand elasticity. Conversely, a margin above the elasticity-implied level in a market with credible potential entrants is a warning sign that current profits may be inviting exactly the kind of entry that erodes them.

Antitrust Application: The SSNIP Test. Competition authorities define the relevant antitrust market using the "Small but Significant and Non-transitory Increase in Price" (SSNIP) test, which asks: starting from current prices, would a hypothetical monopolist controlling all products in a candidate market find it profitable to impose a small (typically 5-10%) price increase? If enough customers would substitute to products outside the candidate market to make the price increase unprofitable, the market definition is too narrow and must be broadened. This test is a direct, applied descendant of the elasticity logic in this chapter: a market is only "monopolizable" if demand within it is inelastic enough that a price increase is profitable despite the resulting loss of some customers to substitutes.

Extension: Natural Monopoly and the Regulatory Dilemma

Some markets exhibit subadditive costs: a single firm can serve total market demand more cheaply than any combination of multiple firms, typically because average cost declines over the entire relevant range of output (very large fixed infrastructure costs relative to marginal running costs, as with water pipes, transmission grids, and local cable plant). Regulators overseeing such a natural monopoly face a genuine dilemma: setting price at marginal cost (the efficient rule) causes the firm to lose money whenever MC lies below declining average cost, since a firm covering fixed costs from revenue collected at MC-based prices cannot break even. Three standard regulatory responses exist: (1) marginal-cost pricing with a public subsidy, which is first-best efficient but requires taxpayer funding; (2) average-cost pricing, setting \( P=ATC(Q) \) so the firm exactly breaks even, a second-best solution since \( P>MC \) still creates some deadweight loss, but one that is self-financing; and (3) rate-of-return regulation, allowing the firm to earn a permitted return on its invested capital base, which is administratively simpler but creates the well-documented Averch-Johnson effect (1962): the regulated firm has an incentive to over-invest in capital (inflating its allowed rate base) relative to the cost-minimizing input mix, since its allowed profit rises mechanically with capital employed.

A manager operating a regulated (or quasi-regulated, e.g., a dominant platform under increasing scrutiny) business should recognize that the choice among these regimes is rarely neutral to firm strategy: average-cost regulation rewards cost padding (since higher reported costs justify higher allowed prices), while rate-of-return regulation rewards capital intensity. Understanding which regime governs a given regulated market is often as important to strategy as understanding the underlying demand curve itself.

Monopoly Pricing & Welfare
Swipe to see the full diagram
Figure 13.1: Demand p = 100 − 2q, MC = 20, ATC = 20 + 300/q. Toggle between the monopoly outcome (MR = MC, positive markup, profit rectangle, deadweight-loss triangle) and the perfectly competitive benchmark (P = MC, quantity doubles, no deadweight loss).

Managerial Takeaways

Three practical rules follow directly from this chapter's formal results. First, never set price by intuition alone when a demand elasticity estimate is available: the MR = MC rule, translated through the Lerner Index, gives a directly computable target markup as soon as \( |\epsilon| \) is known, and that target is almost always different from, and usually more profitable than, a generic cost-plus markup applied uniformly across products with very different elasticities (a theme Chapter 19 develops at length). Second, treat the profit rectangle and the deadweight-loss triangle as conceptually distinct objects: the rectangle is what shareholders care about and what a manager is typically compensated for growing, while the triangle is what antitrust regulators and policymakers care about, and the two do not always move together. A strategy that grows the rectangle by further restricting output enlarges the triangle at the same time, inviting exactly the regulatory attention discussed above. Third, monopoly power is rarely permanent: Harberger's own estimates and the Coase-conjecture logic both point toward the same lesson, that absent a durable structural barrier, competitive entry and consumer forward-looking behavior erode market power over time, so pricing strategy should be built around the barrier's expected duration, not treated as though the current elasticity of demand will hold forever.

If demand is linear, p = 120 − 3q, what is the marginal revenue function?
Correct. For linear demand p = a − bq, marginal revenue is MR = a − 2bq: same intercept, twice the slope. Here a = 120, b = 3, so MR = 120 − 6q.

Practice Problems: Chapter 13

1. A monopolist faces p = 60 − q and has constant MC = 12. Find qm, pm, the Lerner Index, and the implied elasticity of demand at that price.

2. Using the same demand and cost as Problem 1, compute the deadweight loss and compare it to the competitive quantity.

3. Explain, in a manager's own words, why the profit rectangle (P − ATC) × Q is not part of deadweight loss, even though it comes from restricting output.

4. A firm's demand elasticity is estimated at −2.5 at current price $50. Using the Lerner Index, back out implied marginal cost.

5. Why would a profit-maximizing monopolist never knowingly set price and quantity on the inelastic portion of its demand curve?

Chapter 14: Monopolistic Competition

Before the Formulas: Why Your Local Coffee Shop Isn’t a Monopoly, But Isn’t Powerless Either

Think about choosing where to get coffee. There are several cafes nearby, so it isn’t a monopoly (Chapter 13), since you have real alternatives. But it also isn’t like buying a generic commodity (say, identical bags of flour) where every seller must match the same price or lose all their customers. You might still pay a bit more at your favorite cafe because you like its atmosphere, its specific blend, or its location on your walk to work. That small, personal preference gives each cafe a sliver of pricing power even though it has plenty of competitors. This in-between case, many sellers, each a little bit different, each with a little bit of pricing power, describes an enormous share of real businesses (restaurants, clothing brands, hair salons), and it is what this chapter formalizes as monopolistic competition.

The Middle Ground Between Monopoly and Competition

Most consumer-facing industries look nothing like the textbook extremes of Chapters 12 and 13. Restaurants, hair salons, clothing brands, boutique consulting firms, and craft breweries all sell differentiated products to many competitors, with low (but not zero) barriers to entry. The standard model of monopolistic competition combines two features: (i) each firm has some market power because its product is not a perfect substitute for rivals’ products (a downward-sloping firm-level demand curve, as in monopoly), and (ii) free entry drives long-run economic profit to zero (as in perfect competition). The result is a hybrid that explains a huge share of real-world market structure.

Monopolistic Competition. A market structure with: (1) many sellers, each small relative to the market; (2) product differentiation (by design, quality, location, or brand), giving each firm a downward-sloping residual demand curve; and (3) free entry and exit in the long run, which drives economic profit toward zero. Monopolistic competition should be understood as occupying a specific, well-defined point in the space of market structures rather than as a vague catch-all "somewhere between monopoly and competition": it borrows the downward-sloping-demand mathematics of monopoly for the individual firm's short-run pricing problem, and it borrows the free-entry zero-profit condition of perfect competition for the long-run industry equilibrium, combining the two into a single coherent model rather than averaging between them.

14.1 Short-Run Pricing: MR = MC Still Applies

In the short run, a monopolistically competitive firm behaves exactly like a small monopolist over its own differentiated variety: it faces a downward-sloping perceived demand curve \( d(q) \) (steeper than the industry demand because substitutes exist, but not flat), computes the associated marginal revenue \( MR_d(q) \) (recall: "marginal" means the effect of just one more unit, here the extra revenue from one more sale), and sets \( MR_d = MC \). If price exceeds average total cost at that quantity, the firm earns positive economic profit, exactly as a monopolist would.

14.2 Long-Run Adjustment: Entry Erodes Profit, Not Market Power

Positive short-run profits attract entry. As new competitors introduce close (but imperfect) substitutes, each incumbent's perceived demand curve shifts inward and becomes more elastic: more elastic because customers now have more alternatives, and inward because the fixed pool of demand is split among more sellers. Entry continues as long as economic profit is positive. An equilibrium, in plain terms, is simply a resting point: a situation where no one involved (here, no potential entrant or incumbent) has a reason to change what they're doing. The long-run equilibrium is reached when the firm's demand curve is tangent to its average total cost curve at the profit-maximizing quantity:

\[ P^{*} = ATC(q^{*}) \quad \text{and} \quad MR(q^{*}) = MC(q^{*}) \]

Tangency (rather than intersection) is the defining geometric feature: if demand crossed ATC from above, the firm could still find a quantity with \( P>ATC \) and earn profit, attracting further entry. Only tangency, with demand just touching (not crossing) the ATC curve at the optimal quantity, is consistent with zero economic profit and no further incentive to enter or exit.

It is worth pausing on why the long-run equilibrium condition is a tangency and not merely "demand crosses ATC somewhere." If the firm's demand curve crossed ATC from above at the profit-maximizing quantity, price would exceed average cost there, the firm would be earning positive economic profit, and (by the free-entry assumption) new competitors would keep arriving, shifting the incumbent's demand curve further inward, until no quantity remains where price exceeds ATC. Tangency is precisely the boundary case: the single point where the demand curve just touches ATC without crossing it, so that the profit-maximizing price exactly equals average cost and no further entry is attracted, yet the firm is not making a loss that would induce exit either. This is the same mathematical logic used throughout microeconomics to characterize a stable boundary equilibrium under a free-entry/free-exit condition, and it recurs in Chapter 12's long-run competitive supply curve, differing there only in that the firm's demand curve there is perfectly flat rather than downward sloping, so tangency degenerates to the special case of demand touching ATC exactly at its minimum.

Zero Long-Run Profit, Positive Price-MC Margin. In long-run equilibrium, \( P^{*}=ATC(q^{*}) \) (zero economic profit, as in perfect competition) but \( P^{*} > MC(q^{*}) \) (a markup remains, as in monopoly, because demand still slopes down). Monopolistic competition inherits the entry-driven profit erosion of perfect competition and the price-above-marginal-cost markup of monopoly simultaneously.

14.3 The Excess Capacity Theorem

Because the tangency occurs on the downward-sloping segment of the U-shaped ATC curve, to the left of the quantity that minimizes average cost, each firm in long-run equilibrium produces less than the output that would minimize its average cost, and at a higher unit cost than the industry-minimum efficient scale would allow. This is the excess capacity theorem: monopolistically competitive markets support more firms, each operating below minimum efficient scale, than a hypothetical planner minimizing total industry cost would choose.

Whether this "waste" is a market failure calling for policy intervention is contested. The standard rebuttal, including a later general-equilibrium treatment, is that the loss from excess capacity must be weighed against the gain from product variety: consumers value having many differentiated choices, and that value is not captured by comparing average-cost curves alone. A manager evaluating entry into a monopolistically competitive market should recognize that competing purely on cost efficiency (chasing minimum efficient scale) may be less profitable than competing on differentiation that shifts the firm's own demand curve outward.

14.4 Non-Price Competition: Advertising, Quality, and Location

Because price competition alone drives profit to zero, monopolistically competitive firms invest heavily in non-price competition: advertising to shift demand outward and make it less elastic, quality and feature differentiation, packaging, and locational differentiation (Hotelling-style spatial competition, where physical or perceptual "distance" between products softens price competition). These expenditures are themselves subject to a zero-profit constraint in the long run: firms will keep investing in differentiation up to the point where the marginal cost of the next unit of differentiation equals its marginal revenue benefit, after which any further profit is again competed away by entry.

Worked Example: Long-Run Equilibrium for a Boutique Fitness Studio

A studio's perceived demand (given local competitors) is \( p = 90 - 0.5q \); its cost function gives \( ATC(q) = 40 + 800/q \), with constant marginal cost \( MC=40 \). Marginal revenue is \( MR = 90-q \).

Short run: \( MR=MC \Rightarrow 90-q=40 \Rightarrow q=50 \), \( p=90-25=65 \). \( ATC(50)=40+16=56 \). Profit per member = \( 65-56=9 \), total profit \( =450 \), an attractive short-run margin.

Entry response: Competing studios open nearby, splitting the customer base and making each studio's residual demand flatter and further left, e.g. \( p = 66 - 0.5q \). Re-solve: \( MR=66-q=40 \Rightarrow q=26 \), \( p=66-13=53 \). \( ATC(26)=40+30.8=70.8 \), now a loss, so some studios exit.

Long-run tangency (illustrative): Entry/exit continues until demand is tangent to ATC, e.g. at \( q^{*}=40 \), \( ATC(40)=40+20=60 \), and demand \( p=90-1.25q \) gives \( p(40)=90-50=40 \)... a manager fitting these curves in practice uses regression estimates of local demand and cost to locate the tangency numerically rather than solving it in closed form, but the qualitative lesson is constant: watch profit margins compress toward zero as visible competitors enter, and expect equilibrium capacity utilization below the level that would minimize unit cost.

Horizontal vs. Vertical Product Differentiation

Differentiation, the source of each firm's downward-sloping demand curve, comes in two economically distinct flavors. Horizontal differentiation means products differ along a dimension where consumers disagree about which variant is "better" (flavor, style, or location), so that even at equal prices, the market splits across varieties according to taste. Vertical differentiation means products differ in a dimension (quality, durability, reliability) that all consumers agree ranks one variant above another; at equal prices, everyone would choose the higher-quality option, so vertically differentiated firms must compete partly on price to sell the lower-quality variant at all. The distinction matters strategically: horizontally differentiated firms can often coexist profitably serving distinct taste segments with limited price competition, while vertically differentiated firms face a built-in tension in which the low-quality producer must either drop price substantially or exit.

The Hotelling Model of Spatial (Horizontal) Competition

The Hotelling linear-city model formalizes horizontal differentiation with a clean, widely applicable geometry. Consumers are distributed uniformly along a line of length 1 (representing physical location, or more abstractly, a taste spectrum, e.g., "mild" to "spicy," or "minimalist" to "feature-rich"). Two firms locate at positions \( x_1 \) and \( x_2 \) and charge prices \( p_1, p_2 \). Each consumer incurs a "transport cost" \( t \) per unit of distance to the firm they patronize (interpreted as disutility from getting a less-preferred variant), and chooses whichever firm minimizes price plus transport cost. With firms located at the two endpoints (maximal differentiation) and linear transport cost, the resulting Nash equilibrium prices are:

\[ p_1^{*} = p_2^{*} = t \]

Each firm earns positive margin over marginal cost (assumed zero here) proportional to the transport cost parameter \( t \), a direct formalization of the intuition that more differentiation (higher \( t \), or equivalently, firms locating further apart on the taste spectrum) softens price competition and raises equilibrium margins, while less differentiation intensifies price competition and drives margins toward marginal cost. This is the rigorous foundation behind a piece of standard business advice, "differentiate to avoid price wars," and it directly explains why monopolistically competitive industries with many close substitutes (commodity-like restaurant chains, generic consumer electronics) tend to see thinner margins than industries where firms successfully carve out clearly distinct positions.

Advertising as a Signal, Not Just Information. Early theories treated advertising purely as informative (telling consumers a product exists and what it costs). A subtler account holds that for "experience goods" (quality is not verifiable before purchase), the mere fact that a firm spends heavily on advertising can itself signal confidence in product quality, because only a firm expecting substantial repeat purchases (and hence high quality) would find heavy up-front advertising spend profitable to recoup. This is why "wasteful"-looking advertising (a celebrity endorsement conveying no verifiable product information) can be economically rational: the expenditure itself, not its content, is the credible signal.

Worked Example: Pricing Response to a New Entrant

A boutique coffee roaster currently faces perceived demand \( p = 24 - 0.3q \) (q in pounds per week) with \( MC=6 \). \( MR=24-0.6q \); setting \( MR=MC \): \( 24-0.6q=6 \Rightarrow q=30 \), \( p=24-9=\$15 \). With \( ATC(30)=6+180/30=12 \), weekly profit \( =(15-12)(30)=\$90 \).

A new specialty roaster opens two blocks away, and the incumbent's demand rotates and shifts inward to \( p=19-0.3q \). Re-solving: \( MR=19-0.6q=6\Rightarrow q=21.7 \), \( p=19-6.5=\$12.5 \). Weekly profit falls to roughly \( (12.5-6-180/21.7)(21.7)\approx(12.5-14.3)(21.7) < 0 \), a short-run loss. Facing this, the incumbent has two broad options consistent with the theory above: exit (if losses persist and no repositioning is viable), or invest in differentiation, such as a loyalty program, a distinctive roast profile, or a taproom experience, to rotate its perceived demand curve back outward and restore a defensible niche, illustrating why non-price competition, not price-matching alone, is the theoretically predicted response to entry in a monopolistically competitive market.

Extension: Chamberlin's Large-Group vs. Small-Group Case

Chamberlin distinguished the "large group" case (many small competitors, each ignoring the effect of its own decisions on any single rival: the standard textbook monopolistic competition model developed above) from the "small group" case, where the number of firms is small enough that each must explicitly account for rivals' reactions to its price or product decisions. The small-group case blends into oligopoly (Chapters 15-16): differentiated-product oligopoly models (Bertrand competition with differentiated goods) generalize the tangency logic here by replacing the single tangency condition with a full system of best-response functions across a small number of interacting firms, each internalizing that a price cut steals share from specific, identifiable rivals rather than from an anonymous "market" residual demand curve.

This distinction matters operationally: in a large-group monopolistically competitive market, a firm can largely ignore any single rival's specific reaction when setting price (rivals are too numerous and each too small to warrant individual game-theoretic modeling); in a small-group (oligopolistic) market, ignoring a specific rival's likely response is precisely the mistake Chapter 16's game-theoretic framework is built to correct.

Monopolistic Competition: Long-Run Equilibrium
Swipe to see the full diagram
Figure 14.1: In long-run equilibrium the firm's downward-sloping demand curve is tangent to (not crossing) its ATC curve, giving zero economic profit but P > MC. The tangency occurs left of minimum efficient scale: the excess-capacity theorem.

Managerial Takeaways

Monopolistic competition is the natural habitat of most consumer-facing small and mid-sized businesses, and its central lesson cuts against the instinct to compete primarily on cost and price. Because free entry guarantees that any purely price-based advantage is temporary (competitors can always match a lower price once they observe it is profitable), sustainable margin in a monopolistically competitive market comes from shifting the firm's own perceived demand curve outward and making it less elastic, not from operating at ever-larger scale to push ATC down. Investments in brand, location, service quality, and switching costs are not "soft" alternatives to real economic strategy; in the Chamberlin-Hotelling framework developed here, they are the primary lever available once price competition alone has been shown, by the zero-profit tangency condition, to erode toward break-even. A manager should also expect, and not necessarily fight, some degree of excess capacity relative to a cost-minimizing benchmark: it is the structural cost of offering customers meaningful variety, and eliminating it entirely would typically require eliminating the very differentiation that lets any individual firm hold price above marginal cost at all. Finally, the Hotelling result that greater differentiation softens price competition gives a concrete design principle for market entry and repositioning decisions: when deciding where to position a new product or location, moving away from a strong incumbent (accepting a smaller, more distinctively-served segment) is frequently more profitable than moving toward the center of the market to maximize reach, precisely because proximity to rivals intensifies price competition and compresses the very margins that justify entry in the first place.

What is the defining geometric feature of long-run equilibrium under monopolistic competition?
Correct. Tangency (not intersection) between the firm's downward-sloping demand curve and its ATC curve is what pins down zero economic profit while price still exceeds marginal cost.
Do not confuse monopolistic competition with oligopoly. The "large group" assumption, that each firm treats rivals' reactions as negligible when setting its own price, is what makes the simple tangency solution in this chapter valid. If the number of active competitors in a real market is small enough that a manager would genuinely expect a specific named rival to respond to a price move, the correct tool is not the Chamberlin tangency model but the explicit strategic reasoning of Chapters 15 and 16. A common analytical error is applying the zero-profit tangency logic to a market that is, in fact, a small-group oligopoly in disguise (e.g., three national chains with many small independent competitors filling out an HHI calculation): the large incumbents' pricing is oligopolistic even if the market as a whole looks fragmented on paper. A reliable practical test is simply to ask whether a specific, named competitor would plausibly notice and react to a contemplated price change within a relevant planning horizon; if the honest answer is yes, oligopoly tools apply, whatever the raw firm count in the industry happens to be.

Practice Problems: Chapter 14

1. Explain why P = ATC in long-run monopolistic competition equilibrium but P ≠ MC, and why this is different from perfect competition.

2. A firm's short-run profit attracts three new entrants. Describe, qualitatively, what happens to the shape and position of the firm's perceived demand curve.

3. Is excess capacity necessarily a market failure? Summarize the counter-argument based on product variety.

4. Give two real-world examples of non-price competition you would expect in a monopolistically competitive industry, and explain the underlying economic logic.

Chapter 15: Oligopoly: Interdependence, Kinked Demand, Collusion

Before the Formulas: When There Are Only a Few Rivals, Their Reactions Matter

Picture a small city with only three gas stations. If one station drops its price, the other two will almost certainly notice within a day and probably cut their own prices too, because losing customers to a cheaper rival right across the street is too costly to ignore. Contrast that with a city of 300 gas stations, where one station's price change barely registers with any single rival. A market with just a handful of sellers, where each firm's pricing decision visibly affects, and is watched by, its rivals, is called an oligopoly (from Greek: "few sellers"). This chapter is about the extra layer of thinking a manager needs in that situation: not just "what price is best for me?" but "what price is best for me, given how my rivals will likely respond?"

Strategic Interdependence

An oligopoly is a market with a small number of sellers, each large enough that its output and pricing decisions visibly affect rivals, and each aware that rivals will react. This single fact, strategic interdependence, is what separates oligopoly analytically from every market structure studied so far. A perfectly competitive firm and a monopolist both solve an optimization problem taking some external constraint (price, or the entire demand curve) as fixed. An oligopolist cannot: any pricing decision must anticipate how rivals will respond, and rivals know this too. This chapter develops the classical models of interdependent pricing, kinked demand and tacit collusion, and sets up the formal game-theoretic toolkit of Chapter 16. A useful way to place oligopoly within the broader market-structure spectrum developed across this module is to note that it nests both extremes as limiting cases: as the number of firms shrinks to one, standard oligopoly models collapse to the monopoly outcome of Chapter 13; as the number of firms grows large and each firm's individual influence on price vanishes, they converge toward the perfectly competitive outcome of Chapter 12. The genuinely new content in this chapter is everything that happens in between, where neither limiting simplification applies and a firm's optimal action depends explicitly on beliefs about what rivals will do.

Oligopoly. A market with a small number of interdependent firms, each possessing some market power, where strategic behavior, anticipating and reacting to rivals’ choices, is central to pricing and output decisions. Contrast with perfect competition (many firms, no strategic interaction) and monopoly (one firm, no rivals to react to).

15.1 The Kinked Demand Curve and Price Rigidity

One classic model was proposed to explain a commonly observed fact: oligopoly prices often stay fixed for long periods even as costs fluctuate. The model rests on an asymmetric conjecture about rival behavior. Suppose a firm is currently pricing at \( P_0 \), selling \( Q_0 \). It conjectures that:

• If it raises price above \( P_0 \), rivals will not follow; they are happy to let the firm lose customers to them. Demand for a price increase is therefore highly elastic (recall: elastic means customers are very sensitive to price and will readily switch away, so here the firm loses a lot of market share).

• If it lowers price below \( P_0 \), rivals will match the cut to avoid losing customers. Demand for a price decrease is therefore relatively inelastic (little gain in market share, since everyone cuts together, and total demand for the category grows only modestly).

The perceived demand curve is thus kinked at \( (Q_0, P_0) \): flat (elastic) above the kink, steep (inelastic) below it.

The mechanism is the discontinuity in MR, not merely the kink in demand. This is the step most treatments get wrong. Because the demand curve has two different slopes on either side of the kink, it has two different, non-coincident marginal revenue curves: the (flatter) upper segment implies an MR curve that is relatively high just left of \( Q_0 \); the (steeper) lower segment implies an MR curve that jumps discontinuously downward at \( Q_0 \) before continuing. The result is a vertical gap in the MR curve directly below the kink. Any marginal cost curve that happens to pass through this gap still intersects "MR = MC" at exactly \( Q_0 \), so the profit-maximizing price and quantity do not change even though marginal cost has shifted. This is the actual explanation for observed price rigidity: cost shocks are absorbed by movement of the MR = MC intersection point along the vertical gap, not by any change in price, as long as the shock is not large enough to push MC out of the gap altogether.

15.2 Limitations of the Kinked Demand Model

The model has two well-known weaknesses a manager should keep in mind. First, it explains price rigidity once a price is established, but it never explains how \( P_0 \) was determined in the first place; it is a model of the "stickiness" of an existing focal price, not a model of price formation. Second, empirical tests have found that oligopoly prices do not, in fact, move asymmetrically in the way the model predicts as often as the theory suggests; observed price rigidity is sometimes better explained by menu costs, long-term contracts, or implicit collusion sustained by repeated interaction (Chapter 16) than by kinked conjectures alone. Still, the model remains a valuable illustration of how firms' beliefs about rivals' reactions, not just cost and demand fundamentals, shape pricing behavior, a lesson that generalizes far beyond the specific kinked-demand story.

15.3 Cartels, Collusion, and the Incentive to Cheat

If oligopolists could act as a single decision-maker, they would maximize joint profit by restricting total output to the monopoly level and dividing the resulting monopoly profit among themselves. This is the logic of a cartel. The formal cartel problem: choose \( q_1, \dots, q_n \) to maximize \( \sum_i \pi_i \), which (with identical constant marginal costs \( c \)) yields the same \( MR(Q)=c \) condition as ordinary monopoly, with \( Q \) then allocated across members (often, though not necessarily efficiently, via output quotas).

Before turning to the instability of cartels, it is worth noting that the same joint-profit-maximization logic applies whether the coordination is explicit (an illegal cartel agreement) or purely tacit (independently-reached but mutually understood output restriction sustained through repeated interaction, examined formally in Chapter 16). Antitrust law treats these very differently: explicit price-fixing agreements are per se illegal in most jurisdictions, while tacit coordination that arises purely from each firm's independent, repeated best-response reasoning is generally lawful, however similar the resulting market outcome may look. The fundamental instability of cartels is that each member has a private incentive to secretly produce beyond its quota. If rivals hold output at the cartel level, a single firm expanding output slightly raises its own profit (it sells more units at only a marginally lower price, since it alone is deviating) while imposing most of the price-decline cost on the other members. This is a coordination problem with the same structure as the prisoner's dilemma formalized in Chapter 16: joint restriction is collectively optimal, but unilateral cheating is individually optimal, so without an enforcement mechanism (monitoring, punishment for deviation, repeated interaction with credible retaliation) cartels tend to break down from within. OPEC's history, marked by periods of successful output restriction interrupted by member cheating and price collapses, is the canonical real-world illustration.

Key Result. A cartel replicates the monopoly outcome for the industry as a whole (restrict Q, raise P), but is internally unstable: each member's private marginal incentive is to expand output beyond its quota, because the cost of the resulting price decline is shared across all members while the revenue gain from the extra units accrues to the cheater alone.

15.4 Concentration and Market Power: The Herfindahl-Hirschman Index

Managers assessing competitive intensity, and regulators assessing merger effects, commonly use the Herfindahl-Hirschman Index (HHI), the sum of squared market shares (expressed in percentage points, so shares sum to 100):

\[ HHI = \sum_{i=1}^{n} s_i^2 \]

A single monopolist has \( HHI=10{,}000 \); \( n \) equal-sized firms give \( HHI = 10{,}000/n \). U.S. antitrust guidelines treat markets with \( HHI<1500 \) as unconcentrated, \( 1500{-}2500 \) as moderately concentrated, and above \( 2500 \) as highly concentrated, with mergers that raise HHI by more than 200 points in an already-concentrated market drawing heightened scrutiny. The HHI is closely linked to oligopoly theory: under Cournot competition with linear demand and identical constant marginal cost, the industry-average Lerner Index equals \( HHI/(10{,}000\,|\epsilon|) \): concentration and market power are formally, not just intuitively, connected.

Worked Example: Kinked Demand and a Cost Shock

Three rival firms currently price at \( P_0 = \$40 \), each selling \( Q_0 = 1{,}000 \) units. Suppose the "upper" (elastic) MR value just left of \( Q_0 \) is \$34 and the "lower" (inelastic) MR value just right of \( Q_0 \) is \$22, a gap of \$12 per unit.

Scenario A: small cost shock. An input-cost increase raises marginal cost from \$25 to \$30. Since \$30 still lies inside the \$22-\$34 gap, \( MR=MC \) still holds at \( Q_0 \): the firm's optimal response is to hold price at \$40. This matches the empirical observation of price stickiness after moderate cost shocks in oligopoly.

Scenario B: large cost shock. A severe input shortage raises MC to \$38, outside the gap. Now MC exceeds even the elastic-segment MR value, so the firm's optimum shifts: it must raise price above \$40, and because the shock is common to all rivals (they face the same input market), the kinked-demand conjecture "rivals won't follow a price increase" breaks down: rivals raise price too, and the market re-anchors at a new, higher \( P_0 \).

Cournot Competition: Quantity-Setting Rivals

The Cournot model, the oldest formal oligopoly model, assumes firms simultaneously choose quantities, and price is then determined by total industry output via the market demand curve. Each firm maximizes its own profit taking the rival's quantity as given, generating a best-response (reaction) function. For linear demand \( p=a-b(q_1+q_2) \) and constant marginal cost \( c \) for both firms, Firm 1's profit is \( \pi_1 = [a-b(q_1+q_2)-c]q_1 \); maximizing over \( q_1 \) gives the reaction function:

\[ q_1 = \frac{a-c-bq_2}{2b} \]

By symmetry, Firm 2's reaction function is analogous. Solving the two reaction functions simultaneously gives the Cournot-Nash equilibrium: \( q_1^{*}=q_2^{*}=(a-c)/3b \), industry output \( Q^{*}=2(a-c)/3b \), and price \( p^{*}=(a+2c)/3 \). Compare this to the monopoly quantity \( (a-c)/2b \) and the competitive quantity \( (a-c)/b \): Cournot duopoly output lies strictly between them. Generalizing to \( n \) symmetric firms, industry output converges toward the competitive level as \( n\to\infty \), and the industry-wide Lerner Index equals \( HHI/(10{,}000\,|\epsilon|) \) under Cournot conduct, the formal link between market concentration (Chapter 15.4) and market power referenced above.

Bertrand Competition and the Bertrand Paradox

The Bertrand model criticizes the Cournot quantity-setting assumption, arguing that firms actually compete by setting prices, with customers buying entirely from whichever firm charges less (splitting demand if prices are tied). With homogeneous products and identical constant marginal cost \( c \), the striking result is the Bertrand Paradox: even with just two firms, the unique Nash equilibrium is \( p_1^{*}=p_2^{*}=c \), the fully competitive outcome. The logic: at any price above \( c \), either rival has an incentive to undercut by an arbitrarily small amount and capture the entire market, so any price above cost cannot be an equilibrium; at a price below \( c \), a firm loses money on every unit sold, so neither firm will go below cost. Only \( p=c \) survives as mutual best response. This is a sharp warning against the naive intuition that "only two firms means market power is safe": the form of competition (price vs. quantity) matters enormously, and the paradox is resolved in practice by exactly the frictions absent from the stark Bertrand model: capacity constraints (research shows Cournot-like outcomes re-emerge when firms first commit capacity, then compete on price), product differentiation (softening the "customers buy entirely from the cheaper firm" assumption, as in Chapter 14's Hotelling model), and repeated interaction (Chapter 16), all of which restore positive margins.

Stackelberg Leadership: The First-Mover Advantage in Quantities. The Stackelberg model modifies the Cournot simultaneous-move assumption by letting one firm (the leader) commit to its quantity first, observed by the follower before the follower chooses. Solving by backward induction, the leader anticipates the follower's Cournot reaction function and internalizes the effect of its own quantity choice on the follower's response, yielding \( q_L^{*}=(a-c)/2b \) (the monopoly quantity) and \( q_F^{*}=(a-c)/4b \): the leader produces more and earns strictly higher profit than in simultaneous Cournot play, while the follower produces less and earns less. Credible first-mover commitment (visible, hard-to-reverse capacity investment, as previewed in Chapter 16.4) is therefore strategically valuable precisely because it changes the game from simultaneous to sequential in the leader's favor.

Contestable Markets: Competition From the Threat of Entry

The theory of contestable markets makes a provocative claim: the number of firms currently operating in a market may matter less for pricing discipline than the ease with which potential entrants could enter and exit. If entry requires no sunk (unrecoverable) cost and can occur quickly enough to undercut any incumbent pricing above cost before the incumbent can retaliate ("hit-and-run entry"), then even a single incumbent firm may be forced to price near competitive levels simply to deter entry, regardless of how concentrated the industry currently appears by an HHI calculation. The managerial implication is that concentration measures alone can be misleading: a highly concentrated market with low entry barriers (many airline routes, for instance, where aircraft can be redeployed quickly) may exhibit near-competitive pricing discipline, while a less concentrated market with high sunk costs of entry may sustain persistent super-competitive pricing.

Kinked Demand & Price Rigidity
Swipe to see the full diagram
Figure 15.1: The kinked demand curve and its discontinuous marginal revenue. As long as marginal cost shifts remain inside the MR gap directly below the kink, the profit-maximizing price and quantity are unchanged: the actual mechanism behind observed oligopoly price rigidity.

Managerial Takeaways

Oligopoly strategy requires holding two ideas in tension simultaneously: rivals' likely reactions must shape every pricing and output decision, yet the specific model of interaction, whether kinked-demand price-followership, Cournot quantity competition, Bertrand price competition, or Stackelberg leadership, changes the predicted outcome dramatically, so a manager's first analytical task in any concentrated market is to correctly identify which competitive game is actually being played before applying any single model's prescriptions. A market that looks identical on an HHI or share basis can behave completely differently depending on whether firms compete primarily by announcing capacity/output plans (pushing outcomes toward the Cournot intermediate case) or by matching or undercutting posted prices on a largely homogeneous product (pushing outcomes toward the much more competitive Bertrand case, absent capacity constraints or differentiation). The kinked-demand model's specific lesson, that observed price stability can coexist with real, unexercised market power, is itself a warning against reading price rigidity as evidence of weak competitive intensity; the underlying MR discontinuity can mask a great deal of latent responsiveness to any cost or demand shock large enough to push marginal cost outside the gap. Finally, the fundamental instability of collusive arrangements documented here is precisely why Chapter 16's repeated-game machinery, not one-shot reasoning, is required to understand when tacit coordination on high prices can actually be sustained in practice.

In the kinked demand model, what explains why an oligopolist's price stays fixed after a moderate cost increase?
Correct. The kink in demand creates a vertical discontinuity in marginal revenue. As long as the shifted MC curve remains inside that gap, MR = MC still holds at the original quantity, so price does not change.
Cournot, Bertrand, and kinked demand are complements, not competitors, as models. A common mistake is to ask "which oligopoly model is correct?" as though only one could apply. In reality, different industries genuinely operate closer to different assumptions: capital-intensive industries with long lead times to adjust output (steel, semiconductors, airline seat capacity set months in advance) behave more like Cournot quantity competitors; industries with flexible, easily-adjusted prices and largely homogeneous products (many retail and commodity markets) behave closer to Bertrand price competitors; and industries where firms observe each other's posted prices closely and adjust slowly, with a strong status-quo price anchor, exhibit kinked-demand-like rigidity. Correctly diagnosing which friction dominates in a given market is itself a critical managerial skill, prior to and more important than mechanically applying any one model's formulas.

Practice Problems: Chapter 15

1. Explain why the kink in the demand curve alone is not sufficient to generate price rigidity: what additional feature of MR is required?

2. A cartel of four firms with identical MC = 15 faces market demand p = 100 − q. Find the joint-profit-maximizing price and total quantity, then explain why each member has an incentive to secretly exceed its 25% quota.

3. Market shares are 40%, 30%, 20%, 10%. Compute the HHI. Would a merger between the two smallest firms likely trigger antitrust scrutiny under the 200-point-increase guideline?

4. Why did Stigler's empirical tests cast doubt on the kinked demand model as a complete theory of oligopoly pricing?

Chapter 16: Game Theory for Managers: Strategic Decision-Making

Before the Formulas: What Is "Game Theory," Really?

Think about a simple everyday standoff: two food trucks decide, independently and at the same time, whether to park on the busy corner or the quiet side street. What each one should do depends entirely on what it expects the other to do. If both expect the other to take the busy corner, maybe one should pick the side street to avoid a crowded, price-cutting standoff there. Game theory is simply the formal toolkit for reasoning through situations like this, where your best choice depends on someone else's choice, and their best choice depends on yours. It sounds abstract, but managers use this kind of reasoning constantly: should we launch a promotion now or wait to see if a competitor launches first? Should we build extra factory capacity to look intimidating to a potential new entrant? This chapter gives that everyday intuition a precise, checkable structure.

Thinking One Move Ahead

Game theory formalizes exactly the strategic reasoning that Chapter 15 introduced informally: what should I do, given that my rivals are simultaneously reasoning about what to do given what they expect me to do? Game theory is now the standard language for pricing, entry, capacity, R&D, and negotiation decisions under rivalry. This chapter builds the manager's toolkit: dominant strategies, Nash equilibrium, the prisoner's dilemma, and the effect of repeated play. Game theory is the unifying language beneath every model in Chapter 15: Cournot, Bertrand, Stackelberg, and the cartel-stability problem are all, formally, specific games with specific strategy spaces and payoff structures, and the general equilibrium concept developed here, mutual best response, is exactly what "Cournot-Nash equilibrium" and "Bertrand-Nash equilibrium" both name. Understanding this chapter first would, in principle, make every equilibrium concept in Chapter 15 a direct application rather than a new idea; the two chapters are presented in the reverse pedagogical order here specifically so that the abstract machinery of this chapter arrives already motivated by concrete oligopoly pricing problems.

Normal-Form Game. A game is specified by (1) a set of players, (2) a set of strategies available to each player, and (3) a payoff function giving each player's payoff for every combination of strategies chosen. A dominant strategy for a player is one that yields a higher payoff than every other available strategy, regardless of what any other player chooses.

16.1 Dominant Strategies and the Prisoner's Dilemma

Consider two firms, A and B, each choosing between a "High Price" and a "Low Price" strategy. If both hold price high, each earns a healthy \$20 (thousand); if both cut price, competition erodes profit to \$10 each; if one cuts while the rival holds high, the price-cutter grabs share and earns \$25 while the rival is left with only \$5. Ask: what is each firm's best response to each possible action by the rival?

• If B holds High: A's best response is Low (25 > 20).

• If B goes Low: A's best response is Low (10 > 5).

Low Price is a dominant strategy for A: it is strictly better no matter what B does. By the symmetry of the payoffs, Low Price is dominant for B as well. Since both firms play their dominant strategy, the unique Nash equilibrium (in plain terms: an outcome where neither player, knowing what the other chose, would want to switch their own choice) is (Low, Low), yielding (\$10, \$10), even though both firms would strictly prefer the (High, High) outcome of (\$20, \$20). This is the prisoner's dilemma structure, and it is the canonical illustration of a general and important warning for managers:

Nash equilibrium is not necessarily Pareto efficient. A Nash equilibrium is a strategy profile in which every player is playing a best response to every other player's strategy: no one can unilaterally improve their payoff. This is entirely different from saying the outcome maximizes total or even individual welfare. In the prisoner's dilemma, both players individually rational, both best-responding, and the joint result is worse for both of them than a jointly (but not individually) sustainable alternative. Do not equate "equilibrium" with "optimal"; this conflation is one of the most common errors in applying game theory to business strategy.
Nash Equilibrium (formal definition). A strategy profile \( (s_1^{*}, \dots, s_n^{*}) \) is a Nash equilibrium if, for every player \( i \), \( s_i^{*} \) maximizes player \( i \)'s payoff given the strategies \( s_{-i}^{*} \) chosen by all other players: \( \pi_i(s_i^{*}, s_{-i}^{*}) \geq \pi_i(s_i, s_{-i}^{*}) \) for every alternative strategy \( s_i \). Finding it: for each player, find the best response to every possible strategy of rivals; a Nash equilibrium is any cell where every player's strategy is simultaneously a best response to the others’.

16.2 Finding Equilibrium Without a Dominant Strategy

A player's strategy that is dominant is always a best response, but the converse is not true: a strategy can be someone's optimal choice against one particular rival action while being a poor choice against another, so identifying a Nash equilibrium generally requires checking best responses cell by cell rather than assuming a shortcut dominant-strategy solution exists. Not every game has a dominant strategy for every player. When it does not, the manager must construct the best-response correspondence for each player (the optimal action conditional on each possible rival action) and look for a cell where both players' strategies are simultaneously best responses to each other. This is the general method underlying the game-matrix diagram below: underline (or otherwise mark) each player's best response in every column/row, and the Nash equilibrium is any cell where both players' entries are marked.

16.3 Repeated Games, Tit-for-Tat, and Sustaining Cooperation

The one-shot prisoner's dilemma predicts mutual defection (Low Price), but real business rivalries are rarely one-shot; firms interact period after period. Famous computer tournaments found that a remarkably simple strategy, tit-for-tat (cooperate on the first move; thereafter, do whatever your rival did last period), performed better than far more complex strategies in a repeated prisoner's dilemma. The logic: tit-for-tat rewards cooperation (sustaining High Price as long as the rival does) and punishes defection swiftly (one period of retaliation), while remaining forgiving (a rival who returns to cooperation is immediately met with cooperation again).

Formally, if the game is repeated infinitely (or with sufficiently high probability of continuation \( \delta \) each period, interpreted as a discount factor), tacit collusion on (High, High) can be sustained as a subgame-perfect equilibrium via "grim trigger" or tit-for-tat punishment strategies, provided the discount factor is high enough that the future value of continued cooperation outweighs the one-period temptation to defect (the Folk Theorem). The condition for firm A to prefer cooperating forever over defecting once and being punished forever is approximately:

\[ \frac{\pi^{coop}}{1-\delta} \geq \pi^{deviate} + \frac{\delta\,\pi^{punish}}{1-\delta} \]

the present value of perpetual cooperation must exceed the one-time gain from cheating plus the discounted present value of the subsequent punishment phase. This explains why tacit collusion (implicit price coordination without explicit communication, which would be illegal price-fixing) is more commonly observed and more stable in concentrated industries with few, patient, repeatedly-interacting firms (exactly the oligopoly setting of Chapter 15) than in fragmented markets with many anonymous, short-horizon players.

16.4 Sequential Games and First-Mover Advantage

When one player moves before the other and the second player observes the first player's action before choosing, the game is sequential and is best analyzed by working backward from the end (backward induction) rather than by a simultaneous payoff matrix. A classic managerial application is capacity commitment: if Firm A can credibly commit to a large capacity investment before Firm B decides whether to enter, and if that commitment makes aggressive post-entry competition credible, Firm A may deter entry altogether: a first-mover advantage. The key requirement is commitment: the first move must be costly to reverse (sunk capacity, a long-term contract, a public price announcement with reputational cost to reneging) or the threat is not credible and the second mover will rationally ignore it. The insight that deliberately reducing your own future options can be a source of strategic strength is the intuition behind this result, formalized by the concept of subgame-perfect equilibrium, which requires that every threat and promise in a strategy be credible at the point it would actually need to be carried out.

Worked Example: Finding Nash Equilibrium by Best Response

Two airlines choose Capacity: "Add Route" or "Don't." Payoffs (Airline 1, Airline 2), in $ millions:

(Add, Add) = \( (-2, -2) \)    (Add, Don't) = (8, 0)    (Don't, Add) = (0, 8)    (Don't, Don't) = (3, 3)

Best responses for Airline 1: if 2 Adds, 1's best response is Don't (\( 0 > -2 \)). If 2 Doesn't, 1's best response is Add (\( 8 > 3 \)). There is no dominant strategy; 1's optimal choice depends on 2's action.

By symmetry, Airline 2 has the same conditional best responses.

Nash equilibria: check each cell for mutual best response. (Add, Don't): 1 is best-responding (8 is 1's best against Don't) and 2 is best-responding (0 is 2's best against Add, since Adding would give 2 a payoff of \( -2 \)). Both conditions hold, so (Add, Don't) is a Nash equilibrium. By symmetry, (Don't, Add) is also a Nash equilibrium. This is a coordination game with two pure-strategy Nash equilibria (plus a mixed-strategy equilibrium): each airline wants to be the one that adds the route, and whichever moves first (or signals commitment first) may secure the favorable outcome, illustrating first-mover advantage in a sequential extension of this game.

Mixed-Strategy Nash Equilibrium

Not every game has a Nash equilibrium in pure (deterministic) strategies. Consider a simplified market-entry deterrence game: an incumbent decides whether to "Fight" or "Accommodate" a potential entrant, who decides whether to "Enter" or "Stay Out." If the entrant Stays Out, the incumbent earns a monopoly payoff regardless of its (unobserved) fighting intention, and the entrant earns 0. If the entrant Enters, the incumbent prefers to Accommodate (higher payoff to it than Fighting) but would credibly threaten to Fight if that threat were believed; the entrant prefers to Enter only if it expects Accommodation. With no pure-strategy equilibrium sustaining a credible threat (Fighting is not actually the incumbent's best response once entry has already occurred, so the threat is not subgame-perfect; this is the "chain store paradox"), the equilibrium instead involves the incumbent randomizing between Fight and Accommodate with specific probabilities that make the entrant exactly indifferent between Entering and Staying Out, and the entrant randomizing so as to make the incumbent indifferent between its own two actions.

Formally, in a mixed-strategy Nash equilibrium, each player randomizes over available actions with probabilities chosen so that every action played with positive probability yields the same expected payoff (otherwise the player would deviate to the higher-payoff pure action). Mixed strategies are not merely a mathematical technicality: they correctly describe strategic settings with an element of unpredictability by design: audit and inspection games (a regulator randomizes inspection intensity so a firm cannot simply learn "when it's safe to cheat"), pricing games with matching-guarantee promotions, and penalty-kick strategy in sports are all empirically well-described by mixed-strategy equilibrium play.

Extensive-Form Games and Backward Induction

When decisions unfold over time with each player observing prior moves, the extensive form (a game tree) represents the strategic situation more naturally than a simultaneous-move payoff matrix. The solution concept, subgame-perfect Nash equilibrium, requires that a strategy be a Nash equilibrium not just for the whole game but for every subgame (every decision point reachable during play), ruling out equilibria that rely on incredible threats or promises. The solution technique is backward induction: start at the final decision nodes, determine the optimal action there, then work backward, at each earlier node assuming all players correctly anticipate the (already-solved) optimal play that follows.

Backward induction is exactly what resolves the entry-deterrence puzzle above once the game is made properly sequential and complete information is assumed: if entry has already occurred, an incumbent rationally compares its own payoff from Fighting versus Accommodating at that point, ignoring the (already sunk, from its perspective) desire to have deterred entry in the first place. If Accommodating strictly dominates Fighting once entry has occurred, a rational entrant, correctly anticipating this, will always Enter. The "threat" to Fight is empty unless the incumbent can find some way to make Fighting genuinely optimal ex post (for example, by making a costly, observable commitment beforehand, such as building excess capacity specifically so that flooding the market with output actually becomes the profit-maximizing response to entry, converting the incredible threat into a credible one).

Extension: Bounded Applicability, Auctions and Mechanism Design

The strategic reasoning developed in this chapter, anticipating rivals' best responses and verifying credibility via backward induction, extends directly to competitive bidding. A manager bidding in a first-price sealed-bid auction must shade their bid below their true valuation (bidding one's true value is not a best response, since it guarantees zero surplus conditional on winning), while in a second-price (Vickrey) auction, bidding one's true valuation is a dominant strategy: the mechanism is specifically designed so that truthful bidding is individually optimal regardless of what other bidders do. Recognizing which auction format a firm is bidding into, and adjusting strategy accordingly, is a direct, high-stakes managerial application of the dominant-strategy reasoning introduced in Section 16.1. This connects forward to the broader field of information economics and mechanism design (Chapter 21) built on the same equilibrium foundations developed here.

Two-Firm Pricing Game (Prisoner's Dilemma)
Swipe to see the full diagram
Figure 16.1: Payoffs (Firm A, Firm B) in $ thousands. Both firms have a dominant strategy of Low Price, giving the unique Nash equilibrium (Low, Low) = (10, 10), strictly worse for both than the jointly cooperative (High, High) = (20, 20), which is Pareto efficient but not individually stable.

Managerial Takeaways

Game theory's most important discipline for a manager is not any single equilibrium formula but the habit of reasoning it enforces: before committing to a strategy, explicitly map out the rival's available responses and their payoffs, verify that any threat or promise embedded in the plan would still be optimal to carry out if it were actually tested (the backward-induction, subgame-perfection standard), and never conflate "the equilibrium outcome" with "the best possible outcome." The prisoner's dilemma structure recurs constantly in business (capacity races, advertising wars, R&D duplication, price wars) precisely because privately rational behavior does not automatically aggregate into collectively rational outcomes. Where a firm finds itself trapped in a dilemma-structured rivalry, the escape routes identified in this chapter are concrete and actionable: shift from one-shot to repeated interaction (making retaliation credible and future cooperation valuable), find or create a credible commitment device that changes the game's structure in the firm's favor (converting a sequential game with first-mover advantage from theory into practice), or, where mixed-strategy reasoning applies, recognize that some managerial decisions are optimally unpredictable by design rather than a failure of planning. None of this licenses collusive communication or price-fixing agreements, which remain per se illegal in most jurisdictions regardless of the economic logic that might rationalize them. The legal line is between independently reaching a cooperative-looking equilibrium through the structure of repeated interaction (lawful) and reaching an explicit agreement to coordinate (unlawful), a distinction developed further alongside the regulatory material in Chapter 24.

In a one-shot prisoner's dilemma, why do both players end up at the mutually worse outcome?
Correct. Each player rationally plays their dominant strategy, and the resulting equilibrium is worse for both than the cooperative outcome. Nash equilibrium describes mutual best responses, not welfare maximization.

Practice Problems: Chapter 16

2. Explain, using the Folk Theorem condition, why tacit collusion is more likely to be sustained when firms interact repeatedly and are patient (high δ) than in a one-shot game.

3. Describe a real managerial situation where credible commitment (making a threat costly to reverse) could produce a first-mover advantage, and identify what would make the commitment credible.

4. In the airline capacity game in the worked example, identify both pure-strategy Nash equilibria and explain, intuitively, why neither the "Add/Add" nor the "Don't/Don't" cell is an equilibrium.

Chapter 17: Price Discrimination

Before the Formulas: Why Does a Student Discount Exist?

Movie theaters routinely charge students less than adults for the exact same seat, showing the exact same film. It costs the theater no less to seat a student than to seat anyone else, so why the discount? The theater has figured out that students, on average, have less money and more free time (more substitutes for how to spend an evening), so they'll skip the movie entirely rather than pay full price, while a working adult is more likely to pay regardless. By charging students less, the theater keeps that seat filled instead of empty. This everyday practice, called price discrimination, charging different customers different prices for essentially the same thing, is one of the most powerful and widely used pricing tools available to any business with even a little bit of pricing power, and this chapter works out exactly when it's possible and how to do it well.

Charging Different Customers Different Prices

Price discrimination is the practice of selling the same (or nearly the same) product to different customers at different prices, where the price differences are not explained by cost differences. Airlines, pharmaceutical companies, software firms, and universities are textbook practitioners. Price discrimination is often the single most powerful lever a manager with any market power can pull to increase profit, because it converts consumer surplus that would otherwise be left with the customer into producer profit, and under some conditions it can even increase total output and total welfare relative to uniform pricing. The tools in this chapter and its companion, Chapter 18, together constitute the applied core of a pricing manager's toolkit: where Chapters 13 through 16 explained how much market power a firm has and where that power comes from, this chapter and the next explain how to convert that power into profit as efficiently as possible, given whatever constraints on information, sorting, and resale the firm actually faces in its specific market.

Three Necessary Conditions for Price Discrimination. A firm can profitably price-discriminate only if all three hold:

(1) Market power. The firm must face a downward-sloping demand curve (some ability to set price above marginal cost); a perfectly competitive price-taker cannot price-discriminate at all.

(2) Ability to sort consumers by willingness to pay. The firm must be able to identify, or induce self-selection among, groups or individuals with different reservation prices, via observable characteristics (age, location, membership), purchase behavior (quantity, timing), or a self-selecting menu.

(3) No resale / no arbitrage. Customers who buy at the low price must be unable to resell profitably to customers who would otherwise pay the high price. If arbitrage is possible, resale competes away the price gap and discrimination collapses to a single effective price.

This is stated as three separate conditions deliberately: "charging different prices to different people" is a description of the outcome, not an explanation of when it is feasible. All three conditions must hold simultaneously.

17.1 First-Degree (Perfect) Price Discrimination

Under first-degree discrimination, the firm charges each unit at each customer's exact willingness to pay, extracting the entire area under the demand curve as producer surplus. The profit-maximizing rule becomes simple: sell every unit for which willingness to pay exceeds marginal cost, i.e., produce up to \( q \) where \( p(q) = MC(q) \), the same efficient quantity as perfect competition. Remarkably, perfect price discrimination is efficient: there is no deadweight loss, because output expands all the way to the point where price equals marginal cost. The entire surplus, however, accrues to the firm rather than being split with consumers. Pure first-degree discrimination is rare in practice (it requires knowing each individual's exact reservation price) but is closely approximated by negotiated B2B contracts, personalized online pricing algorithms, and per-unit versioned tariffs.

17.2 Second-Degree Price Discrimination: Self-Selection

The three degrees of price discrimination are best organized by what information the firm has about each customer: first-degree assumes the firm observes each individual's exact reservation price; third-degree assumes the firm observes only group membership; and second-degree assumes the firm observes nothing about the individual customer beyond what that customer's own choices among a self-selecting menu reveal. When the firm cannot observe each customer's type directly, it can still discriminate by offering a menu of price-quantity or price-quality bundles designed so that different customer types voluntarily self-select into different options, such as quantity discounts, airline fare classes (economy/business), or "good-better-best" product versioning. The design problem must satisfy two constraints for each customer type \( \theta \): individual rationality (the chosen bundle gives non-negative surplus) and incentive compatibility (the type genuinely prefers its intended bundle to any other bundle on the menu). The classic qualitative result is "no distortion at the top, distortion at the bottom": the highest-value segment is offered the efficient (cost-based) quantity/quality, while lower segments are offered a deliberately reduced quantity or quality, not because it is efficient, but to make the low-tier option unattractive to the high-value segment, preserving the firm's ability to charge the high segment more.

17.3 Third-Degree Price Discrimination: The Inverse Elasticity Rule

When customers can be sorted into observable groups with different demand elasticities (recall: elasticity measures how sensitive a group's buying is to price: a group with high elasticity walks away easily, while a group with low elasticity keeps buying even if the price rises) (students vs. non-students, business vs. leisure travelers, domestic vs. export markets) but not sorted individual by individual, the firm sets marginal revenue equal across all segments and equal to marginal cost:

\[ MR_1(q_1) = MR_2(q_2) = \cdots = MR_n(q_n) = MC(Q), \qquad Q=\sum_i q_i \]

Applying the Lerner Index segment by segment gives the inverse elasticity rule:

\[ \frac{P_i - MC}{P_i} = \frac{1}{|\epsilon_i|} \quad \Longrightarrow \quad P_i = \frac{MC}{1-\frac{1}{|\epsilon_i|}} \]

The segment with the less elastic demand (fewer substitutes, more price-insensitive) is charged the higher price.
