It can be shown that whether this raises or lowers total welfare relative to uniform pricing is ambiguous: if discrimination does not change total output, it merely reallocates units from high-value to low-value consumers, reducing welfare; but if discrimination brings a previously unserved low-value segment into the market (one that would not have been served at the uniform price), total output rises and welfare can improve even though the firm's profit unambiguously rises.

Key Result. Three conditions are jointly necessary for price discrimination: market power, an ability to sort consumers by willingness to pay, and no resale. Degree of discrimination: first (perfect, per-unit personalized pricing, efficient but zero consumer surplus), second (menus/self-selection, distortion at the bottom), third (group pricing by the inverse elasticity rule, ambiguous welfare effect).

Worked Example: Third-Degree Discrimination for an Airline Route

An airline serves business travelers (Market A, less elastic) and leisure travelers (Market B, more elastic) on the same route, MC = \$20 per seat.

Market A demand: \( p_A = 90 - 2q_A \), so \( MR_A = 90-4q_A \). Set \( MR_A=MC \): \( 90-4q_A=20 \Rightarrow q_A=17.5 \), \( p_A = 90-35=\$55 \).

Market B demand: \( p_B = 70-q_B \), so \( MR_B=70-2q_B \). Set \( MR_B=MC \): \( 70-2q_B=20 \Rightarrow q_B=25 \), \( p_B=70-25=\$45 \).

Business travelers (less elastic, at their equilibrium: elasticity \( |\epsilon_A| = p_A/(2q_A) \)… more directly, using the Lerner Index: \( L_A=(55-20)/55=0.636 \Rightarrow |\epsilon_A|=1.57 \); \( L_B=(45-20)/45=0.556\Rightarrow|\epsilon_B|=1.80 \)) pay a higher price precisely because their demand is less elastic, exactly as the inverse elasticity rule predicts. Total profit: \( (55-20)(17.5)+(45-20)(25) = 612.5+625 = \$1{,}237.50 \), compared to a lower profit achievable under any single uniform price across both segments.

Implementing Discrimination in Practice: Coupons, Rebates, and Versioning

Managers rarely announce "we charge Group A more than Group B"; instead, discrimination is implemented through mechanisms that make consumers self-select without requiring the firm to directly observe type, effectively converting a third-degree problem into a second-degree (self-selection) design. Coupons and rebates exploit the fact that price-sensitive consumers are more willing to spend time clipping coupons or mailing in rebate forms than time-constrained, less price-sensitive consumers: the "hassle cost" of redemption is itself the sorting mechanism. Damaged goods (IBM's famous LaserPrinter E, deliberately slowed via firmware relative to the otherwise identical LaserPrinter) let a firm sell an artificially degraded low-end version specifically to prevent high-value customers from choosing the cheap option, implementing the "distortion at the bottom" result of Section 17.2 in physical (or digital) form. Geographic and channel-based pricing (different list prices by country or by retail channel) implements third-degree discrimination directly, provided arbitrage across borders or channels is costly enough (tariffs, shipping cost, warranty voiding) to satisfy the no-resale condition.

Revenue (Yield) Management

Airlines, hotels, and rental car companies pioneered revenue management (or yield management): a dynamic, algorithm-driven extension of third-degree price discrimination in which the firm segments a perishable, fixed-capacity inventory (a seat that flies empty tonight earns nothing tomorrow) across booking classes distinguished largely by purchase timing and restrictions (advance-purchase requirements, non-refundability, Saturday-night-stay rules) rather than by directly observed customer identity. The logic mirrors the inverse elasticity rule precisely: business travelers, who book close to departure and have inelastic demand (a meeting cannot simply be postponed), are systematically routed into higher fare classes, while leisure travelers, who can plan ahead and substitute across dates or destinations, self-select into cheaper advance-purchase fares by accepting the associated restrictions. Modern systems solve a real-time capacity-allocation problem (how many seats to protect for each fare class given a forecast demand distribution) that is a direct computational descendant of the static third-degree discrimination model developed in this chapter, extended to allow for uncertainty and a hard capacity constraint (previewed further in the peak-load pricing model of Chapter 18.3).

Legal Boundaries: The Robinson-Patman Act. In the United States, the Robinson-Patman Act (1936) restricts price discrimination in sales of physical goods to different business purchasers (e.g., different retailers) of the same commodity, in the same grade and quality, when the effect may be to substantially lessen competition, subject to defenses including cost justification (the price difference merely reflects a genuine cost difference in serving different buyers) and meeting-competition (matching a rival's price in good faith). Discrimination among final consumers (the primary focus of this chapter) is generally not covered by Robinson-Patman and is instead regulated, if at all, under broader unfair-competition or consumer-protection law. A manager designing a segmented-pricing program involving business resellers, rather than end consumers, should treat this legal boundary as a binding operational constraint, not merely an economic optimization problem.
Price Discrimination Across Segments
Swipe to see the full diagram
|ε| = 3.0, P = 30.0
Figure 17.1: With MC = $20, the optimal segment price is \( P = MC / (1 - 1/|\epsilon|) \). Segment A (business, less elastic, fixed at \( |\epsilon|\approx 1.8 \)) is charged more than Segment B; drag the slider to see Segment B's optimal price fall toward marginal cost as its demand becomes more elastic (more substitutes available).

Managerial Takeaways

Before designing any segmented-pricing program, a manager should explicitly verify all three necessary conditions from this chapter, in order: is there genuine market power to exploit (without it, discrimination is simply infeasible, regardless of how cleverly customers are segmented); can customers actually be sorted, directly or via a well-designed self-selection mechanism, into groups with materially different willingness to pay; and can resale or arbitrage between the resulting price tiers be prevented at reasonable cost. Many pricing programs fail not because the underlying elasticity logic is wrong but because the third condition is quietly violated: a "loyalty discount" that can be freely resold, or a geographic price gap large enough to make cross-border reselling profitable even after shipping and tariff costs, will simply be arbitraged away no matter how carefully the segment elasticities were estimated. Once all three conditions are confirmed, the inverse elasticity rule gives a precise, checkable pricing target for each segment, and the slider exercise above illustrates the single most important comparative-static fact for revenue management: optimal segment price is highly sensitive to elasticity near \( |\epsilon|\approx 1 \) (where small changes in substitute availability produce large price swings) and comparatively insensitive at high elasticity, where price has already been driven close to marginal cost and further elasticity increases have limited remaining room to compress it. Finally, remember Robinson's welfare ambiguity result: profit-maximizing discrimination is not automatically socially harmful, and in markets where a segment would otherwise be priced out entirely under a single uniform price, discrimination can expand total output and make the segmentation program a net social gain even as it also, unambiguously, raises the firm's own profit, a useful distinction to have on hand in a public or regulatory defense of a segmented-pricing strategy.

Which of the following is NOT one of the three necessary conditions for profitable price discrimination?
Correct. A price-taker in a perfectly competitive market has no market power and therefore cannot price-discriminate at all; market power is required, not the absence of it.
Second-degree and third-degree discrimination are frequently conflated; keep them distinct. Third-degree discrimination charges different prices to different observable groups (the firm sets the price; the customer's group membership is exogenous and typically verifiable, e.g., a student ID). Second-degree discrimination instead offers a menu from which customers of unobserved type choose voluntarily, and the firm's entire design problem is to structure that menu (via incentive-compatibility constraints) so that each type selects the option intended for it. A student discount verified by ID is third-degree; an airline's economy/business fare menu, where no one checks whether you "are" a business traveler and instead relies on your own choice among restricted vs. unrestricted fares, is second-degree. The inverse elasticity rule (Section 17.3) applies directly only to the third-degree case; the second-degree case requires solving the full incentive-compatibility problem, which generally yields prices that deviate from the simple inverse-elasticity formula precisely to preserve self-selection. Confusing the two is not merely a labeling error: applying the inverse-elasticity formula directly to a self-selection menu, without checking incentive compatibility, will typically produce a menu that high-value customers can profitably game by choosing the tier meant for lower-value customers, silently destroying the intended segmentation and the revenue gain it was designed to capture. Always check incentive compatibility explicitly, tier by tier, before finalizing any self-selecting price menu.

Practice Problems: Chapter 17

1. A firm serves two markets: \( p_1=120-2q_1 \), \( p_2=80-q_2 \), constant MC=\$20. Find the profit-maximizing price and quantity in each market under third-degree discrimination.

2. Explain why perfect (first-degree) price discrimination produces the efficient quantity even though the firm has full market power.

3. A software company sells "Basic" and "Pro" tiers. Explain, using the second-degree discrimination framework, why the Basic tier is often deliberately crippled in features rather than priced at its true (low) marginal cost of provision.

4. Using the inverse elasticity rule, explain why students typically receive discounts on movie tickets and software while professionals do not, even though the marginal cost of serving each customer is identical.

5. Under what condition does third-degree price discrimination raise total welfare relative to uniform pricing, and under what condition does it lower welfare?

Chapter 18: Advanced Pricing: Bundling, Two-Part Tariffs, Peak-Load, Transfer Pricing

Before the Formulas: More Ways to Price Than Just "One Price Per Item"

Not every price is a simple dollar amount per item. A gym membership charges a monthly fee plus (sometimes) extra for classes. A cable package bundles channels together instead of selling each one separately. An electric utility charges more during hot summer afternoons than at 3am. A big company might "sell" parts from one of its own factories to another of its own factories, and even that internal transaction needs a price. These are all examples of pricing structures more creative than a flat per-unit price, and each one exists because it lets a business capture more value (or use its resources more efficiently) than a single uniform price could. This chapter works through four of the most common and economically important ones.

Pricing Structures Beyond a Single Per-Unit Price

Chapter 17 showed how a firm with market power extracts more surplus by charging different customers different per-unit prices. This chapter develops four further pricing structures used routinely by managers with market power: bundling (selling goods together), two-part tariffs (a fixed fee plus a per-unit price), peak-load pricing (time-varying prices to manage capacity), and transfer pricing (pricing transactions between divisions of the same firm). Each is a distinct mechanism for capturing surplus or allocating resources efficiently, and each has a precise optimal design rule. Despite their surface variety, all four are best understood as answers to the same underlying question raised at the end of Chapter 17: once a firm has identified a way to sort demand into distinguishable groups, time periods, quantity tiers, or internal divisions, what is the profit-maximizing (and, in the transfer-pricing case, the firm-value-maximizing) structure to impose on the resulting set of prices?

18.1 Bundling: Reducing the Dispersion of Willingness to Pay

Pure bundling sells two or more goods only as a package; mixed bundling offers the bundle alongside individually priced components; component pricing sells each good separately. Bundling is most profitable when individual customers' valuations for the component goods are negatively correlated: a customer who values good A highly tends to value good B less, and vice versa. Because the sum of two negatively correlated random valuations has lower variance than the sum of their individual variances, the distribution of bundle valuations across customers is more homogeneous than the distributions of either component alone, letting the seller set a single bundle price that extracts a larger share of total surplus than two separately optimized component prices could, a logic long recognized in cases ranging from mixed consumer bundles to the block-booking of films.

Worked Example: Bundling Two Enterprise Software Modules

Two customers value modules Analytics (A) and Reporting (B) as follows. Customer 1: A=\$120, B=\$40; Customer 2: A=\$40, B=\$120. Marginal cost is \$0 for both (pure software).

Separate pricing: does optimal \( p_A=\$40 \) (sell to both, revenue \$80) beat \$120 (sell to one, revenue \$120)? Compare: at \$40, revenue \( = 2\times 40=\$80 \) per module \( \times \) 2 modules = \$160 total. At \$120, revenue \( =\$120 \) per module \( \times \) 2 = \$240. Here selling only to the high-valuation customer at \$120 for each module actually dominates, giving \$240.

Pure bundling: both customers value the bundle at \$160. Set bundle price = \$160, sell to both: revenue = 2 × \$160 = \$320, strictly higher than the best separate-pricing outcome. Bundling homogenizes willingness to pay (both customers value the bundle identically at \$160) and lets the seller capture the full amount from both, rather than being forced to choose between a low price that leaves surplus on the table or a high price that excludes a customer entirely.

18.2 Two-Part Tariffs: Fixed Fee Plus Marginal Price

A two-part tariff charges total payment \( T(q) = A + pq \): a fixed fee \( A \) (membership, subscription, access charge) plus a per-unit price \( p \). The optimal design, when all consumers are identical, is straightforward and powerful:

\[ p^{*} = MC \qquad \text{and} \qquad A^{*} = CS(MC) \]

Set the per-unit price at marginal cost; this maximizes the total surplus available (the efficient quantity is purchased, since the consumer's marginal decision is undistorted), and then set the fixed fee equal to the consumer surplus generated at that price, capturing the entire surplus as fixed-fee revenue. This achieves the same first-best outcome as perfect price discrimination (efficient quantity, full surplus extraction) through a far simpler pricing instrument, which is why warehouse clubs (membership fee + near-cost unit prices), gym memberships, and razor-and-blade-style business models (heavily discounted device, above-cost consumables) all approximate this structure.

With heterogeneous consumers whose type cannot be observed, the problem becomes a menu-design problem exactly analogous to second-degree discrimination: if the firm sets a single tariff for everyone, it faces a tradeoff between a high fixed fee (which excludes low-value customers entirely if it exceeds their surplus) and a low fixed fee (which leaves money on the table from high-value customers). The profit-maximizing single tariff typically sets \( A \) equal to the low-type's consumer surplus at the chosen \( p \) (to keep them in the market) and raises \( p \) somewhat above marginal cost to extract additional revenue from the high-value segment's larger volume, a "second-best" distortion directly comparable to the "distortion at the bottom" logic of Chapter 17's menu pricing.

Key Result: Optimal Two-Part Tariff (homogeneous consumers). Set the per-unit price at (or very near) marginal cost to maximize the surplus pie, then set the fixed fee to capture that surplus. Any per-unit markup above MC shrinks total surplus (fewer units bought) without generating extra fixed-fee capacity to compensate, so a pure markup strategy is dominated by the MC-plus-fixed-fee structure whenever type is not an issue.

18.3 Peak-Load Pricing

Many goods (electricity, cloud computing capacity, hotel rooms, toll roads) face demand that fluctuates predictably across time (peak vs. off-peak) while capacity, once installed, is largely fixed in the short run and costly to expand. Peak-load pricing charges a higher price during high-demand (peak) periods and a lower price during low-demand (off-peak) periods, and the efficiency logic is precise: let \( b \) be the operating (running) marginal cost per unit and \( k \) be the capacity cost per unit of capacity per period. In the simplest two-period model (peak demand strictly exceeds off-peak demand at any common price), the efficient prices are:

\[ P_{off\text{-}peak} = b \qquad \qquad P_{peak} = b + k \]

Off-peak users pay only the marginal running cost, since existing capacity is not scarce off-peak (there is no opportunity cost, in plain terms the value of the next-best alternative use you give up, to serving them at the margin). Peak users pay the running cost plus the full per-unit capacity cost, because peak demand is precisely what determines how much capacity must be built in the first place. Peak users, collectively, are responsible for the capacity cost and efficiently bear it. This is sometimes summarized as "the peak period pays for the plant." If both periods have positive demand even at the price \( b+k \) (a "shifting-peak" case, where enough off-peak users would shift to peak that the identity of the peak period is itself price-dependent), the efficient solution requires solving for both prices and periods' quantities simultaneously so that capacity is exactly the binding constraint in whichever period(s) turn out to have the higher price.

Worked Example: Peak-Load Pricing for a Data Center

Running (operating) marginal cost per server-hour: \( b=\$0.02 \). Capacity cost per server-hour of installed capacity: \( k=\$0.05 \). Daytime (peak) demand exceeds nighttime (off-peak) demand at any common price.

Efficient prices: off-peak \( P=\$0.02 \) (running cost only; idle capacity has zero opportunity cost off-peak). Peak \( P = 0.02+0.05=\$0.07 \) (running cost plus the full capacity charge, since peak usage is what forces the firm to build more servers).

Managerial interpretation: if the firm instead charged a single flat rate averaging \$0.045 around the clock, off-peak users would be overcharged relative to the true (near-zero incremental) cost of serving them, discouraging efficient off-peak usage that would otherwise fill idle capacity, while peak users would be undercharged relative to the capacity cost they impose, encouraging excessive peak demand and forcing costly over-investment in capacity. Differentiated peak/off-peak pricing improves both efficiency (usage matches true marginal cost) and capacity planning (built capacity matches willingness to pay for it).

18.4 Transfer Pricing: Pricing Internal Transactions

When one division of a firm (the "upstream" division, producing an intermediate good) sells to another division (the "downstream" division, which uses it to produce a final good), the firm must set an internal transfer price even though no external market transaction occurs. Getting this price wrong distorts both divisions' incentives and can reduce firm-wide profit even though each division, individually, appears to be maximizing its own reported profit.

No external market for the intermediate good. The efficient benchmark is to set the transfer price equal to the upstream division's marginal cost of production. This is the internal analogue of the P = MC efficiency condition: the downstream division, purchasing at the upstream division's marginal cost, faces the true marginal cost of the input to the whole firm, and will make the firm-wide-optimal make/buy/output decision. Any transfer price above marginal cost causes the downstream division to under-use the input relative to what maximizes total firm profit: the same distortion a monopoly markup causes in an external market, just relocated inside the firm's own boundaries.

A competitive external market exists for the intermediate good. If the upstream division could sell the input externally (or the downstream division could buy it externally) at a competitive market price \( P_{mkt} \), the efficient transfer price is that market price, not internal marginal cost. Setting transfer price below \( P_{mkt} \) would encourage the downstream division to over-consume an input that has a higher-valued external use, destroying firm value; setting it above \( P_{mkt} \) would push the downstream division to buy externally even when internal supply is cheaper for the firm as a whole (assuming internal MC < \( P_{mkt} \)) . Using the external market price as the transfer price ensures each division's local optimization is also globally optimal for the firm: the opportunity-cost logic that unifies both cases:

\[ \text{Transfer Price} = MC_{upstream} + \text{(opportunity cost of the unit to the firm)} \]

With no external market and unconstrained upstream capacity, opportunity cost is zero and the rule collapses to transfer price = marginal cost. With a competitive external market, the opportunity cost of using a unit internally is exactly the market price forgone, and the rule collapses to transfer price = market price. When the upstream division has limited capacity and no external market, the opportunity cost is the upstream division's shadow price of capacity (its own internal scarcity rent), so the correct transfer price is marginal cost plus that scarcity rent: effectively the upstream division's short-run marginal cost inclusive of the capacity constraint.

Key Result: Transfer Pricing Rule. No external market, no capacity constraint: transfer at marginal cost. Competitive external market exists: transfer at the market price. Capacity-constrained with no external market: transfer at marginal cost plus the internal shadow price (opportunity cost) of the constrained capacity. In every case, the rule is the same underlying principle: price the internal transaction at its true opportunity cost to the firm as a whole, so that each division's decentralized profit-maximizing decision also maximizes consolidated firm profit.

Mixed Bundling and Tiered Software Pricing

Mixed bundling, which offers the bundle alongside individually priced components, weakly dominates both pure bundling and pure component pricing because it nests both as special cases: setting the discount for buying the bundle versus components separately to zero recovers component pricing, while setting individual component prices prohibitively high recovers pure bundling. This is why mixed bundling, not pure bundling, is the overwhelmingly dominant real-world strategy: enterprise software suites are typically sold both as an integrated bundle (at a discount) and as standalone modules (at a premium per module); cable and streaming services sell both full packages and, increasingly, individual channel or content add-ons; fast-food "value meals" are offered alongside items priced individually. The mixed-bundling discount is calibrated precisely to steer the negatively-correlated-valuation customers (who gain the most from bundling, per Section 18.1) toward the bundle, while still allowing customers with an unusually strong preference for only one component to buy it alone at a price that still exceeds marginal cost.

Transfer Pricing and International Tax. Beyond the internal-efficiency question developed in Section 18.4, transfer pricing has a second, entirely distinct dimension: when the upstream and downstream divisions sit in different tax jurisdictions, the transfer price also determines how much reported profit falls in each jurisdiction, and therefore how much tax liability does too. Tax authorities (and the OECD's transfer-pricing guidelines) require multinational firms to set transfer prices for tax purposes using an arm's-length standard: the price that unrelated parties would have negotiated in a comparable transaction. This tax-driven arm's-length rule can, in principle, coincide with the efficiency-driven market-price rule developed above when a genuine external comparable market exists, but the two objectives (efficient internal resource allocation vs. tax-compliant profit allocation across jurisdictions) are conceptually separate, and firms with genuinely unique intermediate goods lacking any external comparable face substantial complexity in satisfying both simultaneously.
Optimal Two-Part Tariff Design
Swipe to see the full diagram
Figure 18.1: With demand p = 80 − 1.6q and MC = $20, setting the per-unit price at marginal cost maximizes the surplus pie (efficient quantity q* = 37.5); the fixed fee then captures the entire consumer surplus triangle as firm profit.

Managerial Takeaways

All four tools in this chapter share a single underlying design principle worth stating explicitly: separate the pricing instrument that determines how much gets produced or consumed (which should generally be set at, or very close to, true marginal cost, to avoid distorting the efficient quantity decision) from the pricing instrument that determines how much surplus the firm captures (the fixed fee, the bundle price, the capacity charge, the internal transfer premium). Two-part tariffs make this separation explicit by construction; bundling achieves a similar effect indirectly, by homogenizing the population of buyers so a single extraction-oriented price can be set without distorting any individual purchase decision as severely as an equivalent per-unit markup would; peak-load pricing applies exactly the same logic across time rather than across customers, letting the off-peak price track true marginal running cost while the peak price carries the full capacity-recovery burden; and transfer pricing applies it across the firm's own internal boundaries, ensuring that a division's local optimization based on the internal price also happens to be the firm-wide optimal decision. A manager evaluating any new pricing structure should ask, concretely: which part of this price is doing the "efficiency" work of guiding the quantity decision to its optimal level, and which part is doing the "extraction" work of capturing surplus, and whether the extraction mechanism is actually calibrated to what different customers, periods, or divisions are willing to bear, or is it simply a uniform markup dressed up in a more complex structure?

In the absence of any external market, what is the efficient transfer price for an intermediate good sold between two divisions of the same firm?
Correct. With no external market and no capacity constraint, transferring at marginal cost ensures the downstream division sees the true firm-wide cost of the input and makes the globally optimal output decision.

Practice Problems: Chapter 18

1. Two customers value Product A and Product B at (100, 30) and (30, 100) respectively, MC = 0 for both. Compare revenue from optimal separate pricing versus optimal pure bundling.

2. A club has identical members with demand \( q = 40 - 2p \) for visits. MC per visit = \$4. Find the optimal per-visit price and the optimal membership fee.

3. A toll road has off-peak running cost \$1 and capacity cost \$3 per vehicle-hour of installed capacity. Find the efficient peak and off-peak tolls.

4. An upstream division can sell its output externally at a competitive price of \$45 per unit, and its internal marginal cost is \$30. What transfer price should be used for internal sales to the downstream division, and why would setting it at \$30 instead distort the firm's decisions?

5. Explain, in your own words, why "no distortion at the top, distortion at the bottom" describes both second-degree price discrimination menus (Chapter 17) and heterogeneous-consumer two-part tariffs.

Module IV: Pricing, Risk & Strategic Investment

Chapter 19: Practical Pricing Strategies: Cost-Plus, Value-Based, Psychological Pricing

Three Ways Managers Actually Set Prices

In plain terms, pricing is simply the question “what number do we put on the price tag, and why that number and not another?” Every dollar higher risks losing a sale to a substitute; every dollar lower gives away profit on customers who would have happily paid more. A manager who has never taken an economics course usually reaches for one of two shortcuts: add a markup to what it cost me to make, or roughly match what competitors charge. This chapter takes those instincts seriously and shows precisely when they work, when they quietly cost the firm real money, and what a more disciplined alternative looks like. Two terms recur constantly below: marginal cost is simply the extra cost of producing one more unit, not the average cost across everything made so far, and elasticity is a plain measure of how sensitive customers' buying behavior is to a price change (highly “elastic” demand means a small price increase causes a big drop in sales; “inelastic” demand means customers barely react at all).

Every prior pricing chapter in this course (Ch. 13, 17, 18) derived the profit-maximizing price from marginal revenue and marginal cost. That analysis is correct, but managers rarely announce a price by writing out an inverse demand curve. In practice, price-setting in most firms follows one of three routines: cost-plus pricing (mark up a cost base by a target percentage), value-based pricing (set price according to what the price-setting rule actually implies about customer willingness-to-pay and elasticity), or psychological pricing (exploit systematic, well-documented departures from the frictionless rational-agent model of price perception). This chapter shows that value-based pricing is simply the MR = MC rule made operational, that cost-plus pricing is a special case that is correct only under a narrow and usually false condition, and that psychological pricing effects are real, measurable, and exploitable within the same profit-maximizing framework, not a separate, ad hoc theory.

Cost-Plus (Markup) Pricing. The firm computes a cost base, typically average variable cost, full average cost, or standard cost at some assumed volume, and adds a fixed percentage markup \( m \):
\[ P = AC \times (1 + m) \]
The markup \( m \) is usually set by industry convention, historical practice, or a target return on investment, not by any explicit reference to customer demand.

19.1 Why Cost-Plus Pricing Is Popular, and Why It Is Usually Wrong

Cost-plus pricing survives in practice for good operational reasons: it is simple to compute, easy to defend to a board or a regulator, gives salespeople an unambiguous floor, and feels “fair” because it visibly ties price to the firm’s own effort. It also has three serious analytical defects.

First, it ignores demand entirely. The markup formula contains no term for elasticity, willingness-to-pay, or competitive intensity. A firm using cost-plus pricing will set the same percentage markup on a highly inelastic, differentiated product as on a commodity item facing brutal competition, systematically underpricing the former and overpricing the latter.

Second, allocated fixed cost creates circular reasoning. When the cost base includes fixed costs allocated across an assumed sales volume, the price depends on the volume assumed, and the volume actually achieved depends on the price. If demand comes in below the assumed volume, average fixed cost per unit rises, which (under a fixed markup rule) raises price further, which reduces demand further: the cost-plus death spiral, a documented failure mode in declining-volume industries (airlines and railroads in the 1970s are the textbook cases).

Third, it can be correct only by coincidence. As shown below, the profit-maximizing markup over marginal cost is determined by the price elasticity of demand at the optimum. A fixed cost-plus markup will equal the profit-maximizing markup only for the one elasticity value that happens to justify it, and that elasticity is not something a cost-plus rule ever checks.

The Optimal Markup Rule (Value-Based Pricing, Formalized). A firm with market power facing demand elasticity \( E \) (with \( E < -1 \) at the optimum) maximizes profit where \( MR = MC \). Since \( MR = P\left(1 + \dfrac{1}{E}\right) \), setting \( MR = MC \) and solving for price gives:
\[ P^{*} = \frac{MC}{1 + \dfrac{1}{E}} \]
Equivalently, the optimal proportional markup over marginal cost is \( \dfrac{P^{*}-MC}{P^{*}} = -\dfrac{1}{E} \) (the Lerner Index). The correct markup is inversely related to the elasticity of demand: price-insensitive customers justify a large markup; price-sensitive customers justify a thin one. This is what “value-based pricing” means in rigorous terms: not a vague appeal to customer perception, but markup set by elasticity, segment by segment.
Precision check. Cost-plus pricing and value-based (optimal-markup) pricing are not different theories of the firm; they are different rules for setting the same variable, price. Cost-plus asks “what multiple of cost should I charge?” and answers with a constant \( m \) unrelated to demand. Optimal markup asks the same question and answers with \( m = -1/E \), which varies by product, segment, channel, and time. A cost-plus rule is optimal only in the knife-edge case where the firm’s conventional markup happens to equal \( -1/E \) for every product it sells, which is essentially never true across a product line with heterogeneous demand.

Worked Example: Cost-Plus vs. Value-Based Pricing

A manufacturer faces linear demand \( P = 100 - Q \) and constant marginal cost \( MC = \$20 \). Standard industry practice is a 50% markup over cost.

Cost-plus price: \( P_{CP} = 20 \times 1.5 = \$30 \). At \( P = 30 \), \( Q = 70 \). Profit \( = (30-20)\times 70 = \$700 \).

Value-based (profit-maximizing) price: \( MR = 100 - 2Q \). Setting \( MR = MC \): \( 100 - 2Q = 20 \Rightarrow Q^{*} = 40 \), \( P^{*} = \$60 \). Profit \( = (60-20)\times 40 = \$1{,}600 \).

Verification via the Lerner Index: at \( Q=40, P=60 \), elasticity is \( E = -\dfrac{dQ}{dP}\cdot\dfrac{P}{Q} = -(-1)\cdot\dfrac{60}{40} = 1.5\), so \( |E| = 1.5 \) and the optimal markup is \( -1/E = 0.667 \), i.e., \( (P-MC)/P = (60-20)/60 = 0.667 \). Checks out exactly.

The firm using the industry-standard 50% cost-plus markup leaves \( \$900 \) of profit on the table, more than doubling profit by pricing to elasticity instead of to cost. This is the single most common and most costly pricing error documented in managerial pricing audits.

Cost-Plus vs. Value-Based Pricing
Swipe to see the full diagram
Figure 19.1: Linear demand \( P=100-Q \), constant \( MC=\$20 \). Cost-plus pricing at a 50% markup yields \( P=\$30 \); the profit-maximizing (value-based) price where \( MR=MC \) is \( P^{*}=\$60 \), more than double the cost-plus profit.

19.2 Value-Based Pricing in Practice: Economic Value to the Customer

Formalizing the optimal markup rule requires knowing the elasticity of demand at the candidate price, which in turn requires understanding what the customer is actually willing to pay. Practitioners operationalize this through the Economic Value to the Customer (EVC) framework:

\[ EVC = P_{ref} + \Delta V \]

where \( P_{ref} \) is the price of the customer’s next-best alternative (the reference price) and \( \Delta V \) is the net value of the differentiation your product provides relative to that alternative: positive for benefits the customer values (durability, reduced downtime, better performance) and negative for costs the customer bears that the alternative does not (switching costs, integration effort). \( EVC \) is the ceiling: a fully informed, rational customer would be indifferent between paying \( EVC \) for your product and paying \( P_{ref} \) for the alternative. A firm that prices below \( EVC \) is leaving customer surplus, and a share of the value it created, unclaimed; the pricing question is how large a share of \( (EVC - P_{ref}) \) the firm can capture given its bargaining position and the customer’s outside options.

Estimating \( \Delta V \) rigorously requires the same demand-estimation toolkit from Chapter 6: conjoint analysis, hedonic regression on comparable products, or controlled price experiments. The EVC framework is therefore not an alternative to the elasticity-based optimal markup rule; it is a method for estimating the elasticity and willingness-to-pay parameters that rule requires, segment by segment.

A Segmented Pricing Checklist

Combining cost-plus, value-based, and psychological elements into a single defensible pricing process:

  1. Compute marginal cost by segment/channel: this is the floor, never the price itself.
  2. Estimate willingness-to-pay and elasticity by segment using demand estimation methods (Ch. 6–7).
  3. Apply the optimal markup rule \( (P-MC)/P = -1/E \) segment by segment, subject to arbitrage/resale constraints (Ch. 17’s price discrimination conditions).
  4. Adjust the resulting numeric price for psychological thresholds (charm pricing, round-number anchors) without moving far from the elasticity-justified level.
  5. Audit realized volumes against the assumed elasticity and re-estimate: treat the markup as a hypothesis, not a policy.

19.3 Psychological Pricing

A large empirical literature documents that consumers do not process prices as continuous real numbers the way the rational-agent model assumes. Three effects are best established and most exploitable by managers:

Charm pricing (the “9-ending” or “left-digit” effect). Prices ending in 9 (e.g., $59.99 rather than $60.00 or even $58.00) systematically outsell round-number prices that are numerically close or even lower, because consumers encode price magnitude primarily from the leftmost digit and truncate or underweight the digits after it. A controlled field experiment with a mail-order catalog found that changing a price from $34 to $39 increased demand by roughly 20–30% for the item tested, a result the standard downward-sloping demand curve cannot explain locally, since it implies a non-monotonic segment in perceived, though not actual, price-quantity space near round numbers.

Reference-price and anchoring effects. Consumers judge a posted price relative to an internal or externally supplied reference price (a “was $199, now $149” framing, a manufacturer’s suggested retail price, or a competitor’s shelf tag) rather than in isolation. A price that is objectively high can be perceived as a bargain if the reference price is set high enough, and vice versa; this is the economic logic behind list prices that are rarely paid and behind the widespread use of anchoring “decoy” options in menu pricing (Ch. 18’s bundling logic extends naturally here).

Price-quality inference. In markets with search costs or unobservable quality (directly connected to the information-economics material in Chapter 21), consumers sometimes use price itself as a signal of quality, particularly for experience goods (wine, consulting services, cosmetics). This creates a subtle trap for value-based pricing: cutting price to match a competitor can reduce perceived quality and demand simultaneously, partially or fully offsetting the intended volume gain: the demand curve is not the fixed object of the standard model but shifts with the price itself.

Where psychological pricing backfires. Charm pricing and reference-price framing are best documented in consumer retail, catalog, and e-commerce contexts with relatively low per-unit stakes and limited buyer sophistication. In B2B procurement, government contracting, and any setting with professional buyers, competitive bidding, or formal request-for-proposal processes, these effects are far weaker or absent. Sophisticated buyers with time and incentive to compute total cost of ownership are not reliably fooled by a $9,999 versus $10,000 framing, and a firm that relies on such tactics in a professional-buyer market risks appearing unserious. Match the tactic to the buyer’s decision process, not the product category alone.
For instructors: emphasize that psychological pricing effects are demand-side phenomena that shift or reshape the demand curve itself; they are fully compatible with, not a contradiction of, the MR = MC optimization rule. The correct way to teach this: charm pricing means the true demand curve has small discontinuous jumps in willingness-to-pay just above round numbers, and the optimal-markup rule still applies to that (more complicated, empirically estimated) demand curve.

19.4 Segmented and Dynamic Value-Based Pricing

The optimal-markup rule generalizes cleanly once a firm recognizes it is not selling to a single homogeneous demand curve but to a collection of segments with different elasticities. This is the analytical bridge between this chapter and the third-degree price discrimination result of Chapter 17: if a firm can identify segments \( j=1,\ldots,J \) with elasticities \( E_j \) and can prevent resale across segments, the optimal price in each segment independently satisfies \( P_j^{*} = MC/(1+1/E_j) \). A SaaS company charging students a discounted rate, a free tier funded by a premium tier, and a full enterprise price for the same underlying software is applying exactly this logic: the free/freemium tier targets the most elastic segment (price near zero, since \( E \) is very large in magnitude there), while the enterprise tier targets the least elastic segment (large markup, since enterprise buyers' willingness to pay is driven by mission-critical need rather than by close substitutes). The pricing tactic looks different from a textbook Lerner Index calculation, but the underlying economics is identical.

Dynamic (or "surge") pricing extends the same logic across time rather than across customer segments: demand elasticity for a ride-share trip or a hotel room is lower during a highly time-constrained, low-substitutability moment (a concert just ending, the last room in town during a convention) than during an ordinary Tuesday afternoon with many substitutes available. A time-varying price that raises price when \( |E| \) is temporarily low and lowers it when \( |E| \) is temporarily high is, again, just the optimal markup rule applied period by period rather than a distinct pricing philosophy, though the psychological-pricing literature also documents that consumers react to the visible framing of a price increase (a posted "surge" multiplier feels more adversarial than an equivalent price arrived at through opaque yield-management software), which is a genuine behavioral cost a purely elasticity-based calculation omits and that value-based pricing in practice must weigh against the static profit gain.

Why Managers Still Use Cost-Plus Despite Its Flaws

It is worth taking the defense of cost-plus pricing seriously rather than dismissing it outright, because its persistence in practice is not pure irrationality. First, estimating demand elasticity reliably (Ch. 6) is genuinely costly and uncertain, while a cost base is directly observable from the firm's own accounting records. Cost-plus substitutes a cheap, noisy-but-verifiable number for an expensive, more accurate one, and for low-stakes products the expected loss from mispricing may not justify the cost of estimating demand precisely. Second, in industries with tacit collusion or informal price leadership (Ch. 15), a common cost-plus convention can serve as a coordinating "focal point" that stabilizes prices across competitors without explicit communication, reducing the risk of a price war, a strategic benefit unrelated to, and potentially in tension with, static single-firm profit maximization. Third, cost-plus pricing is more defensible to regulators and to the public in contexts where "excessive" pricing draws political or legal scrutiny (utility rate cases, wartime price controls, government contracting under cost-reimbursement rules) precisely because it is transparent and auditable in a way that an elasticity-based price is not. None of this makes cost-plus pricing optimal in the profit-maximization sense this chapter has derived; it makes it a rational second-best choice when the cost of computing the true optimum, or the strategic and political costs of visibly pursuing it, are high enough to outweigh the foregone profit documented in the worked example above.

Practice Problems: Chapter 19

1. A firm faces demand \( P = 200 - 4Q \) and constant \( MC = \$40 \). Compute the profit-maximizing price and the implied optimal markup. If the firm instead uses a fixed 30% cost-plus markup, compute the resulting price, quantity, and profit, and compare to the optimum.

2. Explain, using the Lerner Index, why a single company-wide cost-plus markup policy is more damaging to profit the more heterogeneous the firm’s product-line elasticities are.

3. A component supplier’s product reduces a customer’s downtime cost by $12,000/year relative to the next-best alternative priced at $50,000/year. What is the EVC? If the supplier wants to capture 40% of the value created, what price should it charge?

4. Using the cost-plus “death spiral” logic, explain why cost-plus pricing is especially dangerous for a firm with high fixed costs and declining volume, and propose an alternative pricing rule.

5. A retailer is deciding between pricing an item at $49.99 and $50.00. Using the concept of left-digit bias, explain the mechanism by which the one-cent difference can produce a discontinuous change in quantity demanded, and explain why this is not evidence against downward-sloping demand in general.

A firm sets price using a fixed 40% cost-plus markup on every product it sells, regardless of the product's demand elasticity. What is the most precise diagnosis of this policy?
Correct. The profit-maximizing markup over marginal cost is \( -1/E \), which varies by product and segment. A single fixed markup coincides with the optimum only where the firm's chosen markup happens to match \( -1/E \) for that specific product's elasticity: true by luck, not by design, and essentially never true across a whole product line.

Chapter 20: Decision-Making Under Risk & Uncertainty

Beyond the Certain World

Before the formulas, think about how you personally feel about a bet. Most people would rather have $50,000 guaranteed than a coin flip between $0 and $100,000, even though the average payout of the coin flip is also $50,000. That gut preference for the safe amount over an equally-valuable gamble is what economists call risk aversion, and it is not irrational: losing a dollar typically hurts more than gaining a dollar helps, especially once a person or firm cannot easily absorb a large loss. This chapter turns that everyday intuition into a precise tool for evaluating real managerial bets: whether to launch a risky product, enter a new market, or extend credit to an uncertain customer.

Every model so far has implicitly assumed the manager knows outcomes with certainty, or at least treats expected values as if they were certain. Real managerial decisions, such as launching a product, entering a new market, investing in capacity, or extending credit, involve outcomes that are not known in advance. A classic distinction separates risk, where the possible outcomes and their probabilities are known (or can be reliably estimated), from uncertainty, where even the probability distribution is unknown (a genuinely novel technology, an unprecedented regulatory regime, a pandemic). This chapter builds the standard tool for decision-making under risk, expected utility theory, and the practical tool for structuring sequential risky decisions: the decision tree.

Expected Value. For a random outcome \( X \) taking values \( x_1, \ldots, x_n \) with probabilities \( p_1, \ldots, p_n \) (\( \sum p_i = 1 \)), the expected value is:
\[ E[X] = \sum_{i=1}^{n} p_i x_i \]
Expected value is a summary of the outcome distribution's location. It says nothing about how a decision-maker feels about the spread of possible outcomes around that location, which is exactly what expected utility theory adds.

20.1 Why Expected Value Is Not Enough

Consider a manager offered a choice between (A) a certain $50,000 and (B) a coin flip paying $0 or $100,000. Both options have expected value $50,000. Most managers, and most people, strictly prefer the certain $50,000. This preference is not irrational; it reflects risk aversion, and expected value alone cannot represent it, because expected value treats the two options as equivalent. We need a criterion that assigns a number to each outcome reflecting the decision-maker’s satisfaction, not just the dollar amount, and then takes the expectation of that number.

Expected Utility. Let \( U(\cdot) \) be a von Neumann–Morgenstern utility function representing the decision-maker's preferences over outcomes. For a gamble with outcomes \( x_1,\ldots,x_n \) and probabilities \( p_1,\ldots,p_n \), expected utility is:
\[ EU = \sum_{i=1}^{n} p_i\, U(x_i) \]
Under the von Neumann–Morgenstern axioms (completeness, transitivity, continuity, and independence of irrelevant alternatives applied to lotteries), a decision-maker's preferences over risky prospects can be represented by ranking gambles according to \( EU \), and \( U \) is unique up to a positive affine transformation \( aU + b,\ a>0 \).
Risk Attitude and Curvature. Whether a decision-maker is risk averse, risk neutral, or risk seeking is determined entirely by the curvature of \( U(W) \) over wealth \( W \):
\[ \begin{aligned} U''(W) < 0 \ (\text{concave}) &\Rightarrow \text{risk averse} \\ U''(W) = 0 \ (\text{linear}) &\Rightarrow \text{risk neutral} \\ U''(W) > 0 \ (\text{convex}) &\Rightarrow \text{risk seeking} \end{aligned} \]
For a risk-averse decision-maker, Jensen's Inequality gives \( E[U(W)] < U(E[W]) \): the expected utility of a risky wealth outcome is strictly less than the utility of receiving its expected value for certain. This is the formal content of "risk aversion": not a vague dislike of uncertainty, but a specific inequality implied by concavity.

20.2 Certainty Equivalent and Risk Premium

Jensen's Inequality tells us the risk-averse decision-maker values the risky gamble at less than its expected value. We can quantify exactly how much less using two related concepts.

Certainty Equivalent (CE). The certainty equivalent of a gamble is the certain wealth level that gives the same utility as the gamble itself:
\[ U(CE) = E[U(W)] \]
Risk Premium (RP). The risk premium is the amount of expected wealth the decision-maker is willing to sacrifice to eliminate the risk entirely:
\[ RP = E[W] - CE \]
For a risk-averse decision-maker (\( U'' < 0 \)), \( CE < E[W] \) and therefore \( RP > 0 \): the decision-maker would strictly prefer a certain payment of \( CE \) (less than the actuarially fair expected value) to facing the gamble, and this is precisely why insurance markets exist: a risk-averse policyholder will pay a premium above the actuarially fair value of a loss to transfer risk to an insurer who can pool many independent risks.
Expected Utility: Risk Aversion
Swipe to see the full diagram
r = 0.50
Figure 20.1: Power (CRRA-style) utility \( U(W)=W^{a} \), \( a=1-r \). As \( r \) rises toward 1, curvature increases: expected utility falls further below \( U(E[W]) \), the certainty equivalent falls further below \( E[W]=\$100 \), and the risk premium grows. At \( r=0 \), \( U \) is linear, \( CE=E[W] \), and \( RP=0 \).

Worked Example: Should the Manager Accept the Gamble?

A manager with utility function \( U(W) = \sqrt{W} \) (thousands of dollars) currently has wealth \( W_0 = \$100{,}000 \) (i.e., \( W_0=100 \) in thousands). A risky project requires committing this entire wealth to an investment that returns \( W=50 \) (thousand) with probability 0.5 or \( W=150 \) (thousand) with probability 0.5.

Step 1: Expected value: \( E[W] = 0.5(50) + 0.5(150) = 100 \).

Step 2: Expected utility: \( EU = 0.5\sqrt{50} + 0.5\sqrt{150} = 0.5(7.071) + 0.5(12.247) = 9.659 \).

Step 3: Certainty equivalent: solve \( \sqrt{CE}=9.659 \Rightarrow CE = 9.659^2 = 93.3 \) (thousand), i.e., \( \$93{,}300 \).

Step 4: Risk premium: \( RP = E[W]-CE = 100 - 93.3 = \$6{,}700 \).

Decision: the manager should take the risky project only if its certainty equivalent, $93,300, exceeds the value of the safest alternative use of the same capital. If a guaranteed alternative pays more than $93,300 for certain, the manager (with this utility function and this wealth level) should reject the gamble even though its expected value, $100,000, is higher than the guaranteed alternative. This is a direct, quantified application of Jensen's Inequality to a real capital-allocation decision.

20.3 Measuring Risk Aversion: The Arrow–Pratt Coefficients

Absolute Risk Aversion. \( A(W) = -\dfrac{U''(W)}{U'(W)} \). Measures aversion to a fixed dollar bet as a function of wealth level. Constant Absolute Risk Aversion (CARA), \( U(W)=-e^{-\alpha W} \), implies willingness to bet a fixed dollar amount does not change with wealth. This is a common simplifying assumption in finance because it makes portfolio choice independent of initial wealth.

Relative Risk Aversion. \( R(W) = -\dfrac{W\,U''(W)}{U'(W)} = W\cdot A(W) \). Measures aversion to a bet sized as a proportion of wealth. Constant Relative Risk Aversion (CRRA), \( U(W)=\dfrac{W^{1-\gamma}}{1-\gamma} \) (the functional form used in the diagram above, with \( \gamma=r \)), implies the decision-maker's willingness to risk a given fraction of wealth is unchanged as wealth grows. This is the standard assumption in most capital-budgeting and portfolio applications, and the empirically better-supported of the two for wealth changes of realistic magnitude.

20.4 Decision Trees for Sequential Decisions

Many managerial decisions unfold over time, with later choices contingent on the resolution of earlier uncertainty: launch a pilot, observe results, then decide whether to scale. A decision tree represents this as alternating decision nodes (squares, where the manager chooses) and chance nodes (circles, where nature resolves uncertainty), solved by backward induction (folding back): evaluate the tree from the terminal branches inward, computing the expected value (or expected utility) at each chance node and taking the maximum expected payoff at each decision node.

Worked Example: Test-Market Before a National Launch?

A firm can launch a new product nationally now, or spend $200,000 on a regional test market first and then decide whether to launch nationally based on the result. Without testing: national launch succeeds (probability 0.5, profit $3,000,000) or fails (probability 0.5, profit \( -\$1{,}000{,}000 \)). \( EMV(\text{launch now}) = 0.5(3{,}000{,}000) + 0.5(-1{,}000{,}000) = \$1{,}000{,}000 \).

With testing: the test costs $200,000 and produces a signal correlated with true demand. Suppose the test predicts success with probability 0.6 overall; conditional on a positive test result (probability 0.6), true success probability rises to 0.75; conditional on a negative result (probability 0.4), true success probability falls to 0.15. If the firm launches only after a positive signal: expected profit given positive signal \( = 0.75(3{,}000{,}000)+0.25(-1{,}000{,}000) = 2{,}250{,}000-250{,}000=2{,}000{,}000 \). If negative, the firm does not launch (payoff $0). Folding back: \( EMV(\text{test first}) = 0.6(2{,}000{,}000) + 0.4(0) - 200{,}000 = 1{,}200{,}000-200{,}000=\$1{,}000{,}000 \) net of the test cost.

Expected Value of Perfect Information (EVPI): if the firm could learn the true outcome with certainty before deciding, it would launch only when success is guaranteed: \( EMV(\text{perfect info}) = 0.5(3{,}000{,}000) + 0.5(0) = \$1{,}500{,}000 \) (skip launching when failure is certain). \( EVPI = 1{,}500{,}000 - 1{,}000{,}000 = \$500{,}000 \): this is the maximum the firm should ever be willing to pay for a perfectly accurate signal, and it caps how much value any imperfect test (like the $200,000 test above, worth exactly its cost in this example) could possibly add.

EMV vs. Expected Utility: when they diverge. Decision trees are usually folded back using Expected Monetary Value (EMV), which implicitly assumes risk neutrality. This is a reasonable approximation for decisions that are small relative to firm or decision-maker wealth (most routine operational choices), but it can give the wrong answer for large, wealth-threatening decisions. A $50 million bet-the-company investment should be folded back using expected utility with a realistic risk-aversion parameter, not raw EMV, precisely because Jensen's Inequality drives a wedge between the two whenever stakes are large relative to wealth.

20.5 Diversification and Portfolio Risk

A related and managerially crucial idea is that not all risk is equally costly to bear, because some risk can be diversified away at no cost while some cannot. Consider a firm holding \( n \) independent risky projects, each with variance \( \sigma^2 \) and uncorrelated with the others. The variance of the average return across all \( n \) projects is \( \sigma^2/n \), which shrinks toward zero as \( n \) grows: the statistical law of large numbers at work. This idiosyncratic (diversifiable) risk is, in principle, costless to eliminate simply by holding a large enough portfolio of independent bets, and a well-diversified investor or firm should not demand any compensation (any risk premium) for bearing it. Systematic (non-diversifiable) risk, the portion of a project's variance that is correlated with the broader economy or market and therefore does not average out no matter how many projects are combined, is the only risk that should command a risk premium in a well-functioning capital market, which is precisely the logic embedded in the Capital Asset Pricing Model's use of \( \beta \) (a pure measure of systematic risk) rather than total variance \( \sigma^2 \) to price risk, taken up formally in the discount-rate discussion of Chapter 23. This has a sharp practical implication for the expected-utility framework of this chapter: a manager evaluating a single risky project in isolation, using their own personal risk aversion and the project's total variance, will generally overstate the true cost of the project's risk to a well-diversified corporate owner, for whom much of that variance is idiosyncratic and irrelevant. The appropriate risk-aversion parameter and the appropriate measure of "risk" for a corporate capital-budgeting decision are not the same as for an individual manager's personal decision, even though both use the same underlying expected-utility mathematics.

A caveat on the theory itself. Expected utility theory is the standard normative benchmark, but extensive experimental evidence (documented further in behavioral-economics treatments) shows real decision-makers systematically violate its axioms: loss aversion, probability weighting that overweights small probabilities, and ambiguity aversion under Knightian uncertainty (the Ellsberg paradox) are all robust departures. The practical implication for managers: use expected utility as the disciplined baseline for structuring a decision and stress-testing intuition, but recognize that your own and others' snap judgments about risky choices will be systematically distorted relative to it, especially under true uncertainty rather than known-probability risk.

20.5 Risk Pooling and the Logic of Insurance Markets

The certainty-equivalent and risk-premium framework explains precisely why insurance markets exist and how they create value even when they are actuarially unfair (i.e., even when the premium exceeds the expected payout). A risk-averse policyholder with wealth exposed to a loss \( L \) with probability \( p \) has a risk premium \( RP > 0 \) that measures the maximum amount above the actuarially fair premium \( pL \) they would rationally pay to eliminate the risk. An insurer that is effectively risk neutral, because it pools many independent (or imperfectly correlated) policies and, by the law of large numbers, faces near-certain aggregate losses close to the expected value even though each individual policy is genuinely risky, can profitably charge a premium anywhere between \( pL \) (its own break-even point) and \( pL+RP \) (the policyholder's own certainty-equivalent ceiling), capturing the gap as profit while making the policyholder strictly better off. This is a genuine, mutually beneficial trade created purely by the difference in risk-bearing capacity between the two parties, not a zero-sum transfer. The same logic underlies why a large, diversified corporation (effectively risk neutral across many projects) is frequently the natural risk-bearer in a supply contract, joint venture, or employment relationship with a smaller, less diversified counterparty, a theme this course returns to directly in Chapter 22's principal-agent analysis, where the risk-neutral principal optimally absorbs as much risk as incentive constraints allow.

Common Functional Forms for U(W)

Applied work needs a specific utility function, not just the qualitative property \( U''<0 \). Three forms recur throughout finance and managerial economics:

Quadratic utility, \( U(W)=W-\frac{b}{2}W^2 \), is analytically convenient (expected utility depends only on the mean and variance of \( W \), which is why it underlies mean-variance portfolio theory) but has the unrealistic property of eventually declining in \( W \) and exhibiting increasing absolute risk aversion, meaning it predicts wealthier decision-makers become more, not less, willing to bear a fixed dollar risk, the opposite of most empirical evidence.

Exponential (CARA) utility, \( U(W)=-e^{-\alpha W} \), has constant absolute risk aversion \( A(W)=\alpha \) for all \( W \): the dollar amount the decision-maker will risk on a fixed-stakes gamble does not change as wealth grows. This is analytically convenient (it is what produces the clean linear-contract result used in Chapter 22) but implies unrealistic behavior for decisions involving a large fraction of total wealth.

Power (CRRA) utility, \( U(W)=\dfrac{W^{1-\gamma}}{1-\gamma} \) (the form used in the Figure 20.1 diagram above, with \( \gamma=r \)), has constant relative risk aversion: the decision-maker's willingness to risk a fixed proportion of wealth is unchanged as wealth scales up. CRRA is the standard assumption in capital-budgeting, portfolio-choice, and lifetime-consumption applications precisely because it is far better supported empirically across a wide range of wealth levels than either quadratic or CARA utility.

Practice Problems: Chapter 20

1. A decision-maker has \( U(W) = \ln(W) \) and faces a gamble paying $80 or $180 with equal probability. Compute \( E[W] \), \( EU \), \( CE \), and \( RP \).

2. Explain, using the Arrow-Pratt measures, the practical difference between assuming CARA and CRRA utility when modeling a manager's willingness to risk a fixed dollar amount as the firm grows.

3. Draw a decision tree for a firm deciding whether to enter a new market immediately, wait one year to observe a competitor's entry decision, or abandon the opportunity. Assign your own plausible probabilities and payoffs, fold back the tree, and state the optimal strategy.

4. Using the test-market example, recompute the EMV of testing first if the test costs $600,000 instead of $200,000. At what test cost does testing first become inferior to launching immediately without testing?

5. Explain why EVPI is an upper bound on the value of any real (imperfect) information-gathering activity, and why a manager should never pay more for a market research study than the EVPI.

A risk-averse manager's certainty equivalent for a risky project is $93,300, and the project's expected value is $100,000. What does this imply?
Correct. The certainty equivalent is defined by \( U(CE) = E[U(W)] \); for a risk-averse (concave) utility function, Jensen's Inequality guarantees \( CE < E[W] \), and the difference is the risk premium the decision-maker would sacrifice to trade the gamble for a sure payment. This does not mean automatic rejection; it means the risky project should be compared to alternatives using $93,300, not $100,000, as its certainty-equivalent value.

Chapter 21: Information Economics: Asymmetric Information, Signaling & Screening

Two Different Problems That Get Conflated

Before the formal definitions, picture buying a used car. The seller has driven it every day and knows exactly what is wrong with it; you, the buyer, get a fifteen-minute test drive. That gap, one side of a deal knowing more than the other, is what economists call asymmetric information, and it shows up everywhere a manager negotiates, hires, lends, or insures: a job candidate knows their own work ethic better than the interviewer does, and a borrower knows their own repayment intentions better than the bank does. This chapter is about the two different ways that information gap causes trouble, and why a manager needs to correctly diagnose which one they are facing before designing a fix.

Asymmetric information (one party to a transaction knows something relevant that the other does not) breaks the efficiency results of Chapter 24's welfare theorems and creates real managerial problems in hiring, lending, insurance, procurement, and product markets. The literature (and unfortunately much applied discussion) frequently blurs together two economically distinct phenomena that happen to share a family resemblance. This chapter draws the line precisely, because the diagnosis determines the remedy: a firm that treats a moral hazard problem with an adverse-selection remedy (or vice versa) will design the wrong contract.

Adverse Selection. A problem of hidden information (hidden type) that exists before a contract is signed. One party knows something relevant about a pre-existing, fixed characteristic (the quality of a used car, the health status of an insurance applicant, the creditworthiness of a borrower) that the other party cannot observe directly. Because the informed party self-selects into the transaction based on this hidden characteristic, the pool of counterparties who choose to transact is systematically skewed. Classic examples: the used-car "lemons" market; health and life insurance, where sicker applicants are more likely to seek coverage.
Moral Hazard. A problem of hidden action that arises after a contract is signed, because one party's subsequent behavior cannot be fully observed or verified by the other, and the contract changes that party's incentives. The term originates in the insurance industry: an insured party, once covered, has weaker incentive to take costly precautions against loss because the insurer bears the financial consequence. Classic example: insurance-induced risk-taking. A driver with full collision coverage drives less carefully than an uninsured driver would, not because their underlying character changed, but because the contract altered their incentive at the margin.
The distinction, made unmissable. Ask two questions about any information problem: (1) When does the private information exist, before or after the contract? Adverse selection: before (a fixed type). Moral hazard: the informational gap opens after (a chosen action). (2) Is it about a characteristic or a behavior? Adverse selection: a characteristic the informed party cannot change (or already possesses) at the moment of contracting. Moral hazard: a behavior the informed party actively chooses once the contract's incentives are in place. A single market can exhibit both simultaneously and they still do not become the same thing: health insurance has adverse selection (sicker people disproportionately buy coverage) and moral hazard (insured people take fewer precautions and consume more care than they would if uninsured) as two separate, independently-operating problems requiring two separate remedies.

21.1 Adverse Selection: The Market for Lemons

The classic "lemons" model is the canonical demonstration that asymmetric information about a fixed characteristic can destroy a market that would function efficiently under full information, not merely make it less efficient, but in the extreme case eliminate trade entirely.

Worked Example: Procurement and the Unraveling Problem

A manufacturer sources a component from many potential suppliers whose quality \( q \) is uniformly distributed on \( [0,1] \) (1 = highest quality), known to each supplier but not to the buyer. A supplier's cost of producing quality \( q \) is \( q \) (in $ thousands per unit); the buyer's value for quality \( q \) is \( 1.5q \) (in $ thousands per unit); buyer value always exceeds seller cost, so under full information every supplier should sell to the buyer, and doing so is efficient for every single quality level.

Under asymmetric information, the buyer cannot observe an individual supplier's \( q \) and can only condition on the price offered. At any price \( p \), only suppliers with cost \( q \le p \) will accept (suppliers with \( q > p \) refuse, since the price does not cover their cost). Given \( q \) uniform on \( [0,p] \) among accepting suppliers, average accepted quality is \( p/2 \). The buyer's willingness to pay for the average quality actually on offer is \( 1.5 \times (p/2) = 0.75p \), which is less than \( p \) for any \( p>0 \).

Unraveling: if the buyer offers \( p \), rational suppliers with \( q>p \) refuse, average quality falls, the buyer's willingness to pay falls below \( p \), forcing a lower offer, which drives out more suppliers, and so on. The only fixed point is \( p^{*}=0 \): the market completely unravels and no trade occurs, even though every single supplier-buyer pair with \( q \le 1 \) has strictly positive gains from trade under full information. This is the central, striking result of the lemons model: a small amount of asymmetric information can destroy an entire market, not just shrink it.

General Lesson. Adverse selection unravels markets whenever (i) quality/type is continuous or has enough variation, (ii) the informed party can walk away from a price that does not cover their private cost/value, and (iii) the uninformed party can only condition price on average, not individual, quality. The severity of unraveling depends on how much the uninformed party's willingness to pay responds to quality relative to how the price itself signals quality to the market; markets with strong outside remedies (below) unravel less.
Formal condition for full unravelling. Generalize the worked example: suppliers' cost equals quality \( q\sim U[0,1] \), and the buyer's value for quality is \( kq \) for some constant \( k \). At any offered price \( p \), only suppliers with \( q\le p \) accept, so average accepted quality is \( p/2 \), and the buyer's willingness to pay for that average quality is \( k(p/2) \). An equilibrium with positive trade at price \( p>0 \) requires the buyer's willingness to pay to at least match the price:
\[ k\cdot\frac{p}{2} \ \ge\ p \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad k \ge 2 \]
If \( k<2 \) (buyer value does not exceed roughly double seller cost, in expectation over the pool), the market unravels completely to \( p^{*}=0 \), exactly as in the worked example where \( k=1.5<2 \). If \( k\ge 2 \), a positive-price, positive-trade equilibrium can be sustained (indeed at \( k=2 \) exactly, trade at any \( p\in[0,1] \) is a knife-edge equilibrium, and for \( k>2 \) the market can clear at \( p=1 \), the full range trading). This single inequality is the formal statement of the lemons-model insight: it is not that asymmetric information always destroys markets, but that it destroys markets whenever the buyer's valuation of average quality is not comfortably above the seller's cost, a condition equally easy or hard to satisfy depending on how large the underlying quality variation and value gap are.

21.2 Remedies for Adverse Selection: Signaling and Screening

Two distinct remedies restore some or all of the lost trade, distinguished by which side of the market takes the action.

Signaling. The informed party takes a costly, observable action to credibly reveal private information to the uninformed party. For a signal to be credible in equilibrium, it must be costly, and critically it must satisfy the single-crossing property: the cost of producing the signal must differ systematically by type, so that mimicking the signal is worthwhile for a high type but not for a low type. The classic application is the education-as-signal model: education need not raise a worker's productivity at all to be economically valuable as a signal, as long as it is cheaper for high-productivity workers to acquire than for low-productivity workers.
Screening. The uninformed party, unable to observe type directly, designs a menu of contracts and lets the informed party self-select, exploiting the fact that different types have different preferences over the menu's terms. The classic application is insurance menu design: an insurer offers a low-premium, high-deductible policy and a high-premium, low-deductible policy; low-risk customers, who rarely expect to claim, prefer the high-deductible option (they are effectively self-insuring the small expected claim in exchange for a lower premium), while high-risk customers pay more for full coverage. The menu must satisfy two constraints for each type \( i \): incentive compatibility (type \( i \) weakly prefers its intended contract to any other contract on the menu) and individual rationality (type \( i \) weakly prefers its contract to not participating at all).
Spence Education-Signaling Model
Swipe to see the full diagram
Figure 21.1: Spence signaling model. Cost of education differs by type (single-crossing property): steeper for low-productivity workers. A separating equilibrium wage schedule \( w(e) \) pays 1 below the threshold \( e^{*} \) and 2 above it. Any \( e^{*}\in[1,2] \) can support a separating equilibrium; the least-cost outcome, \( e^{*}=1 \), is the least wasteful signal that still deters the low type from mimicking.
Why e* = 1 is the least-cost separating signal. For separation to hold, the low type must not want to acquire education level \( e^{*} \) to be paid the high wage: the wage gain \( (2-1)=1 \) must not exceed the low type's cost of acquiring \( e^{*} \) education, \( C_L(e^{*})=e^{*} \), giving \( e^{*}\ge 1 \). The high type must still find signaling worthwhile: wage gain \( 1 \) must weakly exceed the high type's cost \( C_H(e^{*})=e^{*}/2 \), giving \( e^{*}\le 2 \). Any threshold in \( [1,2] \) separates the types, but larger thresholds waste more real resources on a signal that (in the original formulation) does not raise anyone's actual productivity; it is purely costly, purely informative. \( e^{*}=1 \), the smallest sufficient signal, is Pareto-preferred by both types among all separating equilibria and is the one predicted by the standard refinement of this model.

Screening in Practice: Menu Design

ContractPremiumDeductibleChosen by
ALow ($400/yr)High ($2,000)Low-risk drivers
BHigh ($900/yr)Low ($200)High-risk drivers

The insurer never directly asks "are you high risk?"; it asks customers to reveal their type indirectly through which contract they choose, because a low-risk driver's small expected claim makes the high deductible cheap in expectation, while a high-risk driver's larger expected claim makes full coverage worth the extra premium. This is the same logic as second-degree price discrimination (Ch. 18): a self-selecting menu that separates types the seller cannot observe directly, here applied to insurance risk rather than willingness-to-pay for quantity.

21.3 Moral Hazard and Its Remedies

Because moral hazard is about post-contract hidden action rather than pre-contract hidden type, its remedies look different from signaling and screening. The core problem is that a contract offering full risk protection (full insurance, a fixed salary regardless of effort) removes the agent's incentive to take costly unobservable precautions, because the agent no longer bears the marginal cost of a bad outcome.

  • Monitoring: directly observe the hidden action where feasible (telematics devices that track driving behavior for auto insurance, on-site supervision), which is costly, and often only partially effective.
  • Deductibles and co-insurance: require the agent to retain some fraction of the loss, restoring partial incentive to take precautions, at the cost of leaving the agent partially exposed to risk, a direct risk-incentive tradeoff that is the central subject of Chapter 22.
  • Incentive contracts tied to observable, correlated outcomes: when the action itself cannot be observed but a noisy signal of its consequences can be (output, claims history, sales), tie compensation to that signal: the foundation of principal-agent contract design, taken up formally next chapter.
  • Reputation and repeated interaction: when the same relationship repeats, the threat of losing future business (higher future premiums, contract non-renewal) can substitute for direct monitoring.
A subtlety worth flagging. Deductibles serve two distinct economic functions simultaneously in an insurance contract, which is part of why the adverse-selection/moral-hazard distinction gets blurred in casual discussion: they act as a moral-hazard remedy (restoring the policyholder's incentive to take care) and as a screening device (separating low-risk from high-risk applicants, per the table above). The same contractual feature is doing two different jobs against two different information problems, which is exactly why careful diagnosis, not just pattern-matching on "insurance has asymmetric information," is required before designing a fix.

21.4 Formalizing Incentive Compatibility in a Screening Menu

The insurance menu in the table above works because it satisfies two formal constraints for each type \( i\in\{L,H\} \) (low-risk, high-risk), where \( U_i(\text{contract}) \) is type \( i \)'s expected utility from a given contract:

\[ \textbf{Incentive Compatibility (IC):}\quad U_i(\text{contract}_i) \ \ge\ U_i(\text{contract}_j) \ \ \text{for all } j\ne i \]
\[ \textbf{Individual Rationality (IR):}\quad U_i(\text{contract}_i) \ \ge\ U_i(\text{no coverage}) \]

IC guarantees each type genuinely prefers the contract designed for it to any other option on the menu: the self-selection actually works. IR guarantees each type prefers participating at all to walking away.
