A further, important negative result holds too: in a competitive insurance market with free entry, a pooling equilibrium (a single contract for both types) can never survive, because a rival insurer can always profitably "cream-skim" by offering a slightly better contract that only low-risk types find attractive. But a separating equilibrium of the kind shown in the table is not guaranteed to exist either: if the fraction of low-risk types in the population is large enough, a pooling contract that both types would prefer to the separating menu becomes feasible, and no equilibrium in pure strategies exists at all, a genuine instability result, not merely a modeling inconvenience, and one of the reasons real insurance markets sometimes require regulatory intervention (mandated minimum coverage, community rating) to function at all.

Cross-application: screening in employment and lending. The same IC/IR logic explains features of labor contracts (a menu of a low base salary with high commission versus a high base salary with low commission, self-selecting risk-tolerant versus risk-averse sales candidates) and consumer lending (a menu of a higher interest rate with no collateral requirement versus a lower rate with collateral posted, self-selecting borrowers who are privately confident versus uncertain about their own repayment ability). In every case, the uninformed party cannot ask directly "what type are you?" and receive a truthful answer, so it instead asks indirectly, through the menu itself, and relies on each type's own self-interest to reveal the truth.

Practice Problems: Chapter 21

1. Classify each as adverse selection or moral hazard, and justify using the timing test: (a) a borrower with hidden poor credit history applies for a loan; (b) a borrower, once approved, takes on riskier side ventures than disclosed; (c) a job applicant conceals a chronic health condition from a self-insured employer; (d) an employee, once hired with a fixed salary, exerts less effort than during the interview process.

2. In the procurement lemons example, suppose buyer value rises to \( 2.2q \) instead of \( 1.5q \). Does the market still fully unravel? Solve for the equilibrium price and quality traded, if any.

3. Using the single-crossing property, explain why a signal that is equally costly for both high- and low-productivity workers cannot support a separating equilibrium.

4. Design a two-contract screening menu for a software vendor selling to both heavy users and light users of a metered service, where usage intensity is private information, ensuring both incentive compatibility and individual rationality.

5. Explain why full insurance is never optimal when moral hazard is present, even though it would be optimal under adverse selection alone with risk-neutral insurers and risk-averse policyholders.

A health insurer notices that people who buy its most generous plan use significantly more medical care per dollar of premium than average. Which explanation requires further evidence to distinguish, and why?
Correct. This is precisely why the timing test matters in practice, not just in theory: higher realized utilization in the generous plan is consistent with sicker people self-selecting into it (adverse selection, a pre-contract type effect) and with the plan's low cost-sharing inducing more utilization after enrollment (moral hazard, a post-contract behavioral effect). Distinguishing them requires data on health status prior to plan choice versus changes in behavior after enrollment, exactly the research design used in the applied insurance-economics literature (e.g., the RAND Health Insurance Experiment).

Chapter 22: Principal-Agent Theory & Incentive Design

The Owner–Manager Problem, Formalized

Before the model, think about hiring someone to run a store you cannot watch all day. You want them to work hard and make good decisions; they might prefer an easier day once you are not looking. You cannot directly observe their effort, only the store's sales, which are also driven by things completely outside their control, like the weather or a nearby road closure. Designing a pay package that gets an employee to act in the owner's interest despite this blind spot, without unfairly punishing them for bad luck they did not cause, is exactly the problem this chapter solves with precise mathematics rather than guesswork. Recall that risk averse (Chapter 20) means a person dislikes an uncertain payoff even when its average value equals a certain one; that fact is central to everything below, because a nervous employee must be paid extra just to accept pay that bounces around with performance they cannot fully control.

Chapter 2 introduced the tension between a firm's owners, who want profit maximized, and its managers, who may prefer perks, empire-building, or a quiet life, and noted that owners cannot perfectly monitor managerial effort. That is a moral hazard problem, applied to the specific institutional relationship between a principal (the party delegating a task and bearing residual risk: the owner, the employer) and an agent (the party performing the task with private, unobservable effort: the manager, the employee). Principal-agent theory is the formal machinery for designing contracts that get the agent to act closer to the principal's interest despite this hidden-action problem, and it is one of the most practically consequential bodies of theory in this entire course: it is the analytical foundation for executive compensation, sales commissions, franchise contracts, and performance pay of every kind.

The Principal–Agent Model, Formal Setup. The agent chooses unobservable effort \( e \ge 0 \) at personal cost \( C(e) \), with \( C'>0, C''>0 \) (increasing marginal disutility of effort). Output is a noisy function of effort, \( x = e + \varepsilon \), where \( \varepsilon \) is mean-zero noise with variance \( \sigma^2 \) reflecting factors outside the agent's control. The principal cannot observe \( e \) directly, only \( x \), and pays the agent according to a contract \( w(x) \). The agent is risk averse over income; the principal is risk neutral (a standard, and often reasonable, assumption since a diversified owner can spread risk across many investments while a single employee cannot diversify their labor income).

22.1 First-Best vs. Second-Best

If effort were observable and verifiable (the first-best case), the principal could write a forcing contract: pay a fixed wage if the agent supplies the contractually specified effort level, and pay nothing (or impose a penalty) otherwise. Because the agent is fully insured against the noise \( \varepsilon \) (pay depends only on the verified effort, not on the realized noisy outcome), this achieves full risk-sharing (the risk-neutral principal absorbs all the output risk) while inducing exactly the efficient effort level. First-best is not attainable in the realistic case where effort is unobservable.

In the second-best case, the principal cannot condition pay on effort directly, only on the noisy output \( x \). To induce any effort above the minimum, the contract must make pay vary with x, but doing so necessarily exposes the risk-averse agent to the output noise \( \varepsilon \), which the agent did not create and cannot control. This is the fundamental risk-incentive tradeoff: incentive pay that motivates effort simultaneously imposes unwanted risk on the agent, and the agent must be compensated (in expectation) for bearing that risk, which is costly to the principal.

The Linear Contract Result (Holmström & Milgrom). Under fairly general conditions (CARA utility, normally distributed noise, continuous-time effort), the optimal contract is linear in the observed performance measure:
\[ w(x) = \alpha + \beta x \]
\( \alpha \) is a fixed base salary; \( \beta \in [0,1] \) is the incentive intensity: the share of marginal output the agent keeps, analogous to a piece rate or a bonus-pool sharing rate. \( \beta=0 \) is a pure fixed salary (no incentive, full insurance); \( \beta=1 \) makes the agent the full residual claimant (maximal incentive, zero insurance, as in simple sole proprietorship or a pure commission structure).

22.2 Solving for the Optimal Incentive Intensity

The agent, facing contract \( w=\alpha+\beta x \) and effort cost \( C(e)=e^2/2 \), chooses \( e \) to maximize expected wage minus effort cost: \( \max_e\ \alpha+\beta e - e^2/2 \) (since \( E[x]=e \)). The first-order condition gives the agent's effort response:

\[ e^{*}(\beta) = \beta \]

Higher-powered incentives induce more effort, one-for-one in this simple quadratic-cost case. But the agent, being risk averse, must also be compensated for the risk imposed by tying pay to the noisy \( x \): under CARA utility with risk-aversion coefficient \( r \), the cost of risk-bearing the agent must be compensated for is \( \frac{1}{2}r\beta^2\sigma^2 \), convex and increasing in \( \beta \), since a higher incentive intensity ties more of the agent's pay to the same underlying noise \( \sigma^2 \).

The Incentive Intensity Principle. The principal chooses \( \beta \) to maximize expected output value net of the agent's total compensation (base pay plus the risk premium the agent must be compensated for). Assuming a dollar of effort is worth \( V \) dollars of output value to the principal, the optimal incentive intensity solves:
\[ \beta^{*} = \frac{V}{1 + r\,\sigma^2 \,C''(e)} \]
which in the simple case above (\( V=1 \), \( C''=1 \)) reduces to \( \beta^{*} = \dfrac{1}{1+r\sigma^2} \). The optimal incentive intensity is higher when: (i) the incremental profit from effort \( V \) is larger, (ii) the performance measure is more precise (\( \sigma^2 \) smaller), and (iii) the agent is less risk averse (\( r \) smaller) or has flatter marginal cost of effort. This is Milgrom & Roberts's Incentive Intensity Principle, and it explains a wide range of real compensation patterns: commission-heavy pay for sales roles where individual output is precisely measurable (\( \sigma^2 \) small) and salary-heavy pay for roles with highly noisy, team-dependent, or hard-to-measure output (\( \sigma^2 \) large), not because one job matters less, but because tying pay tightly to a noisy signal is expensive when the agent is risk averse.
Optimal Incentive Intensity
Swipe to see the full diagram
Figure 22.1: The incentive-intensity tradeoff. Net surplus (navy) peaks at \( \beta^{*} \) where the marginal benefit of stronger incentives equals the marginal cost of the risk premium they impose. Raising \( r\sigma^{2} \) (dashed teal) steepens the cost curve and pulls the optimum leftward, toward flatter, more salary-like pay.

Worked Example: Setting a Sales Commission Rate

A firm's sales output has variance \( \sigma^2 = 4 \) (in appropriately scaled units), the sales rep has CARA risk-aversion coefficient \( r = 0.25 \), and effort cost is \( C(e)=e^2/2 \) so \( C''=1 \). A dollar of effort generates \( V=\$3 \) of value to the firm.

Optimal incentive intensity: \( \beta^{*} = \dfrac{V}{1+r\sigma^2 C''} = \dfrac{3}{1+(0.25)(4)(1)} = \dfrac{3}{2} \). Since \( \beta \) must lie in \( [0,1] \) as a share of a single measure (this formula generalizes the earlier normalized case; a value above 1 here signals that, in the richer model, the firm should supplement the base commission with a multiplier or bonus structure rather than treat \( \beta \) as literally capped). The substantive lesson is comparative: if the same rep's territory has more volatile monthly results (\( \sigma^2 \) rises to 12), \( \beta^{*} \) falls to \( 3/(1+3)=0.75 \), a full third lower. A firm that pays every sales territory the same flat commission rate is, by the Incentive Intensity Principle, over-incentivizing (and over-paying the risk premium for) its most volatile territories and under-incentivizing its most predictable ones.

22.3 The Multitasking Problem

The multitasking extension adds a sharp practical warning. When an agent allocates effort across multiple tasks, some easily measured (units shipped, calls closed) and some not (customer relationship quality, mentoring junior staff, long-run product quality, safety), a high-powered incentive on the measurable task pulls effort away from the unmeasured ones, not because the agent stops caring, but because effort is a scarce resource being reallocated toward whatever is being paid for. This is the formal explanation for well-documented perverse outcomes of aggressive incentive schemes: quotas that induce channel-stuffing or quality shortcuts, piece rates that degrade craftsmanship, and sales incentives that damage customer relationships the metric does not capture. The correct managerial response is not to abandon incentive pay but to either broaden the performance measure to capture more of what matters, or deliberately flatten \( \beta \) on the measurable task when it is highly correlated with effort diverted from unmeasured but valuable tasks.

22.5 Executive Compensation as Applied Principal-Agent Theory

Public-company executive compensation is the highest-stakes, most publicly scrutinized application of this chapter's entire framework, and it is worth working through explicitly. A CEO's total pay package typically combines a base salary (low \( \beta \), pure insurance against firm performance the CEO cannot fully control, such as a recession or an industry-wide shock), an annual cash bonus tied to accounting metrics such as earnings per share or revenue targets (moderate \( \beta \), tied to a noisy but observable short-run performance measure), and equity compensation in the form of stock options or restricted stock (potentially very high \( \beta \), tied to the noisiest but most theoretically appealing performance measure: the firm's own long-run market value, which in principle captures the full discounted value of all the CEO's decisions, including ones with consequences that will not show up in this year's earnings). The Incentive-Intensity Principle explains why boards do not simply set \( \beta=1 \) (pure equity, no salary) despite equity's theoretical appeal: stock price is an extremely noisy measure of CEO effort specifically, since it reflects enormous amounts of information about macroeconomic conditions, industry trends, and investor sentiment that have nothing to do with the CEO's own decisions, and a risk-averse CEO would demand an enormous risk premium to accept full exposure to that noise. The observed mix, typically 10–20% salary, a meaningful cash bonus, and a large but not total equity component, is a real-world instance of exactly the \( \beta^{*} \) tradeoff this chapter derives, further complicated by the multitasking problem: a CEO paid almost entirely on quarterly earnings has a well-documented incentive to cut long-run-value-creating investment (R&D, maintenance capital expenditure, employee training) in order to hit a short-run accounting target, which is exactly why compensation committees increasingly weight multi-year vesting schedules and a blend of accounting and market-based metrics rather than a single easily-gamed number.

Relation to Chapter 21. Principal-agent theory is not a new information problem; it is moral hazard (hidden action, post-contract) applied specifically to the employment and ownership relationship, with the added structure of an explicit compensation-design optimization. Where Chapter 21 asked "what remedies exist for hidden action in general," this chapter derives the quantitative optimal-contract answer for the specific principal-agent case: the linear contract \( w=\alpha+\beta x \) and the closed-form \( \beta^{*} \).

22.6 Monitoring as a Complement, Not a Substitute

It is tempting to treat monitoring (direct observation of effort) and incentive pay (indirect inference of effort from noisy output) as competing solutions to the same moral hazard problem, but in most real organizations they are complementary, and the theory explains why. Monitoring reduces the effective variance \( \sigma^2 \) of the performance measure the principal actually conditions pay on: a supervisor's periodic direct observation, when combined with output data, produces a less noisy composite signal than output alone. Recall from the Incentive-Intensity Principle that \( \beta^{*} \) is decreasing in \( \sigma^2 \): any investment that reduces measurement noise (better information systems, more frequent check-ins, structured performance reviews) directly raises the optimal incentive intensity the firm can profitably offer, because it lowers the risk premium the agent must be compensated for bearing. This is why organizations that invest heavily in monitoring infrastructure (real-time sales dashboards, project-management software, detailed activity logs) are also, empirically, the organizations best positioned to use high-powered individual incentive pay effectively, since the two investments reinforce each other rather than substituting for one another. The strategic question for a manager designing a compensation and management-control system jointly is not "should we monitor or should we incentivize," but "given our monitoring technology's cost and precision, what is the resulting optimal \( \beta^{*} \), and is further investment in reducing \( \sigma^2 \) worth its cost relative to the incentive gains it would unlock."

For instructors: connect explicitly back to Chapter 2's separation of ownership and control. Stock options and performance bonuses are real-world \( \beta \)'s chosen by boards facing exactly this tradeoff; excessively "gamed" executive compensation scandals (backdating, earnings manipulation to hit bonus thresholds) are multitasking problems, not evidence that incentive pay is a bad idea in principle; they are evidence of a poorly chosen or poorly monitored performance measure.

22.4 Alternative Solutions: Efficiency Wages and Tournaments

The linear-contract framework is the workhorse model, but two other real-world incentive mechanisms address the same underlying moral hazard problem through different channels.

Efficiency wages pay the agent strictly above the market-clearing wage, creating an endogenous cost of job loss: if caught shirking and fired, the agent loses a rent (the gap between the efficiency wage and what they could earn elsewhere) they would not otherwise have had. This works even when output is not observable at all: it substitutes the threat of termination-after-detected-shirking for the pay-for-performance mechanism of the linear contract, and is most useful precisely where individual output cannot be measured with any precision (many white-collar and team-production settings), which is also where the Incentive-Intensity Principle would predict a very low \( \beta^{*} \) and hence very weak performance-pay incentives from the linear contract alone. Efficiency wages and low-powered linear incentives are therefore complements, not substitutes, in noisy-output settings: together they explain why many salaried professional jobs combine a flat salary (low \( \beta \)) that is nonetheless persistently above the competitive market wage.

Tournaments (relative-performance contracts) pay based on an agent's rank among peers rather than their absolute output level: a fixed prize to whoever sells the most, is promoted fastest, or scores highest, regardless of the absolute level of output achieved by anyone. Tournaments have a powerful advantage when the underlying performance measure faces a common shock affecting all agents simultaneously (an industry-wide demand slump, a shared economic downturn): because every competitor's output moves together with the common shock, ranking agents against each other automatically filters out that shared noise, leaving relative effort as the main determinant of the prize. This is a distinct channel from the individual-noise-reduction that the Incentive-Intensity Principle addresses, and it explains why sales-force rankings, promotion tournaments, and relative bonus pools are common specifically in industries with large common demand shocks (real estate, commodity trading) where an individual linear contract on absolute output would force agents to bear a great deal of risk they did not create and cannot control.

Practice Problems: Chapter 22

1. Using \( \beta^{*}=1/(1+r\sigma^2) \) (the normalized case), compute \( \beta^{*} \) for \( r=0.1 \), \( \sigma^2=2 \), and again for \( r=0.5,\ \sigma^2=2 \). Interpret the difference.

2. Explain, in your own words, why a risk-neutral principal facing a risk-averse agent cannot generally achieve the first-best outcome once effort is unobservable, even though the principal would be willing to bear all the risk.

3. A call center pays representatives a piece rate per call closed. Using the multitasking framework, list two dimensions of service quality this metric likely under-incentivizes, and propose a compensation redesign.

4. Explain why franchise contracts (a franchisee pays a fixed fee and keeps all residual profit, \( \beta\approx 1 \)) are a natural principal-agent solution when the franchisee's effort is highly influential and hard to monitor, but local demand shocks are small relative to the franchisee's ability to bear risk.

5. Tie the incentive-intensity tradeoff explicitly back to Chapter 2: explain why an all-equity-compensated startup CEO faces very different \( \beta \) incentives than a large public company's CEO paid mostly in salary, and what that implies about which type of firm should attract more risk-tolerant managers.

A firm moves a manager from a role with highly precise, easily verified individual output metrics to a role with noisy, team-dependent output. According to the Incentive Intensity Principle, what should happen to the optimal share of pay tied to performance (\( \beta \))?
Correct. \( \beta^{*} \) is decreasing in \( r\sigma^2 \): a noisier performance signal forces the principal to pay a larger risk premium for any given incentive intensity, so the optimal contract shifts toward a flatter, more salary-heavy structure. This is exactly why most firms pay individual commission for precisely-measured solo sales roles but salary-plus-modest-bonus for roles with noisy, team-based output.

Chapter 23: Capital Budgeting & Investment Decisions

Evaluating Long-Lived Investment Decisions

Before the formulas, suppose a friend offers you a choice between $1,000 today and $1,000 in three years. Nearly everyone picks today, and not only because of inflation: a dollar in hand can be invested, spent, or simply enjoyed sooner. This is the idea behind present value: putting a dollar figure, today, on a payment that will not actually arrive until later, by “discounting” it to account for the wait. Capital budgeting is this everyday idea applied to a firm's biggest, slowest-payoff decisions: is a new factory, a product launch, or an acquisition actually worth its cost once every future dollar of benefit is translated back into today's dollars? A closely related everyday idea, opportunity cost, the value of the next-best alternative you give up by choosing one option over another, is exactly what the “discount rate” below represents: the return the firm's money could have earned somewhere else instead.

Capital budgeting is the process by which a firm decides which long-term investment projects to undertake (a new plant, an acquisition, a product-development program) when the costs are incurred now (or over several periods) and the benefits arrive later, often over many years. Every capital-budgeting rule is ultimately an attempt to operationalize the same objective from Chapter 2: maximize the wealth of the firm's owners. This chapter derives the two dominant discounted cash flow criteria, Net Present Value and Internal Rate of Return, states precisely why NPV is the theoretically correct rule and IRR is not universally reliable, and covers the simpler payback rule and the practical problem of choosing a discount rate.

Net Present Value (NPV). For a project with initial outlay \( CF_0 \) (a cash outflow, typically negative) and subsequent cash flows \( CF_1, CF_2, \ldots, CF_n \) over \( n \) periods, discounted at rate \( r \) (the cost of capital):
\[ NPV = -CF_0 + \sum_{t=1}^{n} \frac{CF_t}{(1+r)^{t}} \]
Decision rule: accept the project if \( NPV > 0 \); among mutually exclusive projects, choose the one with the highest NPV. A positive NPV means the project's discounted cash inflows exceed its cost by more than the return investors could have earned elsewhere at the same risk; it adds exactly \( NPV \) dollars, in today's money, to shareholder wealth.
Internal Rate of Return (IRR). The discount rate \( r^{*} \) that makes \( NPV = 0 \):
\[ -CF_0 + \sum_{t=1}^{n} \frac{CF_t}{(1+r^{*})^{t}} = 0 \]
Decision rule: accept the project if \( r^{*} \) exceeds the firm's hurdle rate (cost of capital). IRR is popular because it produces a single percentage figure comparable across projects of different scale without first specifying a discount rate.
The IRR Pitfall: Multiple or Non-Existent Roots. \( NPV(r) \) is a polynomial in \( 1/(1+r) \) of degree \( n \). For a conventional cash flow pattern (a single sign change: an initial outflow followed only by inflows), Descartes' Rule of Signs guarantees at most one positive real root, so IRR is well-defined and unambiguous. For an unconventional pattern (cash flows that change sign more than once: an outflow, then inflows, then another outflow, as in a project with a large decommissioning or remediation cost at the end of its life), the polynomial can have multiple positive real roots, or none at all. When multiple IRRs exist, the accept/reject rule "compare \( r^{*} \) to the hurdle rate" is not well defined: which root should govern the decision? IRR should never be presented, or used in practice, as a universally reliable decision rule without checking the sign pattern of the cash flow stream first. NPV, by contrast, is always a single well-defined number for any discount rate and never suffers this ambiguity.
NPV Profile vs. Discount Rate
Swipe to see the full diagram
Figure 23.1: NPV as a function of the discount rate. Conventional cash flows (single sign change) give one well-defined IRR. The classic "oil well" pattern \( -1600, +10000, -10000 \) (invest, extract, remediate) has two sign changes and two IRRs, 25% and 400%, illustrating why IRR alone cannot reliably guide the accept/reject decision for unconventional cash flow patterns.

Worked Example: NPV and IRR for a Conventional Project

A project requires an initial outlay of $100,000 and generates $40,000 per year for 4 years. The firm's cost of capital is 10%.

NPV at 10%: the 4-year annuity factor at 10% is 3.1699, so:

\[ \begin{aligned} NPV &= -100{,}000 + 40{,}000(3.1699) \\ &= -100{,}000 + 126{,}796 \\ &= \$26{,}796 \end{aligned} \]

Since \( NPV>0 \), accept.

IRR: solving \( -100+40\times AF(r,4)=0 \) numerically gives \( r^{*}\approx 21.5\% \). Since \( 21.5\%>10\% \) (the hurdle rate), IRR agrees with NPV: accept. For this conventional single-sign-change cash flow stream, NPV and IRR always agree on the accept/reject decision for a single, independent project (though not necessarily on the ranking of mutually exclusive projects of different scale, a separate, well-documented issue solved by comparing incremental cash flows or simply ranking by NPV directly).

Worked Example: The Oil-Well Problem (Multiple IRR)

A project costs $1,600 to initiate, pays $10,000 at the end of year 1, and requires a $10,000 remediation/shutdown payment at the end of year 2. At the firm's actual cost of capital of, say, 15%:

\[ \begin{aligned} NPV &= -1600 + \dfrac{10{,}000}{1.15} - \dfrac{10{,}000}{1.15^2} \\ &= -1600 + 8{,}696 - 7{,}561 \\ &= -\$465 \end{aligned} \]

NPV says reject. But this project has two IRRs, 25% and 400%, both of which exceed the 15% hurdle rate. A manager who mechanically applies "accept if IRR > hurdle rate" using either root would wrongly accept a project that NPV correctly identifies as value-destroying at the firm's true cost of capital. This is not a contrived textbook curiosity: the pattern (an upfront cost, a large early cash inflow, and a large delayed cash outflow) is common in extractive industries, decommissioning-heavy projects, and any investment with a significant end-of-life liability.

23.1 Why NPV Is Theoretically Correct and IRR Is Not

Beyond the multiple-root problem, NPV and IRR differ in a more fundamental way: their implicit reinvestment rate assumption. NPV implicitly assumes intermediate cash flows are reinvested at the discount rate \( r \) (the cost of capital), a realistic assumption, since \( r \) is precisely the rate at which the firm can access capital in the market. IRR implicitly assumes intermediate cash flows are reinvested at the project's own IRR, \( r^{*} \), which, for a high-IRR project, is an unrealistically optimistic assumption (there is no reason to believe the firm has an ongoing supply of other projects paying that same high rate to reinvest into). NPV is additive across projects and directly measures the dollar increment to shareholder wealth (the owner-value-maximization objective from Chapter 2); IRR is a percentage that does not correctly account for project scale and cannot simply be summed or compared across projects of different size. For all these reasons, standard corporate finance theory treats NPV, not IRR, as the primary decision criterion, with IRR retained as a useful supplementary communication tool precisely because managers find a percentage intuitive, provided its pitfalls are understood.

Payback Period: Simpler, but Theoretically Weaker

The payback period is the number of years required for a project's cumulative cash flows to recover the initial investment. Decision rule: accept if payback is less than some cutoff (often set informally, e.g. 3 years).

Strengths: extremely simple to compute and communicate; serves as a rough liquidity/risk screen, since projects that recoup cash sooner expose the firm to less cumulative uncertainty and free up capital sooner for other uses.

Weaknesses (theoretical): (1) the standard (undiscounted) payback period ignores the time value of money entirely: a dollar in year 1 and a dollar in year 3 are treated identically; (2) it ignores all cash flows occurring after the cutoff, so a project with a marginally longer payback but enormous cash flows in later years can be wrongly rejected in favor of one with a short payback and nothing thereafter; (3) the cutoff itself is arbitrary, with no grounding in the firm's actual cost of capital or shareholder-value-maximization objective. A partial fix, discounted payback, restores the time-value-of-money correction by discounting cash flows at \( r \) before cumulating them, but it does not solve problem (2). Payback is best used as a supplementary risk/liquidity screen alongside NPV, never as the sole capital-budgeting criterion.

23.2 Choosing the Discount Rate: Cost of Capital

NPV's correctness depends entirely on discounting at the right rate. The standard approach is the Weighted Average Cost of Capital (WACC):

\[ WACC = \frac{E}{V}R_e + \frac{D}{V}R_d(1-T_c) \]

where \( E \) and \( D \) are the market values of equity and debt, \( V=E+D \), \( R_d \) is the pre-tax cost of debt, \( T_c \) is the corporate tax rate (debt interest is tax-deductible, hence the \( (1-T_c) \) adjustment), and \( R_e \) is the cost of equity, typically estimated via the Capital Asset Pricing Model:

\[ R_e = R_f + \beta_{equity}(R_m - R_f) \]

with \( R_f \) the risk-free rate, \( R_m \) the expected market return, and \( \beta_{equity} \) the systematic risk of the firm's equity. A critical, frequently violated principle: the discount rate should reflect the risk of the specific project, not the firm's overall WACC, whenever the project's risk differs materially from the firm's average risk. A stable, mature manufacturer evaluating a high-risk biotech venture should discount that venture at a higher, project-specific rate (often estimated using the beta of comparable "pure-play" firms in that industry, unlevered and then relevered to the financing structure of the project), not at its own low corporate WACC. Using the firm-wide WACC for an unusually risky project systematically overstates its NPV and biases the firm toward excessive risk-taking; conversely, using it for an unusually safe project understates NPV and biases the firm toward under-investing in safe, value-creating projects.

A further practical wrinkle in discount-rate selection deserves explicit attention: whether to discount nominal cash flows (including expected inflation) at a nominal discount rate, or real cash flows (stripped of inflation) at a real discount rate. The Fisher relation links the two: \( (1+r_{nominal}) = (1+r_{real})(1+\pi) \), where \( \pi \) is expected inflation. Mixing the two (discounting nominal cash flow projections at a real discount rate, or vice versa) is a common and serious practitioner error that systematically biases NPV, typically overstating it when nominal cash flows are (incorrectly) discounted at a lower real rate, since inflation is then double-counted in the cash flow growth but not netted out in the discount rate. The correct practice is internal consistency: either project nominal cash flows (including an explicit inflation assumption for revenues and costs, which need not inflate at the same rate) and discount at the nominal WACC, or project real, inflation-adjusted cash flows and discount at the real WACC. The two approaches give identical NPV when applied consistently, and the choice between them is a matter of modeling convenience, not of which one is theoretically superior.

Capital rationing and the Profitability Index. When capital is constrained (the firm cannot fund every positive-NPV project), ranking projects by raw NPV can be suboptimal if project sizes differ. The Profitability Index, \( PI = \dfrac{PV(\text{future cash flows})}{\text{initial investment}} \), ranks projects by NPV generated per dollar of scarce capital, giving the capital-constrained firm the combination of projects that maximizes total NPV subject to the budget constraint (formally, this is a special case of the general knapsack-style optimization for capital allocation under a binding budget constraint).
For instructors: emphasize the unifying thread across this chapter's rules. NPV is the theoretically correct criterion because it is the only one that directly measures the change in shareholder wealth in today's dollars, the same owner-value-maximization objective introduced in Chapter 2 and defended against the managerial-discretion problem in Chapter 22. IRR, payback, and the Profitability Index are all useful communication and screening tools that practitioners reach for because they are more intuitive or handle capital constraints more directly, but each has a specific, well-understood domain where it can diverge from the NPV-correct answer, and a well-trained manager should be able to state precisely when and why each rule can mislead, not simply prefer NPV by reflex.

23.3 Sensitivity Analysis, Sunk Costs, and Real Options

A single-point NPV estimate hides the fact that every input (the discount rate, the projected cash flows, the terminal value assumption) is itself uncertain, and a manager should never present or act on a single NPV number without probing how sensitive it is to reasonable variation in those inputs. Sensitivity analysis recomputes NPV while varying one assumption at a time (a "tornado diagram" ranks assumptions by how much NPV moves per unit change), while scenario analysis and Monte Carlo simulation vary several correlated assumptions simultaneously to produce a full distribution of possible NPV outcomes rather than a single number, connecting capital budgeting directly back to the expected-utility and risk-premium framework of Chapter 20: a project with a positive expected NPV but a wide, highly skewed distribution of possible outcomes may still be unattractive to a risk-averse decision-maker (or a capital-constrained firm facing bankruptcy risk at the low end of the distribution) even though its point-estimate NPV clears the bar.

Capital budgeting decisions are also where the sunk cost fallacy most commonly and most expensively appears in practice. Money already spent on a project (a failed prototype, a partially built facility) is a sunk cost: it is unrecoverable regardless of what the firm does next, and therefore it is irrelevant to the forward-looking decision of whether to continue or abandon. The correct capital-budgeting test at any decision point is not "will this project, including money already spent, eventually show a positive return," but "does continuing (spending only the remaining required investment) have positive NPV from today forward, given the project's remaining expected cash flows." A project that has already consumed $40 million with a positive original NPV can rationally become a project to abandon if new information reveals the remaining cash flows no longer justify the remaining required investment. Conversely, refusing to abandon a value-destroying project merely because of the sunk investment already made ("we've come this far, we can't stop now") is a textbook violation of the forward-looking NPV rule, not a prudent application of it.

Finally, standard NPV analysis implicitly assumes a fixed, one-time, now-or-never decision, but many real investment opportunities embed valuable managerial flexibility: the option to expand if a pilot succeeds, to delay until uncertainty resolves, or to abandon early and recover some salvage value. Real options analysis extends capital budgeting by valuing this flexibility explicitly (often using option-pricing techniques adapted from financial options), and can justify accepting a project with a negative static NPV if the embedded option value (particularly the option to expand aggressively upon a favorable early signal, or to abandon cheaply upon an unfavorable one) is large enough. A full treatment of real options valuation is beyond this course's scope, but the qualitative lesson is directly relevant to every capital-budgeting decision a manager makes: a rigid single-point NPV calculation that ignores the value of being able to react to new information as it arrives will systematically undervalue flexible, staged investments relative to rigid, all-at-once ones.

Practice Problems: Chapter 23

1. A project costs $250,000 and returns $70,000/year for 5 years. At a 9% cost of capital, compute the NPV. Compute the IRR (you may approximate numerically) and confirm the two rules agree on accept/reject.

2. Explain, using Descartes' Rule of Signs, why a conventional cash flow stream (one sign change) is guaranteed at most one positive real IRR, while a stream with three sign changes could have up to three.

3. Two mutually exclusive projects have different scales: Project A costs $10,000 and returns $15,000 in one year (IRR=50%); Project B costs $100,000 and returns $130,000 in one year (IRR=30%). At a 10% cost of capital, compute both NPVs and explain why IRR ranking alone would lead to the wrong choice.

4. A firm with WACC = 8% is evaluating a much riskier R&D project with an estimated project-specific discount rate of 16%. Explain, with reference to the CAPM, why using 8% instead of 16% would bias the firm toward accepting too many risky projects.

5. Explain why undiscounted payback period can reject a project that has strongly positive NPV, using a numerical example with a cutoff of 3 years and a project whose largest cash flows arrive in years 4 and 5.

A project has cash flows that change sign twice (e.g., an initial outlay, a large inflow, and a large later outflow for decommissioning). What is the most accurate statement about using IRR to evaluate it?
Correct. Unconventional (multiply sign-changing) cash flow patterns can produce multiple positive real IRRs or none, because NPV(r) is a higher-degree polynomial that can cross zero more than once. NPV itself remains perfectly well-defined at any chosen discount rate and is unaffected by this issue; it is the IRR decision rule specifically, not NPV, that breaks down for unconventional cash flows.

Chapter 24: Government, Regulation & Market Failure

When Markets Do Not Deliver Efficiency

Before the theory, picture a factory next to a river that people downstream use for fishing and drinking water. If the factory dumps waste into the river to save money, it pushes a real cost onto people who were never part of the transaction between the factory and its customers, and who are never compensated for it. That everyday unfairness, one party's decision hurting an outside party who never agreed to anything, is the plain-language version of what this chapter calls an externality, and it is one of the main reasons free markets, left alone, do not always produce the best outcome for society as a whole (a market failure). This closing chapter asks: when exactly does this happen, and what realistic tools, taxes, clearly assigned property rights, or direct regulation, can actually fix it?

This capstone chapter connects managerial economics to public policy: the same efficiency logic that has run through this entire course (profit maximization, MR = MC, welfare-maximizing quantity) also defines exactly when unregulated markets fail to maximize total surplus, and why governments intervene. The classic sources of market failure are externalities, public goods, market power (Ch. 13), and information asymmetry (Ch. 21); this chapter focuses on externalities, the Coase Theorem, public goods, and the economic rationale for antitrust and regulatory policy, closing the loop back to the monopoly deadweight loss first derived in Chapter 13.

Externality. An externality is an uncompensated impact of one party's production or consumption decision on the well-being of a third party who is not party to the transaction, and which is therefore not reflected in the market price. A negative externality (pollution, congestion, noise) imposes an uncompensated cost on third parties; a positive externality (vaccination, R&D spillovers, restored historic buildings) confers an uncompensated benefit.
The Private–Social Divergence. With a negative externality, marginal social cost exceeds marginal private cost, \( MSC(Q) = MPC(Q) + MEC(Q) \), where \( MEC \) is the marginal external cost. Because the market equilibrium equates demand only to \( MPC \) (the firm bears none of the external cost), the market overproduces relative to the socially efficient quantity \( Q^{*} \), where demand (= marginal social benefit, assuming no consumption externality) equals \( MSC \). The excess output between \( Q^{*} \) and the market quantity \( Q_{mkt} \) generates a deadweight loss: for every unit beyond \( Q^{*} \), the marginal social cost of producing it exceeds its marginal value to buyers. Symmetrically, a positive externality means \( MSB(Q) = MPB(Q) + MEB(Q) \) exceeds private marginal benefit, and the unregulated market underproduces relative to \( Q^{*} \).
Negative Externality & the Pigouvian Tax
Swipe to see the full diagram
Figure 24.1: Negative production externality. The market (private) equilibrium at \( Q_{mkt}=57.1 \) exceeds the socially efficient \( Q^{*}=42.9 \), generating the shaded deadweight loss triangle. A Pigouvian tax equal to the marginal external cost shifts the effective private supply curve up to MSC, restoring the social optimum.

Worked Example: Quantifying the Externality and the Corrective Tax

Demand: \( P = 100 - Q \). Private marginal cost: \( MPC = 20 + 0.4Q \). Marginal external cost (pollution damage per unit): constant at \( MEC=\$20 \), so \( MSC = 40+0.4Q \).

Private (market) equilibrium: \( D=MPC \Rightarrow 100-Q=20+0.4Q \Rightarrow Q_{mkt}=57.14,\ P_{mkt}=\$42.86 \).

Social optimum: \( D=MSC \Rightarrow 100-Q=40+0.4Q \Rightarrow Q^{*}=42.86,\ P^{*}=\$57.14 \).

Deadweight loss: the triangle between \( Q^{*} \) and \( Q_{mkt} \), bounded by \( D \) and \( MSC \). Base \( = Q_{mkt}-Q^{*}=14.28 \); height \( = MSC(Q_{mkt}) - D(Q_{mkt}) = 62.86-42.86=20 \) (exactly the constant \( MEC \), since \( D \) and \( MSC \) have the same slope gap as \( D \) and \( MPC \) plus the constant markup). \( DWL = \frac12(14.28)(20)=\$142.80 \).

Pigouvian tax: a per-unit tax of \( t=\$20 \) (exactly equal to the marginal external cost) makes the firm's effective marginal cost \( MPC+t=40+0.4Q=MSC \). The taxed market now settles exactly at \( Q^{*}=42.86,\ P^{*}=\$57.14 \), and the deadweight loss is eliminated. The tax revenue collected, \( t\times Q^{*}=20\times42.86=\$857.20 \), can (though need not) be used to compensate those harmed by the residual, now-efficient level of pollution.

24.1 The Coase Theorem

Coase Theorem. If property rights are clearly defined and transaction costs are zero (or negligible), private bargaining between the parties will achieve the efficient allocation of resources regardless of how the property right is initially assigned. The assignment of the property right affects who pays whom (the distribution of surplus), but not the efficient outcome itself (the quantity of the externality-generating activity).

The classic illustration: a factory's operations impose noise damage of $8,000/year on a neighboring laundry; the factory could install a noise abatement device for $5,000/year, eliminating the damage. If the factory has the legal right to operate freely, the laundry can offer the factory anywhere between $5,000 and $8,000 to install the device; both parties gain from a deal (the factory profits, the laundry avoids $8,000 in damage for less), and the device gets installed. If instead the laundry has the legal right to be free of noise, the factory must either pay to abate ($5,000, cheaper than being shut down) or pay the laundry compensation exceeding $5,000 to continue operating without abatement; either way, the efficient outcome (abatement, since it costs $5,000 to prevent $8,000 of damage) is again reached, just with the money flowing in the opposite direction. In both legal regimes, the device gets installed; only who ends up paying for it differs.

Why the Coase Theorem is not a general argument against regulation. The theorem's conclusion depends entirely on its two conditions holding, and in most real externality problems they do not. Transaction costs are rarely negligible when many parties are affected: negotiating a private bargain between one factory and thousands of nearby residents (let alone the entire population affected by a greenhouse-gas emitter) faces prohibitive coordination and free-rider problems: each individual has an incentive to let others fund the bargain while still enjoying the benefit. Property rights are frequently ill-defined or unenforceable (nobody owns the atmosphere or the ocean). Information about the true magnitude of damages may itself be asymmetric or costly to establish, undermining the bargain. This is precisely why real-world externality policy relies heavily on Pigouvian taxes, cap-and-trade permit systems, and direct regulation rather than private bargaining: as the number of affected parties grows, the Coasean solution's transaction-cost requirement becomes less and less realistic, while the case for a centralized corrective instrument grows stronger.

24.2 Public Goods and the Free-Rider Problem

A public good is characterized by non-rivalry (one person's consumption does not reduce what is available to others: national defense, a lighthouse, a broadcast signal) and non-excludability (no one can be cost-effectively prevented from consuming it once provided). Because no one can be excluded, individuals have an incentive to free-ride, consuming without paying, leading private markets to underprovide public goods relative to the efficient level, or fail to provide them at all.

The Samuelson Condition. For a private good, efficiency requires each individual's marginal rate of substitution to equal the marginal rate of transformation, \( MRS_i = MRT \) for every consumer \( i \) (since consumption is rival: more for one means less for another). For a public good, because everyone consumes the same quantity simultaneously, efficiency instead requires the sum of individual marginal valuations to equal marginal cost:
\[ \sum_{i=1}^{I} MRS_i = MRT \]
A market that only aggregates individually expressed willingness-to-pay (as a private-goods market does) systematically undervalues a public good relative to this efficient condition, because no single consumer's own willingness to pay reflects the value everyone else simultaneously receives. This is the standard economic justification for public provision or subsidization of public goods, from basic research to national defense.

24.3 The Antitrust Rationale: Closing the Loop to Monopoly

Chapter 13 derived the monopoly deadweight loss: a profit-maximizing monopolist restricts output below the competitive (efficient) level, raising price above marginal cost and destroying surplus in the triangle between the competitive and monopoly quantities. This is the precise economic rationale for antitrust policy, not an aesthetic preference for "fair" market structure, but the same deadweight-loss logic applied to market power as a source of market failure. In the United States, the Sherman Act (1890) prohibits monopolization and restraints of trade (price-fixing, market allocation among competitors); the Clayton Act (1914) addresses mergers and exclusionary practices that may substantially lessen competition. Merger review commonly uses concentration measures such as the Herfindahl-Hirschman Index (HHI), \( HHI=\sum_i s_i^2 \) (sum of squared market shares, in percentage points), with thresholds used by antitrust agencies to flag mergers likely to create or enhance market power and the associated deadweight loss. Conduct-based antitrust enforcement targets practices such as predatory pricing (pricing below cost to drive out rivals, then raising price once market power is secured) and illegal tying (Ch. 18's bundling logic used to leverage power from one market into another) precisely because these practices are ways firms attempt to create or extend the same kind of output-restricting market power analyzed in Chapter 13.

24.5 Regulatory Tools Compared

It is worth drawing together, in one place, the full menu of policy responses this chapter has developed and being explicit about the tradeoffs among them, since real policymakers must choose among instruments whose ideal-world equivalence (all can, in principle, reach the same efficient outcome \( Q^{*} \)) breaks down once realistic frictions are admitted. A Pigouvian tax requires the regulator to know the marginal external cost accurately to set the correct rate; if set too low, some deadweight loss remains, and if set too high, the market underproduces relative to the true optimum, but a tax is administratively simple to implement once the rate is chosen, and it raises revenue that can offset other distortionary taxes elsewhere in the economy (the "double dividend" argument for environmental taxation). A cap-and-trade scheme fixes the quantity directly, which is attractive when the environmental or resource-depletion damage from exceeding a threshold rises sharply (a hard ecological limit) rather than smoothly, but it requires credible monitoring and enforcement of the cap and can produce volatile permit prices if demand for permits fluctuates while the fixed supply does not. Command-and-control regulation (mandating a specific technology or a uniform emissions limit for every firm, rather than letting a tax or a tradable permit market determine who reduces how much) is the least economically efficient of the three in general, because it does not equalize the marginal cost of abatement across firms (some firms could abate much more cheaply than others, and a uniform mandate wastes that opportunity for efficient reallocation), but it can be the pragmatic choice when measuring individual firms' emissions or costs precisely enough to price them correctly is itself prohibitively difficult, or when the political process demands a visible, easily verified standard rather than a price signal whose ultimate quantity effect is harder for non-economists to predict and defend.

Natural monopoly and the regulatory dilemma. Some industries (traditional electricity transmission, water utilities) exhibit large fixed costs and declining average cost over the relevant range of output: a natural monopoly, where a single firm can serve the market at lower average cost than multiple competing firms. Regulators face a genuine dilemma here: marginal-cost pricing (the efficient rule) forces the firm into a loss when average cost is falling, since marginal cost lies below average cost; average-cost pricing avoids the loss but sacrifices some efficiency (output priced above marginal cost). Rate-of-return regulation attempts to let the firm earn a fair return on invested capital while capping prices, but is well documented to create its own distortions (an incentive to over-invest in rate base, the Averch-Johnson effect) and carries a persistent risk of regulatory capture, in which the regulated firm gains disproportionate influence over the agency meant to constrain it, a reminder that government intervention corrects one market failure only by introducing its own institutional risks, not a costless cure.
For instructors: this chapter is the natural closing lecture of the course. Have students explicitly trace the single thread running through the entire syllabus: the gap between marginal private incentive and marginal social value is the common diagnostic across monopoly (Ch. 13), externalities, and public goods, and the corrective tools (Pigouvian taxes, antitrust enforcement, public provision, and in Ch. 21–22, mechanism design for information problems) are all instances of the same underlying idea: aligning private incentives with social efficiency.

24.4 The Tragedy of the Commons and Cap-and-Trade

A closely related public-goods problem arises with common-pool resources, rival (one person's use depletes what is available to others) but non-excludable (no one can be cheaply prevented from using them): an open-access fishery, groundwater, uncongested-but-shared road capacity, or the atmosphere's capacity to absorb greenhouse gases. Because no individual user bears the full cost of their own extraction (the depletion cost is spread across all current and future users), each user's private marginal benefit of extraction exceeds the true social marginal benefit once the externality on other users is counted, formally identical to the negative-externality diagram of this chapter, with "other users' foregone future access" playing the role of the third party bearing the uncompensated marginal external cost. The result, famously called the tragedy of the commons, is that absent some corrective institution, a shared resource is systematically overexploited relative to the socially efficient extraction rate, exactly as the standard negative-externality model predicts.

Three broad institutional responses map directly onto the tools already developed in this chapter. Pigouvian taxation (a per-unit extraction tax or emissions tax set equal to the marginal external cost) directly raises the private cost of extraction to the social level, exactly as in the worked pollution example above. Cap-and-trade instead fixes the aggregate quantity of extraction or emissions directly at the socially efficient level \( Q^{*} \) and creates a market in tradable permits, letting the price of a permit emerge endogenously from supply and demand for the fixed number of permits issued, a Coasean solution in the sense that, once property rights (the permits) are clearly defined, firms trade them to reach an efficient allocation of who actually reduces emissions, at lowest aggregate cost, regardless of which firms were initially handed the permits (mirroring the distributional-versus-efficiency distinction of the Coase Theorem itself). Both instruments can, under idealized conditions (certainty about costs and benefits, no monitoring or enforcement costs), achieve the same efficient quantity \( Q^{*} \); they differ mainly in which variable is fixed directly (price, under a tax, versus quantity, under a cap) and therefore in how each performs when the regulator's information about the true marginal cost or benefit curves is imperfect: a classic result on the choice between price and quantity instruments under uncertainty. The third response, direct regulation or assignment of private property rights (privatizing a fishery into individually owned, tradable catch shares, for instance), converts a non-excludable common-pool resource into an excludable private good, internalizing the externality by giving a single owner the full incentive to manage the resource's long-run value, the same logic Coase identified for two-party externalities, scaled up to a resource shared among many.

Practice Problems: Chapter 24

1. Demand is \( P=120-Q \), \( MPC=Q \), and \( MEC=\$30 \) constant. Compute the private equilibrium, the social optimum, the deadweight loss, and the Pigouvian tax that corrects it.

2. Using the Coase Theorem, explain why the efficient outcome (whether the factory installs abatement equipment) does not depend on who holds the property right, using the laundry/factory numbers from the text, but show that the dollar transfer between the parties does depend on it.

3. Explain, with reference to transaction costs, why the Coase Theorem is a poor guide to solving a global externality like greenhouse gas emissions affecting billions of people, even though it works well for the two-party factory/laundry example.

4. A city considers building a public park serving 10,000 residents, each with a maximum willingness to pay of $40/year for it, at an annual cost of $250,000. Using the Samuelson condition, determine whether the park should be built, and explain why a private developer charging an admission fee would likely underprovide it relative to the efficient outcome.

5. Explain, using the deadweight loss logic from Chapter 13, why antitrust enforcement against a merger that would raise the HHI substantially is grounded in the same economic argument as Pigouvian taxation of a negative externality: both are corrections to a gap between private and social marginal outcomes.

A factory's private marginal cost curve lies below the social marginal cost curve because of uncompensated pollution damage. What is the effect on market output relative to the social optimum, and what corrects it?
Correct. Because MSC = MPC + MEC lies above MPC for a negative externality, the market equilibrium (where demand meets only MPC) occurs at a higher quantity than the social optimum (where demand meets MSC). A Pigouvian tax set equal to the marginal external cost internalizes the externality by making the firm's effective marginal cost equal to MSC, restoring the socially efficient quantity without requiring a full ban.
